<p>FOREWORD
</p>
<p>This study was sponsored by the Future Projects Office, Marshall
</p>
<p>Space Flight Center, NASA, Huntsville, Alabama as a portion of its
</p>
<p>large booster feasibility and system cost study program. It is an exten-
</p>
<p>sion of Contract NAS8-2599 with Space Technology Laboratories, Inc.,
</p>
<p>Aerojet-General Corporation is the major subcontractor under this study
</p>
<p>program.
</p>
<p>Previous studies have indicated that application of sea borne
</p>
<p>techniques to very large launch vehicles may offer a more effective
</p>
<p>solution to the launch of very large unit payloads when compared to
</p>
<p>other booster concepts. This study will describe a sea launch booster
</p>
<p>system, define a vehicle and its performance, and apply developed costing
</p>
<p>techniques to show what economies may be expected when vehicle size is
</p>
<p>increased rapidly, vehicle simplicity is emphasized, and vehicle recovery
</p>
<p>and reuse is exploited. The use of the sea launch technique is primarily
</p>
<p>to permit latitude in the choice of vehicle size, simplicity of design,
</p>
<p>handling and launch, and simplicity in recovery techniques.
</p>
<p>Page ii</p>
<p></p>

<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>I. INTRODUCTION
</p>
<p>This volume discusses the vehicle system that has been defined
</p>
<p>and analyzed as a result of the Sea Dragon study.
</p>
<p>The technical approach was taken for several reasons:
</p>
<p>1. The time available was limited--four months for the
</p>
<p>complete task.
</p>
<p>2. Basic data defining the vehicle portion of the overall
</p>
<p>' system was needed early in the course of the study as
</p>
<p>input to supplementary investigations in such phases
</p>
<p>as manufacturing and operational support.
</p>
<p>3. To analyze potential problems in depth, it was neces-
</p>
<p>sary to establish a detailed preliminary vehicle design.
</p>
<p>These factors pointed to the necessity for freezing basic design
</p>
<p>parameters early in the study. The design decisions made at this point
</p>
<p>were on the basis of previous studies, first level optimization studies.
</p>
<p>Page 1-1</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>I, Introduction (cont.)
</p>
<p>and to some extent on arbitrary engineering judgments. Using this
</p>
<p>information, a preliminary expendable vehicle configuration (No. 132)
</p>
<p>was developed by three weeks after tne start of the study. This basic
</p>
<p>design was refined and detailed in a later version (No.134) released
</p>
<p>midway through the study. The final recoverable design (No. 135), which
</p>
<p>is discussed in the vehicle description section, was produced one month
</p>
<p>before the completion of the study. All of these configurations are
</p>
<p>basically similar; various supporting studies and analyses were on the
</p>
<p>basis of the design available at the time the analysis had to be initi-
</p>
<p>ated to meet study deadlines. Wherever possible, the analyses have been
</p>
<p>modified to show how tney relate to Configuration No. 135.
</p>
<p>Because of the design approach followed, the degree of optimization
</p>
<p>possible was obviously limited. Results of the design study and analyses
</p>
<p>show that in some phases of the study, improvement in technical approach
</p>
<p>is possible. Where these problems can be defined, the avenues to possible
</p>
<p>improvement are discussed.
</p>
<p>Page 1-2 (End I)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>II. VEHICLE DESCRIPTION
</p>
<p>A. GENERAL FEATURES AND FUNCTION
</p>
<p>The Sea Dragon is a very large sea-launched two stage liquid
</p>
<p>propellant rocket vehicle that will be used to inject into earth orbit
</p>
<p>large payloads. As presently designed, the vehicle has a gross liftoff
</p>
<p>weight of 40 million Ib and is capable of propelling a payload of approxi-
</p>
<p>mately 1,100,000 Ib to a circular orbit of 300 run altitude. The payload
</p>
<p>was assumed to be liquid hydrogen for this study, but it could be other
</p>
<p>material or equipment, or additional propulsion stages. A command module
</p>
<p>of the Apollo type provides the guidance, control, and communication
</p>
<p>functions; it will be capable of separation, re-entry and recovery as
</p>
<p>well as abort functions.
</p>
<p>The vehicle as shown in Figure II-A-1, II-A-2, and II-A-3 is
</p>
<p>nominally 75 ft in diameter and 500 ft long. Tables II-A-1, II-A-2,
</p>
<p>and II-A-3 list other principle characteristics of the vehicle. Detailed
</p>
<p>propulsion system (stage) drawings are included in Parts 3A and B.
</p>
<p>Page II-1</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, A, General Features and Function (cont.)
</p>
<p>The major components of the vehicle, such as the first
</p>
<p>stage, second stage, and payload will be separately towed by sea to the
</p>
<p>assembly site where they will be assembled in the horizontal position
</p>
<p>while floating in a quiet lagoon. Small components that might be better
</p>
<p>transported by land or air will be installed and checked out at the
</p>
<p>assembly site. The vehicle will be capable of being serviced and operated
</p>
<p>in moderately heavy seas. To achieve proper flotation and stability
</p>
<p>characteristics during towing and while'in the vertical prelaunch atti-
</p>
<p>tude, the vehicle will be equipped with a ballast unit. During the
</p>
<p>launch sequence, the ballast unit is released and later taken in tow for
</p>
<p>reuse. x
</p>
<p>The first stage of the vehicle is recoverable and reuseable.
</p>
<p>After staging, the re-entry flight is passively controlled by an inflated
</p>
<p>drag device that limits its velocity at water impact so that the struc-
</p>
<p>ture is not damaged and can be recovered and reused with minimal refur-
</p>
<p>bishments. Figures II A-4 and II A-5 depict the vehicle flight operations
</p>
<p>functions.
</p>
<p>Page II-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, A, General Features and Function (cont.)
</p>
<p>The main vehicle structure consists of pressure stabilized
</p>
<p>tanks. This structure is pressurized at levels that prevent the develop-
</p>
<p>ment of compressive instability during all modes of sea handling, pro-
</p>
<p>pellant loading, erection, flight, and recovery. The propellant tanks
</p>
<p>are provided with lines for controlled pressure loading and venting in
</p>
<p>both horizontal and vertical positions. Cryogenic tanks and lines are .
</p>
<p>insulated. One large regeneratively-cooled combustion chamber and con-
</p>
<p>ventional De Laval nozzle comprise the first-stage engine. It uses
</p>
<p>RP-1 fuel pressurized by CH4 from a separate tank. The oxidizer is
</p>
<p>liquid oxygen pressurized by an autogenous system utilizing a heat
</p>
<p>exchanger on The engine. The mixture ratio is controlled by the auto-
</p>
<p>genous pressurizing gas flow. The first-stage engine thrust vector is
</p>
<p>controlled by gimbaling the thrust chamber with actuators using RP-1
</p>
<p>fuel at tank pressure. Roll control is provided during both stages of
</p>
<p>flight by four auxiliary engines on the second stage. These engines
</p>
<p>also provide pitch and yaw control for the second stage as well as
</p>
<p>assisting in orbital injection.
</p>
<p>Page II-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, A, General Features and Function (cont.)
</p>
<p>The main second-stage engine has a fixed alignment and uti-
</p>
<p>lizes an expandable nozzle. Propellants are liquid oxygen and liquid
</p>
<p>hydrogen. The oxygen is pressurized by its own vapor pressure plus
</p>
<p>acceleration head. The hydrogen tank pressure is obtained by flow from
</p>
<p>the engine heat exchangers. Thus, one of the main features of the
</p>
<p>entire propulsion system is its simple pressurization system, which
</p>
<p>avoids use of turbopumps. The expandable nozzle on the second stage
</p>
<p>provides a method of conforming to a conventional configuration envelope
</p>
<p>while still producing a large expansion ratio when opened (compared to
</p>
<p>equivalent fixed nozzles).
</p>
<p>An all-inertial guidance system is incorporated in the
</p>
<p>vehicle's command module; it returns with the module for recovery.
</p>
<p>Self-checking and sequencing functions are included in the guidance
</p>
<p>system.
</p>
<p>Page II-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>II, Vehicle Description (cont.)
</p>
<p>B. OPERATIONAL SEQUENCE
</p>
<p>The operational sequence of the vehicle is considered to
</p>
<p>start after the vehicle has been assembled, checked out, and is ready
</p>
<p>for fueling. After propellant servicing, the vehicle is towed to the
</p>
<p>launch site and erected. Operational readiness is verified and the
</p>
<p>launch sequence is started. The main events in the launch sequence are
</p>
<p>illustrated in Figure II-A-4 and the flight sequence in Figure II-A-5.
</p>
<p>A list of the major vehicle operations is presented in
</p>
<p>Table II-B-1. More detailed plans and descriptions of the equipment
</p>
<p>for the entire Sea Dragon operation are presented in the Operational
</p>
<p>Plan section of this report. Detailed operational sequences or dis-
</p>
<p>cussions of the operation of the major subsystems are included in
</p>
<p>corresponding portions of this report.
</p>
<p>The recovery of the Apollo-type command module will be
</p>
<p>similar to that in current earth orbital programs and is also illus-
</p>
<p>trated in Figure II-A-5.
</p>
<p>Page II-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, B, Operational Sequence (cont.)
</p>
<p>The ballast unit will be recovered by inflating flotation
</p>
<p>bags on the unit after launch of the vehicle. Prior to launch, the
</p>
<p>unit will be attached to a buoy by means of a line, and an air hose to
</p>
<p>the flotation bag on the ballast unit is supported by the same line.
</p>
<p>After launch, the air hose will be connected to a service vessel for
</p>
<p>the ballast recovery operations. Retrieval of both the ballast and
</p>
<p>the first stage, after return from flight, is discussed in the section
</p>
<p>on the Operational Plan.
</p>
<p>C. SYSTEM PERFORMANCE
</p>
<p>As presently conceived, the recoverable version of Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>will place into earth orbit a useful payload of 1,100,000 Ib in addition
</p>
<p>to the spent second stage. An orbital altitude of 300 nm with a maximum
</p>
<p>eccentricity of 0.005 and an inclination of 28° will be achieved by
</p>
<p>launching along the Atlantic Missile Range from a launch point 40 mi
</p>
<p>from Cape Canaveral. Orbit injection will occur 22.4 min after launch
</p>
<p>at a point 4,1OO nm downrange.
</p>
<p>Page II-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>II, C, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>After 81 sec of propulsion, the first-stage booster will
</p>
<p>separate ax an altitude of 125, 000 ft and a velocity of 5,800 ft/sec,
</p>
<p>having reached a peak acceleration of 4.2 g and after passing a peak
</p>
<p>q of 1,600 lb/ft2 (Figure II-C-1). The first-stage booster will coast
</p>
<p>to an altitude of 335,000 ft after staging. During this period, infla-
</p>
<p>tion of the conical flare will be initiated. Approximately 200 sec
</p>
<p>later, the flare will be fully inflated and atmospheric re-entry will
</p>
<p>begin, decelerating the first stage at a maximum of 6.5 g to an impact
</p>
<p>velocity of 3OO ft/sec at a location 175 mi downrange of the launch
</p>
<p>site. Water penetration of 75% of one body length occurs after impact
</p>
<p>and causes loading in the tankage, which is adequately balanced by the
</p>
<p>100 psi minimum internal pressure. Because an inflatable flare and
</p>
<p>special structural reinforcement is not needed for an expendable con-
</p>
<p>figuration, a higher stage propellant mass fraction is achievable, and
</p>
<p>additional payload can be delivered into orbit. The payload capability
</p>
<p>of Sea Dragon with an expendable first stage will be 1,121,000 Ib or
</p>
<p>an increase of 21,000 Ib for equivalent launch and orbit conditions.
</p>
<p>Page II-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, C, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>The second-stage booster propels the vehicle with its main
</p>
<p>engine for 260 sec, assisted by the low thrust auxiliary TVC engines,
</p>
<p>which continue burning to orbit injection 1,344 sec after launch.
</p>
<p>The nominal performance characteristics of the system are
</p>
<p>shown in Table II-A-1. The trajectory characteristics are shown in
</p>
<p>Figure II-C-2. Also in Section V. are presented discussions of the per-
</p>
<p>formance analyses that have been conducted to evaluate the vehicle
</p>
<p>propulsion, loads, and dynamics in its main modes of operation. It is
</p>
<p>shown therein that the main propulsion system has been optimized for a
</p>
<p>staging ratio of 1.92:1, a first-stage area ratio of 5.10:1 and a thrust-
</p>
<p>to-weight ratio of 2:1. Limited analyses have shown that the under-
</p>
<p>water launch causes a payload penalty of about 3% when compared to
</p>
<p>surface launch; however, an optimum launch mode has not been determined.
</p>
<p>The combined effects of sea state, wind and thrust perturbations have
</p>
<p>been found to cause launch dispersion effects within the corrective
</p>
<p>Page II-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>II, C, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>capability of the first-stage control system. Subsequent flight
</p>
<p>maneuvers and wind influences, together with longitudinal acceleration,
</p>
<p>give rise to a structural loading that exceeds those developed in the
</p>
<p>most severe water environment prior to flight. Whereas the first-stage
</p>
<p>control system capability is found to be adequate, the auxiliary engines
</p>
<p>for the second stage, as presently sized, appear marginal. Improvement
</p>
<p>to an adequate level of TVC capability represents no basic problem.
</p>
<p>The nature of the assumed Sea Dragon mission is such as to
</p>
<p>require only normal performance of a present day inertial guidance
</p>
<p>system. An error analysis has shown that prelaunch accuracy require-
</p>
<p>ments on alignment, position, and velocity are 1/3 min of arc, .33 nm
</p>
<p>and 3.16 knots respectively. Wtih present day systems, these can be
</p>
<p>achieved easily except for the initial alignment accuracy even though
</p>
<p>the vehicle is in constant motion. Thus it can be concluded that no
</p>
<p>serious performance penalties need be paid to acquire the many advan-
</p>
<p>tages of sea-launched and sea-recovered vehicles of Sea Dragon type.
</p>
<p>Page II-9 (End II)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>III. VEHICLE SUBSYSTEMS
</p>
<p>A. STAGE I PROPULSION SYSTEM
</p>
<p>1. Description - Configuration No. 135
</p>
<p>Stage I utilizes a liquid bipropellant, pressure-fed
</p>
<p>rocket propulsion system. The general arrangement of the stage is
</p>
<p>shown in Figure III-A-1 (Stage I Dwg.). Basic Stage I characteristics
</p>
<p>are given in the following table:
</p>
<p>Stage Diameter
</p>
<p>Stage Length
</p>
<p>Stage Weight, Total
</p>
<p>Usable Propellants
</p>
<p>Propulsion System Mass Fraction
</p>
<p>Propellants
</p>
<p>Mixture Ratio
</p>
<p>Thrust Chamber Type
</p>
<p>Throat Diameter
</p>
<p>75 ft
</p>
<p>262 ft
</p>
<p>28 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>25 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>0.892
</p>
<p>LO2/RP-1
</p>
<p>2.3
</p>
<p>DeLaval, Regeneratively
Cooled
</p>
<p>41.6 ft
</p>
<p>Page III A-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Expansion Area Ratio
</p>
<p>Sea Level Thrust, Nominal
</p>
<p>Chamber Pressure Nominal
</p>
<p>Thrust Vector Control
</p>
<p>Propellant Tank Ullage Pressures
Nominal
</p>
<p>80 x 106 lb
</p>
<p>300 psia
</p>
<p>Gimbal-roll control
by Stage II Aux Engines
</p>
<p>LO2 226 psia
</p>
<p>RP-1 425 psia
</p>
<p>Propellants are fed from integral tankage by a com-
</p>
<p>bination of ullage pressurization and dynamic heads. The tankage is
</p>
<p>shaped conventionally with exception of the forward closure on the
</p>
<p>forward LO2 tank. This closure is a 6O° cone as is required to enable
</p>
<p>water entry and recovery of the stage. The forward closure for an ex-
</p>
<p>pendable stage would utilize a 1.4 to 1 elliptical form. LO2 is fed to
</p>
<p>the thrust chamber through a multiple line that is routed outside of
</p>
<p>the aft RP-1 tank. During expulsion of the LO2, the difference between
</p>
<p>required injector inlet and dynamic heads is provided by ullage pres-
</p>
<p>sure, which is developed by heating a portion of the LO2 flow and
</p>
<p>ducting it to the ullage space. This autogenous system is controlled
</p>
<p>by throttling the flow from the high pressure, LO2 injector inlet mani-
</p>
<p>fold downstream of the main LO2 valve to the heat exchanger that is
</p>
<p>located on the thrust chamber assembly.
</p>
<p>Fage III A-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The RP-1 fuel tank is located aft of and integral
</p>
<p>with the LO2 tank; an insulated bulkhead separates the tank compart-
</p>
<p>ments. The RP-1 tank pressure is developed by methane, which is
</p>
<p>stored in a separate tank at equilibrium with its vapor pressure at
</p>
<p>500 psia. The vapor pressure is used as the driving force to inject
</p>
<p>methane vapor into the bottom of the RP-1 tank where it is heated as
</p>
<p>it bubbles through to the ullage space. This secondary VaPak system
</p>
<p>maintains RP-1 feed line inlet pressure at equilibrium with the methane
</p>
<p>tank pressure. Ullage pressure is maintained at a level equal to" the
</p>
<p>difference between methane tank pressure and the dynamic head. No
</p>
<p>regulating controls are therefore required.
</p>
<p>Ignition is hypergolic. Triethylaluminum (TEA) is
</p>
<p>injected into a gaseous oxygen lead. The TEA is blown down, gas
</p>
<p>pressurized.
</p>
<p>Main propellant valves are poppet types that are
</p>
<p>sealed in the closed position with a clamped diaphragm, which is
</p>
<p>sheared by the poppet movement. Valve actuation forces are provided
</p>
<p>h-age III A-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>by the propellant feed line pressures controlled with mechanical
</p>
<p>detents. The gimbal actuators are low pressure hydraulic cylinders
</p>
<p>that use fuel at tank pressure as the energy source.
</p>
<p>Propellant tanks are designed by the propellant feed
</p>
<p>requirements. The aft skirt consists of stiffened skin and frames that
</p>
<p>react with the gimbal actuator loads.
</p>
<p>2. Propellants
</p>
<p>a. Properties of the Propellants Selected
</p>
<p>Propellants used for the first-stage main
</p>
<p>engine are LOX/RP-1 at a mixture ratio of 2.3:1. Considerations of
</p>
<p>the characteristics of these propellants appear in the table below:
</p>
<p>Advantages Disadvantages
</p>
<p>1. Low cost (now $0.0185/lb) 1. Cryogenic storage
</p>
<p>2. Reasonable specific impulse 2. Icing and thermal
(Theoretical sea level Is = gradients
260 sec at AR = 5.0 and PC =
30O psia) 3. Contamination-sen-
</p>
<p>sitive
</p>
<p>Page III A-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>3. High state of the art
</p>
<p>4. Availability excellent 4. Nonhypergolic
</p>
<p>5. High bulk density results in
attractive mass fraction and
favorable sea handling charac-
teristics
</p>
<p>Large size and reusability of the stage place greater
</p>
<p>importance on propellant costs. Current availability of the propellant
</p>
<p>is also important if propellant plant construction is not to become the
</p>
<p>pacint item.
</p>
<p>Large size diminishes boil-off and insulation penal-
</p>
<p>ties associated with cryogenics. Water handling, on the other hand,
</p>
<p>adds to the icing problems associated with LO2 and LH2. Although the
</p>
<p>propellant selection is not final for the conditions of this particular
</p>
<p>study, the propellants offer the greatest potential for investigation
</p>
<p>of recoverability. For a more optimistic recovery operation than as-
</p>
<p>sumed here, propellant cost can become the limiting item on overall
</p>
<p>operating cost. The cost of storables per pound of payload is about
</p>
<p>$5. If one-hundred uses of each vehicle were possible, the propellant
</p>
<p>Page III A-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>cost would greatly exceed the hardware cost. Use of RP-1 and LC&gt;2 also
</p>
<p>permits full-scale first-stage engine feasibility firings without
</p>
<p>commitment of large amounts of funds.
</p>
<p>Theoretical and estimated actual performance data is
</p>
<p>shown in Figure III-A-2. The specific impulse used for vehicle per-
</p>
<p>formance calculations is 93% of theoretical. This is conservative
</p>
<p>(by 1%) by comparison with current practice and is in keeping with
</p>
<p>the general philosophy of the study. The acceptance of this conserva-
</p>
<p>tive performance goal should result in reduced development program cost
</p>
<p>and increased combustion stability potential.
</p>
<p>a. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The LO /LH combination is attractive because
</p>
<p>of its high specific impulse (theoretical sea level Is = 342 sec at
</p>
<p>mixture ratio 5.O:1, Pc = 300 psia and area ratio = 5.0:1). Its low
</p>
<p>density makes the vehicle buoyancy characteristics undesirable and
</p>
<p>the tanks heavier. However, previous investigations have indicated
</p>
<p>Page III A-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stags I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>that use of an LO2/LH2 first stage can result in attractive vehicle
</p>
<p>geometries. These have not yet been thoroughly explored for Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon.
</p>
<p>Both storable propellants such as ̂
</p>
<p>zine-50 and the high energy cryogenics LO2/LH2 seem worthy of further
</p>
<p>analysis.
</p>
<p>3. Thrust Chamber Assembly
</p>
<p>a. Chamber and Injector
</p>
<p>(1) A single thrust chamber assembly is used
</p>
<p>for" the first stage. This assembly is of conventional DeLaval design,
</p>
<p>regeneratively-cooled with RP-1. The assembly is gimbaled. The im-
</p>
<p>portant design and performance parameters of the first -stage TCA is
</p>
<p>given below.
</p>
<p>fage III A-7</p>

<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(2) Nozzle Type
</p>
<p>At the outset of this study, four
</p>
<p>different exhaust nozzle types had been investigated. These studies did
</p>
<p>not take into account all of the considerations associated with the sea
</p>
<p>launch concept. TCA design types studied were:
</p>
<p>Type I: Conventional DeLaval
</p>
<p>Type II: Plug
</p>
<p>Type III: Annular chamber and nozzle
</p>
<p>Type IV: Expansion deflection.
</p>
<p>Figure III-A-la illustrates these types. The conventional DeLaval
</p>
<p>thrust chamber was selected for the vehicle so that the study could con-
</p>
<p>centrate on the important features of "sea" and "size," without becoming
</p>
<p>excessively involved in other new or novel concepts. Notwithstanding
</p>
<p>this consideration, the DeLaval type has some outstanding advantages
</p>
<p>that in part account for the fact that all important rocket engines in
</p>
<p>use today are of this type. Included in these advantages are:
</p>
<p>Page III A-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(a) High state of art: As indicated
above.
</p>
<p>(b) Low heat flux: Less than plug or
</p>
<p>expansion-deflection types by factors of approximately 2:1.
</p>
<p>(c) Excellent aerodynamic flow charac-
</p>
<p>teristics: The nozzle coefficient is high and very predictable from
</p>
<p>theory and extensive test data.
</p>
<p>(d) Structurally efficient: The chamber
</p>
<p>is basically a pressure vessel designed with tension instead of crushing
</p>
<p>allowables. Thrust takeout structure is also usually direct.
</p>
<p>(e) Simple valving and manifolding:
</p>
<p>Liquid propellant DeLaval thrust chambers usually utilize one fuel and
</p>
<p>one oxidizer valve.
</p>
<p>(f) Segment of chamber can be tested:
</p>
<p>Aerojet-General has demonstrated, using the Titan LR87 first-stage
</p>
<p>engine, that a wedge can be tested and accurate data obtained on heat
</p>
<p>transfer and combustion efficiency.
</p>
<p>Fage III A-10</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AERO J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(3) Number of Chambers
</p>
<p>The thrust level required for injection of
</p>
<p>a payload in excess of a million pounds into a 300-nm circular earth
</p>
<p>orbit was found from computer trajectory studies, performed early in the
</p>
<p>study, to be 80 x 106. This value represents a preliminary optimi-
</p>
<p>zation and was frozen early to develop a vehicle design on which to
</p>
<p>base the other facets of the study. One thrust chamber of this size
</p>
<p>was chosen rather than a cluster of chambers. Considerations- leading
</p>
<p>to this decision included reliability as indicated by the reduction of
</p>
<p>numbers of components such as chambers, valves, manifolds, igniters,
</p>
<p>gimbals, and gimbal actuators. It was believed that the structural
</p>
<p>characteristics of the system would be simplified, and therefore im-
</p>
<p>proved, especially for water handling and recovery conditions. A
</p>
<p>characteristic of large liquid rocket vehicles is that the diameter of
</p>
<p>the thrust chamber increases as the square root of the thrust while
</p>
<p>the tank diameter tends to increase only as the cube root. Thus, as
</p>
<p>size increases, even a single chamber exceeds the tank diametral
</p>
<p>envelope. This has happened on the vehicle studied where the
</p>
<p>Fage III A-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>chamber exit diameter is 94 ft compared to the 75-ft tank diameter. If
</p>
<p>a cluster of chambers were used for this vehicle, their relatively in-
</p>
<p>efficient packaging would result in excessive projections into the
</p>
<p>free stream.
</p>
<p>(4) Chamber Contour
</p>
<p>The conical combustion chamber is conical
</p>
<p>upstream of the throat with a cone half angle of 30°, which is con-
</p>
<p>ventional. This chamber, with a reasonable injector-to-throat area
</p>
<p>ratio, results in a chamber that is more than adequate on the basis
</p>
<p>of residence time or characteristic length, in this case, 342 in.
</p>
<p>(Titan I, burning LOX/RP-1 developing a thrust of 149,400 Ib with
</p>
<p>Pc = 584, has an L* = 42 in.). The exact contour for the diverging
</p>
<p>section of the nozzle is shown in Figure III-A-3.
</p>
<p>(5) Injector Type
</p>
<p>The conical chambers are advantageous
</p>
<p>in that the converging ramp tends to damp out some longitudinal
</p>
<p>Page III A-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>combustion instabilities. Even more important is the injector pattern.
</p>
<p>As a result of the large L* and long residence time, the first-stage
</p>
<p>chamber will be able to use a stable-combustion injector such as the
</p>
<p>showerhead. This injector is very stable in engines of the current size.
</p>
<p>Its main disadvantage in short chambers is its medium rate of performance
</p>
<p>compared to other types. This selected contraction area ratio and
</p>
<p>resulting large chamber volume would provide good combustion efficiency
</p>
<p>and allow flexibility in the exact injector pattern design with virtually
</p>
<p>no performance loss. Showerhead injectors would allow the combustion
</p>
<p>to be spread out over larger volumes and thus tend to produce a combustion
</p>
<p>flow field that is less sensitive to disturbances or oscillations that
</p>
<p>might ordinarily initiate instability. A further discussion of combustion
</p>
<p>of Sea Dragon size chambers is given in Appendix II-2.
</p>
<p>(6) Start Transient
</p>
<p>Starting conditions resulting from large
</p>
<p>line and manifold volumes downstream of the valves can cause hard starts
</p>
<p>and initiate combustion instabilities. The Sea Dragon size and line
</p>
<p>lengths could cause considerable timing difficulties if a conventional
</p>
<p>Fage III A-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>approach is used. To control the start timing, the fuel orifices will
</p>
<p>be sealed with unidirectional burst diaphragms. The RP-1 will be bled
</p>
<p>into the jacket up to the injector diaphragms. This arrangement allows
</p>
<p>precise RP-1 injection timing, keeps out contaminants, such as oxygen,
</p>
<p>and also provides coolant in the cooling jacket at all times. Tne
</p>
<p>chamber pressure buildup assumed for performance and other analytical
</p>
<p>purposes is given in Figure III-A-4.
</p>
<p>(7) Engine Cooling
</p>
<p>The cooling of the injector head and
</p>
<p>chamber tube walls is accomplished by regenerative cooling with RP-1.
</p>
<p>A heat transfer study has been completed on the cooling requirements of
</p>
<p>the first -stage engine in which it was found that the pressure drop
</p>
<p>necessary to cool the engine is not excessive. The study indicated a
</p>
<p>maximum heat flux of 4.72 Btu/in2sec, maximum wall temperature of
</p>
<p>1271°F, bulk temperature increase of RP-1 of 443°F (Case 5, see
</p>
<p>Appendix II-9), total pressure drop from tank to chamber of 170 psi,
</p>
<p>tube pressure drop of 72.1 psi for Case 5, and a total coolant weight
</p>
<p>trapped in the tubes of 107,000 Ib.
</p>
<p>III A-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, Stage I Propulsion System (conx.,
</p>
<p>b. Flow Controls and Lines
</p>
<p>(1) Path of the LO2 Used in First-Stage Engine
</p>
<p>The LO2 feeds from the first-stage tankage
</p>
<p>through a system of 10 tubes of 2.75-ft diameter. The tubes pierce
</p>
<p>the LO2 tank wall around the periphery of the 1.4 to 1 elliptical
</p>
<p>common bulkhead. The common bulkhead is not ported. The LO2 flows
</p>
<p>down through the small tubes and into a horizontal collector manifold
</p>
<p>that lies in the plane of the gimbal axis. The LO2 flows horizontally
</p>
<p>18O° around the engine before flowing into the injector. After the LO2
</p>
<p>turns and flows down, it passes through the main LO2 valve. The main
</p>
<p>LO2 valve is a poppet valve with a burst (shear) diaphragm for rapid
</p>
<p>opening and prefire sealing purposes. Once the valve is opened, the
</p>
<p>LO2 is fed to the injector head where it is distributed to the injector
</p>
<p>orifices. The total propellant feed pressure is the sum of the ullage
</p>
<p>gas pressure in the LO2 tank and an acceleration head. The gas pres-
</p>
<p>sure is the result of autogenous pressurization. This is accomplished
</p>
<p>by bleeding part of the LO2 that normally goes to the injector and
</p>
<p>III A-15</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>sending it to a heat exchanger that is mounted on the exterior of the
</p>
<p>throat section of the engine. A more detailed discussion of this pres-
</p>
<p>surization gas flow is given in the pressurization section of this
</p>
<p>report.
</p>
<p>(2) Path of the RP-1 Used in First-Stage
Engine
</p>
<p>The RP-1 feeds from the tank and is
</p>
<p>collected in a manifold. The manifold runs down to the gimbal plane,
</p>
<p>then runs horizontally 180° around the head of the engine where it goes
</p>
<p>through the main RP-1 valve and into the inlet manifold of the engine.
</p>
<p>The main RP-1 valve also is a poppet valve that has a burst diaphragm
</p>
<p>to start the propellant flow. The inlet manifold is tapered in cross
</p>
<p>section around the periphery of the thrust chamber injector. The RP-1
</p>
<p>comes into the manifold, then into every other coolant tube. For
</p>
<p>details of the exact path of RP-1, see the injector schematic diagram,
</p>
<p>Figure III-A-6. The RP-1 flows down the tubes where it goes into a
</p>
<p>turning manifold and back up to the injector. This provides tow-pass
</p>
<p>Page III A-16</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>regenerative cooling of the engine. It then enters the injector face
</p>
<p>and is injected into the chamber.
</p>
<p>Two special cases of the flow of RP-1
</p>
<p>occur before ignition and before first-stage impact. When the vehicle
</p>
<p>is in the loaded condition, the RP-1 can be bled into the coolant tubes
</p>
<p>from an external source so that the engine start time is decreased and
</p>
<p>some cooling is provided for the tubes during the ignition period
</p>
<p>before steady operation. Before first-stage water impact recovery, the
</p>
<p>RP-1 trapped in the engine coolant tubes must be drained to eliminate
</p>
<p>excessive hydrostatic pressures caused by the 20g or greater decele-
</p>
<p>ration, which could produce pressures of 550 psi or greater. The tube
</p>
<p>drainage is accomplished by a bypass line that allows the RP-1 to
</p>
<p>drain into the hemispherical concave injector head during the nose
</p>
<p>down portion of the recovery trajectory.
</p>
<p>c. Ignition
</p>
<p>The first stage is ignited by introducing TEA
</p>
<p>(triethylaluminum) into the chamber to react with the oxygen. The TEA
</p>
<p>Page III A-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C ORPORATION —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>is stored in a tank that is pressurized by a high pressure bottle.
</p>
<p>The ignition sequence is as follows: (1) the main LCX, valve is
</p>
<p>opened; (2) a pressure sensor in the head of the injector senses the
</p>
<p>pressure of the LO2 and actuates the TEA pressurizing line valve
</p>
<p>causing the TEA to be injected into the combustion chamber of the
</p>
<p>first-stage engine. The TEA is mixed with the LO2 at an overall
</p>
<p>mixture ratio of 20:1. Local mixture ratios will be at or near
</p>
<p>stoichiometric; and (3) the RP-1 valve is opened and main ignition
</p>
<p>occurs. This ignition system was selected for the following reasons:
</p>
<p>(1) TEA is hypergolic with LO2, thus com-
</p>
<p>bustion is reliable and self contained.
</p>
<p>(2) Ignition with small ignition delays occurs
</p>
<p>over a wide range of mixture ratios.
</p>
<p>(3) Readily available at low cost: the
</p>
<p>details of the ignition sequence and further discussion of the ignition
</p>
<p>system and ignition sequence are given in Appendix II-7.
</p>
<p>Page III A-18</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION ——
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>d. Structural Loads and Design
</p>
<p>The TCA is subjected to many types of loading
</p>
<p>starting from towing to final recovery. The following discussion
</p>
<p>applies to the rubber gimbal pad with actuator as presented in the
</p>
<p>vehicle drawings. During towing, the TCA is subject to bending moments
</p>
<p>(approximately 62 x 10^ ft-lb) and body shear forces (approximately
</p>
<p>0.10 x 106 ib) as the result of hogging and sagging. These loads are
</p>
<p>transmitted into the tank walls by use of the gimbal actuator, shear
</p>
<p>in the rubber gimbal pad, and many large structural members connecting
</p>
<p>the ballast unit with the aft skirt of the RP-1 tank. All of the
</p>
<p>bending loads and shear loads from the ballast are taken by the large
</p>
<p>structural members. During the erection process, large moments from
</p>
<p>the ballast are transmitted into the RP-1 aft tank skirt since the
</p>
<p>ballast is flooded to allow rotation of the vehicle to the vertical
</p>
<p>position. These erection shears and moments from the ballast protect
</p>
<p>almost no loading on the TCA because the structural members are de-
</p>
<p>signed to react and compensate.for these loads. The underwater ignition
</p>
<p>of the engine subjects the engine skirts to hydrostatic loads caused by
</p>
<p>Page III A-19</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>separation of the flow because overexpansion increases the load. The
</p>
<p>skirt loads are increased because of the hydrodynanic loads resulting
</p>
<p>from the short underwater trajectory. The nozzle wall is provided with
</p>
<p>extra structural memebers to accommodate these loads. This extra
</p>
<p>structural material represents a weight penalty caused by underwater
</p>
<p>launch.
</p>
<p>During first-stage burning, the loads on the
</p>
<p>TCA are: thrust loads that result from the pressure distribution in
</p>
<p>the engine; aerodynamic loads on the skirt resulting from various
</p>
<p>angles of attack; and gimbal actuator loads. The engine structural de-
</p>
<p>sign enables accommodation of these loads with very little difficulty.
</p>
<p>Wherever possible, pressure forces have been reacted with pressure
</p>
<p>forces and concentrated loads have been transmitted as pressure loads.
</p>
<p>The various TCA loadings that are inherent to sea launch and recovery
</p>
<p>techniques are given in the following:
</p>
<p>(1) Shear and bending loads resulting from
</p>
<p>hogging and sagging during towing operations before launch and after
</p>
<p>recovery
</p>
<p>Fage III A-20</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(2) Shear and bending loads resulting from
</p>
<p>erection during prelaunch operations
</p>
<p>(3) Hydrostatic and hydrodynamic compressive
</p>
<p>loads on the DeLaval nozzle skirt caused by underwater trajectory
</p>
<p>(4) Amplification of gimbal actuator loads
</p>
<p>if thrust vectoring is required while the TCA is underwater
</p>
<p>(5) Gimbal has to transmit tension loads from
</p>
<p>the recovery drag flare to the tankage. This load represents approxi-
</p>
<p>mately 23 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>The impact deceleration "g loading"
</p>
<p>(approximately 20g ) requires that the coolant be bled from the cooling
</p>
<p>tubes to prevent tube rupture, because hydrodynamic pressures of 550
</p>
<p>psi or greater are produced.
</p>
<p>(7) The gimbal region has to be designed to
</p>
<p>transmit the impact loading of the TCA and the tankage during water
</p>
<p>entry (approximately 20g gives loading of 16 x 106 Ib or 400 psi over
</p>
<p>the area of the gimbal bearing).
</p>

<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>e. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>Load reaction and transmission associated with
</p>
<p>sea launch and recovery is practical. The incremental weight of the
</p>
<p>necessary structure is compatible with the Sea Dragon concept and does
</p>
<p>not represent a significant performance penalty.
</p>
<p>Certain problems concerned with the first-
</p>
<p>stage TCA need more investigation, as follows:
</p>
<p>(1) Better definition of loading because of
</p>
<p>underwater TCA vectoring requirements
</p>
<p>(2) Engine start transients chamber pressure
</p>
<p>buildup and emptying of water slug from the engine
</p>
<p>(3) More effective method of ballast arrange-
</p>
<p>ment regarding the TCA
</p>
<p>l-age III A-22</p>
<p></p>

<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(4) Optimization of first-stage engine cooling,
</p>
<p>Several methods of heat protection are given in Figure III-A-6, such as
</p>
<p>film cooling, ablative cooling, and alternate plumbing configurations
</p>
<p>for regenerative cooling
</p>
<p>(5) Better definition of recovery loading on
</p>
<p>TCA from drag flare and impact loading on gimbal
</p>
<p>4. Thrust Vector Control (TVC)
</p>
<p>a. Gimbal
</p>
<p>A gimbal thrust chamber was selected for TVC
</p>
<p>with a capability of +_ 3° gimbal angle deflection. The factors that
</p>
<p>lead to this selection are: potential reliability, relative simplicity,
</p>
<p>and efficiency. The gimbal concept fits into a Sea Dragon development
</p>
<p>program in that much component development and system functional testing
</p>
<p>can be done without operation of the propulsion system.
</p>
<p>Page III A-23</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-QENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The first-stage TCA is gimbaled by mounting
</p>
<p>the assembly on the spherical aft end of the methane tank, which is
</p>
<p>part of the aft closure of the RP-1 tank. After considering more
</p>
<p>conventional configurations, such as a spherical bearing, a slightly
</p>
<p>less conventional design was chosen, which is a rubber pad bonded to
</p>
<p>the tank and TCA and provides movement of the TCA by elastic shearing
</p>
<p>of-the rubber. The latex rubber is cured to contain a central core
</p>
<p>of fluid that can take pressure, but does not offer the shear restraint
</p>
<p>of the rubber itself. The pad edges are restrained with a fabric
</p>
<p>material capable of allowing rubber shear deflections but not radial
</p>
<p>expansion.
</p>
<p>b. Actuators and Controls
</p>
<p>The actuator used is a hydraulic actuator
</p>
<p>using RP-1 as an actuator fluid. The rubber pad allows efficient con-
</p>
<p>version of the thrust force into a pressure force distributed over the
</p>
<p>face of the pressurized spherical methane tank. The design of the
</p>
<p>actuator system required an analysis of the chamber moment of inertia,
</p>

<p></p>

</p>

</p>
<p>shear of the rubber pad, movement of the flexible joints in the
</p>
<p>propellant lines, external aerodynamic loads on skirt, lateral and
</p>
<p>axial accelerations, and jet damping. An analysis of the torque
</p>
<p>requirement of the actuation system is given below:

<p>This total moment is supplied by a hydraulic
</p>
<p>actuator having a moment arm of 32.5 ft and using RP-1 at 400 psi,
</p>
<p>which is fed to a 6-ft diameter piston. The amount of RP-1 dumped
</p>
<p>overboard based on an estimated duty cycle for the trajectory is
</p>

</p>
<p>two-way valve which has a feedback system allowing maximum RP-1 utili-
</p>
<p>zation efficiency. The schematic showing the actuator and valve system
</p>
<p>is given in Figure III-A-8.
</p>
<p>c. Structural Loads and Design
</p>
<p>The thrust load is reacted by the rubber gimbal
</p>
<p>pad at 1,8OO psi in compression and the drag load during recovery will
</p>
<p>reach as high as 400 psi in tension. Both of these loadings are the
</p>
<p>most extreme conditions that the gimbal pad will experience. The
</p>
<p>actuator torque developed is 63 x 10^ ft-lb, which gives an actuator
</p>
<p>force of approximately 2 x 10^ Ib. This load is transmitted into the
</p>
<p>TCA by shear on the periphery of the injector and into the RP-1 tank
</p>
<p>Page III A-26</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>by the aft tank closure skirt, which has additional structural members
</p>
<p>at the actuator attachment point. During flight, when the first-stage
</p>
<p>TCA is not gimbaled, there is symmetric aerodynamic loading on the
</p>
<p>nozzle skirt because the exit diameter of the skirt is 94 ft as com-
</p>
<p>pared to the vehicle diameter of 75 ft. The most severe aerodynamic
</p>
<p>loading incurred in flight occurs when the engine is gimbaled the full
</p>
<p>3° at low altitude. The moment produced on the skirt is less than
</p>
<p>12 x 10^ ft-lb, which results in a tube stress at the throat of 6^600
</p>
<p>psi. This is well below the yield strength of the tube metal at the
</p>
<p>elevated temperature.
</p>
<p>d. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The gimbal system studied can be developed;
</p>
<p>however, several potentially serious problems have been revealed by
</p>
<p>the study. The main problems are associated with sea handling loads
</p>
<p>and these make a rigid chamber installation using secondary injection
</p>
<p>or other alternative TVC system appear worthy of further study.
</p>
<p>Page III A-27</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>From the table of torques, it is clear that the
</p>
<p>largest torque required is used to overcome the shear in the rubber.
</p>
<p>This is one area that could be improved considerably by selection of
</p>
<p>a softer, low shear rubber.
</p>
<p>The total weight penalty of the gimbal system
</p>
<p>is 84,000 Ib, which includes actuator weights and the RP-1 dumped.
</p>
<p>The system could be improved if the dumped RP-1 could be used effec-
</p>
<p>tively for engine cooling or combustion. If the dump pressure of the
</p>
<p>RP-1 is greater than the pressure in the injector head, the fluid could
</p>
<p>be fed back into the injector and used for combustion. It could also
</p>
<p>be fed into the throat region for cooling but would be too undependable
</p>
<p>for design cooling. The present design calls for dumping the RP-1
</p>
<p>overboard during flight.
</p>
<p>An alternative approach to the first stage
</p>
<p>engine TVC is proposed in Appendix 11-10. "An Alternate First-Stage
</p>
<p>TVC System," where a secondary injection system is discussed and a
</p>
<p>schematic is given showing part of the combustion gases being bled
</p>
<p>Page III A-28</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>from the injector region and dumped back into the nozzle downstream of
</p>
<p>the throat. The gases are bled out at an off-optimum mixture ratio so
</p>
<p>that the combustion gases are relatively cool. The prime advantage of
</p>
<p>this system is that the combustion chamber can be mounted rigidly to
</p>
<p>the vehicle structure.
</p>
<p>5. Tankage
</p>
<p>a. General
</p>
<p>Propellants for the Stage I Sea Dragon engine
</p>
<p>are pressure fed from two main tanks. Accessory tankage is provided
</p>
<p>on the first stage for methane, which is used for the pressurization
</p>
<p>of the RP-1 fuel tank and to pressurize the inflatable recovery flare.
</p>
<p>The methane tank also provides gimbal bearing structure that reacts
</p>
<p>against the thrust of the first-stage nozzle.
</p>
<p>Vehicle tankage arrangement is shown in the
</p>
<p>stage layout drawings (Figure III-A-1 and Figure III-B-1). A weight
</p>
<p>breakdown is given in Table III-A-1.
</p>
<p>Page III A-29</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Table III-A-2 provides tankage parameters to
</p>
<p>afford a general understanding of the structure. Tabulated are the
</p>
<p>material thicknesses and weights for 2014-T6 aluminum alloy, an alloy
</p>
<p>showing some promise for the application. This alloy was used for study
</p>
<p>purposes. Material studies resulted in the final conclusion that a
</p>
<p>more optimistic selection would have been 18% maraging steel for all
</p>
<p>but the LH2 tank where a titanium alloy would be applicable. (See
</p>
<p>Appendix II-1 "Tankage Materials" and the remarks at the end of this
</p>
<p>section under Evaluation and Recommendations.)
</p>
<p>Figure III-A-9 compares mass fraction with
</p>
<p>tank material and indicates the relative structural performance of the
</p>
<p>material selected for the Sea Dragon.
</p>
<p>All tankage is designed on the properties of
</p>
<p>the material at working temperature. All tanks are proof tested at a
</p>
<p>pressure level calculated for the proof test temperature at yield in the
</p>
<p>material. The proof test pressure is calculated from maximum expected
</p>
<p>operating pressure at temperature, increased by a factor of 1.15. The
</p>
<p>nominal ultimate design load factor is 1.28.
</p>
<p>Page III A-30</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>All cryogenic tankage is insulated to control
</p>
<p>boiloff and to prevent the'general formation of ice. The insulation
</p>
<p>material and analytical considerations are presented in Appendix II-4.
</p>
<p>The main propellant tanks are cylindrical in
</p>
<p>form at a diameter of 75 ft with end closures selected to best counter-
</p>
<p>act the structural loading imposed. All main tankage is integral; an
</p>
<p>insulated bulkhead is common to the two main tanks of each stage.
</p>
<p>b. Stage I Tankage
</p>
<p>The first-stage tankage assembly consists of
</p>
<p>a forward LO2 tank, an aft RP-1 tank divided by a bulkhead, and a
</p>
<p>CH4 tank located in the aft center of the RP-1 tank aft closure. The
</p>
<p>first-stage tankage is completely enclosed in the second-stage ex-
</p>
<p>pandable nozzle when the vehicle is assembled; the entire first stage
</p>
<p>is submerged when the vehicle is in the sea launch configuration.
</p>
<p>Thrust reaction is through the RP-1 tank aft closure.
</p>
<p>Page III A-31</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(1) RP-1 Fuel Tank
</p>
<p>RP-1 fuel tank is located at the aft end of
</p>
<p>the first stage. This tank serves as the main load reacting element
</p>
<p>of the gimbaled first-stage engine, loads input at the. aft skirt, and
</p>
<p>body forces along its short cylindrical tank length.
</p>
<p>(a) Forward Closure, RP-1 Tank
</p>
<p>The forward closure is a 1.4:1
</p>
<p>ellipse, which is insulated to control LC&gt;2 boiloff and RP-1 ullage
</p>
<p>pressurizing gas (CH4) cooling. The four inches of insulation is
</p>
<p>encapsulated in the space between the membrane and a 1/8 in. thick
</p>
<p>aluminum skin on the RP-1 side of the bulkhead. The bulkhead (RP-1
</p>
<p>tank forward closure) is unported.
</p>
<p>»
</p>
<p>The bulkhead is designed to with-
</p>
<p>stand an internal pressure of 162 psi. Relative tank pressures between
</p>
<p>the L,C&gt;2 an&lt;^ RP-1 are so controlled that crippling pressure loading on
</p>
<p>Page III A-32</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>the bulkhead is not probable. With the exception of the insulation
</p>
<p>and the encapsulating shell, the bulkhead is an unreinforced membrane
</p>
<p>of 1.58 in. uniform thickness.
</p>
<p>(b) Cylindrical Section, RP-1 Tank
</p>
<p>The length of the cylindrical
</p>
<p>section of the RP-1 tank is 6 ft. This structure is unreinforced and
</p>
<p>4.17 in. thick. The cylindrical section is ported for RP-1 vent and
</p>
<p>fill lines and provides for attachment of the aft cylindrical skirt and
</p>
<p>the cylindrical section of the LO2 tank.
</p>
<p>(c) Aft Closure, RP-1 Tank
</p>
<p>The RP-1 tank aft closure is a
</p>
<p>9O° cone. This closure shape is chosen as it best counteracts,
</p>
<p>rigidly, the tank pressure with a concentrated load at the center.
</p>
<p>The closure supports the methane tank through which the engine loads
</p>
<p>are input.
</p>
<p>Page III A-33</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (Cont.)
</p>
<p>The conical aft closure is rein-
</p>
<p>forced at the intersection of the aft spherical element of the CH^ tank
</p>
<p>(gimbal base) as is required to distribute the concentrated load over
</p>
<p>the area necessary to provide RP-1 tank pressure reaction of the thrust
</p>
<p>(first-stage engine). The conical portion of the closure is uninsulated.
</p>
<p>The closure is ported, for main
</p>
<p>propellant feed, near the intersection between the closure and the
</p>
<p>CH4 tank. The port is made of a number of small holes as is required
</p>
<p>to minimize discontinuities in the structure.
</p>
<p>(d) Antislosh Baffles
</p>
<p>Antislosh baffles are mounted on
</p>
<p>the cylindrical and conical portions of the RP-1 tank. The slosh
</p>
<p>baffles are a highly discontinuous structure providing no hoop or
</p>
<p>circular stability to the tank. The baffles are designed to eliminate
</p>
<p>sloshing and to counteract impact loads developed by relative fluid
</p>
<p>motion.
</p>
<p>Page III A-34</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>—— AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(2) Methane (CH4) Tank
</p>
<p>The methane tank is an elongated
</p>
<p>spherical structure. The forward and aft closures are hemispherical
</p>
<p>with a conical connecting element; the forward closure is common to
</p>
<p>the RP-1 tank, insulated, unported, and designed to withstand full
</p>
<p>CH4 tank pressure.
</p>
<p>The conical connecting element provides
</p>
<p>structural reinforcement for the RP-1 aft closure at the joint between
</p>
<p>the two tanks, provides CH4 tank pressure holding structure, and pro-
</p>
<p>vides distribution of the gimbal loads to the RP-1 aft closure. The
</p>
<p>conical element is ported for CH4 fill and vent and CH4 feed to the
</p>
<p>RP-1 tank.
</p>
<p>The CH4 tank aft closure is spherical,
</p>
<p>insulated, and unported. This element serves as the base for a
</p>
<p>bonded rubber gimbal bearing. The insulation is designed to keep the
</p>
<p>rubber at proper working temperatures as well as minimizing the change
</p>
<p>in condition of the CH4 charge.
</p>
<p>Page III A-35</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(3) LO2 Tank
</p>
<p>(a) Forward Closure, LO2 Tank
</p>
<p>The forward closure of the first-
</p>
<p>stage LC&gt;2 tank is a 60° included angle cone. This shape has been
</p>
<p>determined to be best able to counteract the water entry loads during
</p>
<p>recovery of the first stage. The closure is insulated internally with
</p>
<p>a 1.25-in. insulation thickness and is ported for vent and fill line
</p>
<p>access of all lines from all first-stage tankage to the stage umbili-
</p>
<p>cal connection. The closure is designed by the water impact loading;
</p>
<p>analysis indicates that by maintaining a high final LO2 tank pressure
</p>
<p>(130 psia minimum) a forward closure of 2.6-in. constant thickness is
</p>
<p>required. An expendable vehicle would be able to utilize an ellipti-
</p>
<p>cal forward tank closure with some weight saving.
</p>
<p>(b) Cylindrical Section, LO£ Tank
</p>
<p>The LC&gt;2 cylindrical tank wall is
</p>
<p>the primary body load reacting member and is continuous with the RP-1
</p>
<p>Page III A-36</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>tank cylindrical section. The LC&gt;2 cylindrical section is 2.26 in.
</p>
<p>thick, has 1.25 in. of insulation, and is ported for fill and vent
</p>
<p>line access and for main propellant feed. The main feed line ports
</p>
<p>are multiple and of small size located just forward of the aft closure
</p>
<p>(bulkhead). The porting is designed to minimize structural discon-
</p>
<p>tinuity and optimize propellant feed from outage and swirl aspects.
</p>
<p>The cylindrical portion of the LC&gt;2
</p>
<p>tank provides structural support for and counteracts loads input from
</p>
<p>antislosh baffle plates. The slosh baffle plates are highly discon-
</p>
<p>tinuous structures, providing no hoop or circular stability to the
</p>
<p>tankage.
</p>
<p>c. Structural Loads
</p>
<p>(1) Summary
</p>
<p>The sea handling concept is developed
</p>
<p>from a consideration of the weight of structure required to distribute
</p>
<p>Page III A-37</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>concentrated loads over large pressure holding vehicles. To reduce
</p>
<p>the weight of the vehicle and its support equipment, hence its cost,
</p>
<p>it has been practice to avoid the generation of concentrated loads by
</p>
<p>reacting pressure with pressure.
</p>
<p>The self-generated loads developed during
</p>
<p>handling and operation are pressures, shears, and moments. It has been
</p>
<p>found that these loads can be reacted by maintaining pressure in the
</p>
<p>compartments of the vehicle that have pressure holding as a primary
</p>
<p>function.
</p>
<p>(2) Pressures
</p>
<p>The structure must counteract externally
</p>
<p>applied pressures resulting from the sea and atmospheric environment
</p>
<p>and those that are self-generated and result from the pressure fed
</p>
<p>propellant feed system. In the system that has been defined for the
</p>
<p>study purpose, it has been found that internal pressure provides the
</p>
<p>designing criterion and very little stiffening is necessary anywhere
</p>
<p>Page III A-38</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>on the vehicle; it has been possible to counteract pressures with
</p>
<p>pressure in most areas by use of unreinforced membrane structure. All
</p>
<p>handling loading is reacted with pressure, as is all flight loading.
</p>
<p>Stiffening is required in four locations:
</p>
<p>The RP-1 tank aft closure is stiffened to react the thrust chamber loads;
</p>
<p>the LO2 forward closure is of a thickness greater than that .required to
</p>
<p>react the internal tank pressure as a result of concentrated high pres-
</p>
<p>sure forces that exist during water entry; the aft skirt on the RP-1
</p>
<p>tank is structure that distributes concentrated forces from the ballast
</p>
<p>stabilizing struts; and the gimbal TCA (engine) actuators.
</p>
<p>(3) Bending Moments
</p>
<p>Body bending moments are reacted by the
</p>
<p>cylindrical elements of the tankage structure. Pressure charges are
</p>
<p>maintained in all tanks so that no compressive or crippling forces
</p>
<p>are present. During flight and during erection of the floating loaded
</p>
<p>vehicle, large body bending moments, similar in magnitude, are devel-
</p>
<p>oped. These moments are counteracted by the tankage designed by pressures
</p>
<p>Page III A-39</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>required by the propellant feed system. Table III-A-2 shows the pres-
</p>
<p>sure level in tanks required to counteract maximum compressive loads
</p>
<p>developed as a result of bending and other environments.
</p>
<p>(.4) Mechanical Loading
</p>
<p>Concentrated or mechanical loads are
</p>
<p>distributed to and counteracted by the cylindrical structure. This
</p>
<p>approach enables development of rigid points for counteracting con-
</p>
<p>centrated loads and reduces interactions between those hard points
</p>
<p>and the flexible pressure holding structure.
</p>
<p>(a) Ballast Support
</p>
<p>All forces applied by the ballast
</p>
<p>to the vehicle are delivered by struts attached to the ballast and to
</p>
<p>the vehicle at the aft thrust chamber skirt. An axial force reacting
</p>
<p>capability exists through an attachment between the ballast and the
</p>
<p>aft end of the first-stage thrust chamber assembly. The structure
</p>
<p>Page III A-40</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AE R O J E T - G E N E R A I C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>staging joint provided at that location is for retention of the ballast
</p>
<p>during powered operation of the first stage and provides for separation
</p>
<p>of the ballast upon command.
</p>
<p>(b) Force Reaction, Thrust Chamber
Assembly
</p>
<p>The thrust of the first-stage
</p>
<p>engine is counteracted by the gimbal bearing. This bearing can react
</p>
<p>to both positive and negative thrusts of the same magnitude for
</p>
<p>appropriate RP-1 tank pressures. The gimbal bearing reacts side and
</p>
<p>torsional loads through shear in the flexible element.
</p>
<p>(c) Force Reaction, Gimbal Actuator
</p>
<p>The gimbal actuator reactions are
</p>
<p>near the aft skirt extension of the RP-1 tank. Radial components of
</p>
<p>the reactions are through the RP-1 tank conical aft closure, axial
</p>
<p>reactions are directly distributed in shear to the aft strut.
</p>

<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (conu.,
</p>
<p>(d) Stage I Thrust Structure
</p>
<p>The first-stage thrust is trans-
</p>
<p>mitted structurally from the TCA through the gimbal bearing and RP-1
</p>
<p>tank aft closure to the cylindrical tank elements. The stage thrust
</p>
<p>is delivered to the second stage by a cylindrical forward section
</p>
<p>of a staging joint located on the inside contour of the second-stage
</p>
<p>nozzle at the major body diameter.
</p>
<p>(e) Atmospheric Entry
</p>
<p>During atmospheric entry, a large
</p>
<p>drag area will be provided by an inflatable flare, which is attached
</p>
<p>to the first-stage TCA. All loads generated by the flare will be
</p>
<p>counteracted by the TCA.
</p>
<p>(f) Water Entry
</p>
<p>With the exception of a small
</p>
<p>forward portion of the 60° conical forward closure of the LC&gt;2 tank,
</p>
<p>Page III A-42</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>the first-stage water entry loads are counteracted by the retained
</p>
<p>tank gas pressure. Concentrated loads resulting from the 22g deceler-
</p>
<p>ation develop on components such as the TCA and accessory lines and

<p>valves, which must be counteracted as concentrated loads.
</p>
<p>d. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The tankage design described is believed to be
</p>
<p>adequate for the study purposes and feasible in an environment of an
</p>
<p>advancing state of the art. An evaluation of the design described is
</p>
<p>presented below from the aspects of the alternates considered in the
</p>
<p>development of the design.
</p>
<p>(1) Tankage Geometry
</p>
<p>Integral and separate tankage configu-
</p>
<p>rations were studied preliminarily. An analysis was made comparing
</p>
<p>the, weight of integral (common bulkhead) versus separate bulkheads
</p>
<p>between propellant tanks, considering propellant feed lines, distribu-
</p>
<p>tion manifolds, insulating requirements, and intertank structure. The
</p>
<p>Page III A-43</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>results indicated a significant weight penalty for the separate tankage.
</p>
<p>The weight penalty was approximately equal to the weight of one of the
</p>
<p>closures on the separate tanks. Other factors, such as maintaining
</p>
<p>the integrity of the insulation, leaks between tanks, vehicle rigidity,
</p>
<p>manufacturing sequence, and costs have been evaluated only qualitatively
</p>
<p>Consideration of these factors develops arguments for either approach.
</p>
<p>As an example, separate tankage that appears to afford simpler manu-
</p>
<p>facturing and proof test procedures necessitates, in application to
</p>
<p>the vehicle, an additional compartment that would not normally be pres-
</p>
<p>surized. During sea handling operations this compartment must be
</p>
<p>either (1) flooded to equalize pressure differences, (2) must be
</p>
<p>sealed and the intertank structure made capable of taking significant
</p>
<p>crushing pressure, or (3) must be pressurized to offset the high
</p>
<p>crushing pressure level.
</p>
<p>If the compartment is flooded, the
</p>
<p>requirements on the amount of and integrity of the insulation are pro-
</p>
<p>bably more severe than that for the integral tankage configuration.
</p>
<p>This results from the fact that the flooded compartment will be quies-
</p>
<p>cent and regardless of the amount of insulation applied, the water will
</p>
<p>Page III A-44</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJE T - G E N E R A L C ORPORATION —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>freeze in time. Buildup of ice in compartments is an obvious hazard.
</p>
<p>Furthermore venting of the water from the flooded compartment during
</p>
<p>the first portion of flight produces unusual structural requirements.
</p>
<p>If the compartment is sealed, a
</p>
<p>structure required to counteract high crushing pressure loads developed
</p>
<p>during sea handling modes becomes unreasonable from weight aspects.
</p>
<p>If the compartment is pressurized,
</p>
<p>the management of pressure in the compartment becomes of extreme im-
</p>
<p>portance to the success of the mission. Loss of pressure in the com-
</p>
<p>partment while submerged would result in structural failure; failure
</p>
<p>to vent pressure in an intertank compartment could result in collap-
</p>
<p>sing pressures on a closure of one of the propellant tanks, which
</p>
<p>would produce structural failure.
</p>
<p>The conclusions drawn at this time
</p>
<p>are: (1) That separate tankage does not avoid the inadvertent oc-
</p>
<p>currence of a collapsing pressure on a bulkhead and does not avoid or
</p>
<p>Page III A-45</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>minimize the requirements for the integrity of a propellant tank in-
</p>
<p>sulation, (2) the weight penalty for separate tankage as compared to
</p>
<p>integral tankage is approximately equal to the weight of one of the
</p>
<p>tank bulkheads. For these reasons, the Sea Dragon design utilized in-
</p>
<p>tegral tankage.
</p>
<p>Single versus cluster tanks was
</p>
<p>also considered. Reduced wall thicknesses, simplified pressure vessel
</p>
<p>design, and the greater number of units produced tend to reduce de-
</p>
<p>velopment time and fabrication cost and improve reliability and trans-
</p>
<p>portability for clustered tanks. These factors are felt to be offset
</p>
<p>by the very complex clustering structure and the probable vehicle
</p>
<p>weight penalty as compared to single integral tanks. For these reasons,
</p>
<p>the Sea Dragon vehicle design was based on the use of single tanks.
</p>
<p>The locations selected for the
</p>
<p>propellant constituents include consideration of vehicle performance
</p>
<p>and tend to optimize propellant feed systems. The forward location
</p>
<p>of the LO2 tankage provides the most forward vehicle center of gravity
</p>
<p>Page III A-46</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>position. In addition, the long stand pipe length for the LCX, feed
</p>
<p>line provides a large portion of the required LO2 injector inlet
</p>
<p>pressure. This factor is important because the required final LO2
</p>
<p>ullage pressure is low affording a significant pressurant gas weight
</p>
<p>saving. Higher required ullage pressures for the aft located fuel
</p>
<p>tankage does not compensate because the pressurant gases available for
</p>
<p>fuel are less dense. The arrangement chosen provides maximum vehicle
</p>
<p>stability resulting from the forward center of gravity location and
</p>
<p>the inert wei-ght advantage mentioned above.
</p>
<p>The methane tank was located inte-
</p>
<p>gral with the Stage I RP-1 tank because the higher working pressure
</p>
<p>of that compartment would counteract the pressures generated in the
</p>
<p>gimbal bearing. The low temperature of the methane, -128°F, must be
</p>
<p>isolated from the rubber bearing. Insulation provided for that pur-
</p>
<p>pose is internal to the methane tank. The methane tank is designed to
</p>
<p>withstand full tank pressure at room temperature with no reinforcement
</p>
<p>by RP-1 tank pressure.

<p>(3) Tankage Material
</p>
<p>(a) Sea and size effects are strong
</p>
<p>factors affecting selection of tank materials. Galvanic corrosion will
</p>
<p>be an important consideration in operation of the system. The tank
</p>
<p>size will advance the state of the art of flight tankage, especially
</p>
<p>in joining techniques. For purposes of the study, a material was
</p>
<p>chosen that has better known properties, is cheaper, and more avail-
</p>
<p>able than other types that might be used. This was 2014-T6 aluminum.
</p>
<p>Its use results in weight estimates that are conservative. However, a
</p>
<p>difficulty arose in certain phases of the study as a result of the
</p>
<p>specification of this material. This was concerned with the welding of
</p>
<p>the resultant thick walls.
</p>
<p>Appendix II-1, Tankage Materials,
</p>
<p>summarizes some of the data accumulated in the study. The final re-
</p>
<p>sult indicated that 18% maraging steel and some titanium alloys were
</p>
<p>potentially more attractive than the aluminum. Sufficient unknown
</p>

<p>areas exist in the selection of large rocket tank materials to warrant

<p>6. Pressurization and Feed Systems
</p>
<p>a. General Concepts and Advantages
</p>
<p>The propellant feed systems for both stages of
</p>
<p>the Sea Dragon will consist of pressure-fed systems rather than pump-
</p>
<p>fed systems. Three separate concepts are being used in the proposed
</p>
<p>pressure feed systems. These concepts include: (1) Autogenous pres-
</p>
<p>surization, i.e., the utilization of a vaporized and superheater pro-
</p>
<p>pellant for self-pressurization, (2) vapor pressurization, i.e., the
</p>
<p>utilization of a liquid propellant's vapor pressure for self-pressuri-
</p>
<p>zation, and (3) acceleration head effects that become as large as 270
</p>
<p>psi because of the large heights of the propellant tanks.
</p>
<p>Page III A-49</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>A list of the more pertinent advantages and
</p>
<p>disadvantages of a pressure-fed propulsion system for the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>first stage includes:
</p>
<p>Advantages •
</p>
<p>(1) Simplicity
</p>
<p>(2) Rugged tanks for sea handling and re-
covery.
</p>
<p>(3) Because of size, the tanks must be
</p>
<p>designed for moderately-high pressures even with turbopumps.
</p>
<p>(4) Eliminates the high cost of pump de-
</p>
<p>velopment, fabrication, and refurbishment.
</p>
<p>Disadvantages
</p>
<p>(1) Higher tank weights, thicker walls and
</p>
<p>lower mass fractions.
</p>
<p>(2) Tanks may be more expensive.
</p>
<p>Page III A-50</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Oxidizer System
</p>
<p>Figure III-A-10 illustrates the Stage I pres-
</p>
<p>surization system. The first-stage oxidizer tank uses an autogenous
</p>
<p>pressurization system. A small amount of LC&gt;2 is taken from the bottom
</p>
<p>of the LO2 tank, sent through a heat exchanger where it is vaporized
</p>
<p>and superheated, and fed back to the top of the LO2 tank to supply the
</p>
<p>necessary ullage pressure. The driving force for the pressurization
</p>
<p>process is provided by the acceleration head in the LC&gt;2 tank and pro-
</p>
<p>pellant line. The acceleration head effects account for 161 psi at
</p>
<p>first-stage ignition (2.O g) and 117 psi at first-stage burnout (4.6 g).
</p>
<p>The various ullage pressures in the LO2 tank plus the acceleration head
</p>
<p>effects results in the required injector manifold pressures.
</p>

</p>
<p>stage oxidizer pressurization system is listed as follows:

<p>d. Ullages

</p>

</p>

</p>
