</p>
<p>f. Overall Operational Characteristics
</p>
<p>Liquid oxygen flow is ducted from the bottom
</p>
<p>of the LO2 tank near the intersection of the common bulkhead of the
</p>
<p>cylindrical outer tank wall. The cylindrical wall is ported with a
</p>
<p>number of small diameter holes (1O tubes of 2.75 ft dia) to which
</p>
<p>lines are attached to pass the flow outside of the RP-1 tank. The
</p>
<p>small lines are manifolded to a single line that is provided with
</p>
<p>flexible joints enabling gimballing of the first-stage manifold. The
</p>
<p>main LO2 propellant valve is mounted structurally on the forward side
</p>
<p>of the first-stage injector. The main propellant valve in its shut-
</p>
<p>off condition is hermetically-sealed by a diaphragm between the body of
</p>
<p>the valve and its movable element. The valve in its shut off position
</p>
<p>is retained mechanically. The mechanical retention can be released by
</p>
<p>application of pressure or by an explosive device. Propellant flow is
</p>
<p>initiated when the mechanical device is released that permits con-
</p>
<p>trolled travel of the poppet to an intermediate position where it
</p>
<p>engages another mechanical stop. The sealing diaphragm is designed to
</p>
<p>fail in shear when the poppet moves and not because of the direct
</p>
<p>Page III A-55</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cOnt.)
</p>
<p>action of LO2 pressure. The valve is actuated by pressure in the pro-
</p>
<p>pellant line. The main propellant valve is shut off by release of the
</p>
<p>intermediate mechanical stop by mechanical or explosive device and
</p>
<p>moves under control to engage a conical seat at the aft extent of
</p>
<p>travel. The valve is moved to the shutoff position by the pressure
</p>
<p>drop across the valve during propellant flow. The valve is retained
</p>
<p>in the closed position by a mechanical device which tightens as the
</p>
<p>acceleration increases. This device eliminates the possibility of
</p>
<p>propellant flow or tank pressure loss during recovery.
</p>
<p>When the main LG&gt;2 propellant valve is open,
</p>
<p>a line is provided to a component which regulates liquid oxygen flow
</p>
<p>to the heat exchanger located in the throat region of the first-stage
</p>
<p>thrust chamber assembly. This controller responds to sensed pressures
</p>
<p>at the RP-1 injector manifold. Hence, oxidizer flow is controlled to
</p>
<p>match the fuel flow and maintain a constant mixture ratio.
</p>
<p>The RP-1 main propellant feed is ducted from
</p>
<p>the bottom of the RP-1 tank at Station 425 through a number of small
</p>
<p>Page III A-56</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cOnt.)
</p>
<p>ports. Flow from the ports' is manifolded to a main propellant feed
</p>
<p>line that is provided with flexible joints to enable gimballing of the
</p>
<p>first-stage thrust chamber assembly.
</p>
<p>The characteristics of the fuel thrust chamber
</p>
<p>valve are similar to the oxidizer valve and permit the valve to take
</p>
<p>one of three positions. There is no necessity for a high pressure
</p>
<p>actuation system in the valve, and therefore, a weight saving results.
</p>
<p>The fuel valve is normally closed. An annular ring serves as a shear
</p>
<p>diaphragm. This ring is attached between the valve pintle and the
</p>
<p>valve housing, thus restricting any flow from entering the injector.
</p>
<p>Upon receiving the proper signal, an explosive charge blows a pin
</p>
<p>that causes the annular ring to shear and thus gradually opens the
</p>
<p>propellant valve. A second pin is sheared at first-stage burnout.
</p>
<p>This operation causes the valve pintle to drop and seat against the
</p>
<p>lower portion of the valve housing. The valve is completely closed in
</p>
<p>this final position. A mechanical device will lock the valve in the
</p>
<p>closed position to eliminate any leakage during the recovery process.
</p>
<p>Page III A-57</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>g. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>One problem exists in the above described first-
</p>
<p>stage pressurization system. The problem concerns the maintenance of
</p>
<p>sufficient ullage pressures during first-stage ignition. Preliminary
</p>
<p>analysis indicates a decay in LO2 Ullage tank pressure from 266 psia to
</p>
<p>21 psia in ten sec, assuming-full propellant flow and no gaseous pres-
</p>
<p>surization addition (refer to Figure III-A-12). Similarly, the RP-1
</p>
<p>ullage tank pressure will decay from 425 psia to 45 psia in ten sec
</p>
<p>assuming full propellant flow if the methane gas pressure does not
</p>
<p>supplement the initial ullage pressure (refer to Figure III-A-13).
</p>
<p>Naturally, the vehicle would never get out of the water if the tank
</p>
<p>pressures were as low as the above analysis would seem to indicate.
</p>
<p>However, the analysis is somewhat misleading in that it assumes con-
</p>
<p>tinuous full flow, a condition that cannot exist under reduced tank
</p>
<p>pressures.
</p>
<p>v^
</p>
<p>The immediate solution to the problem is to
</p>
<p>be certain that both first-stage propellant tanks are given supplemen-
</p>
<p>tary gas pressures as soon as possible once the expulsion of the
</p>
<p>Page III A-58</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>propellants has been initiated. For this reason, it is recommended
</p>
<p>that additional analysis be conducted. In the case of the fuel circuit
</p>
<p>the time required for the methane to bubble up through the RP-1 tank
</p>
<p>must be determined so that the valve that admits the flow from the
</p>
<p>methane tank (gimbal ball) to the RP-1 tank is properly sequenced. In
</p>
<p>the case of the oxidizer circuit, it is suggested that the propellant
</p>
<p>utilization valve illustrated in Figure III-A-10 be located immediately
</p>
<p>upstream of the heat exchanger. Therefore, as soon as the valve is
</p>
<p>opened, additional LO2 gas pressure becomes available to supplement
</p>
<p>the initial ullage pressure. The pressure differential that pumps
</p>
<p>the pressurization gases from the heat exchanger to the LC&gt;2 tank is the
</p>
<p>result of the difference of acceleration head of the liquid and gaseous
</p>
<p>oxygen.
</p>
<p>7. Aft Skirt
</p>
<p>a. Description
</p>
<p>The aft skirt on Stage I consists of a
</p>
<p>cylindrical extension on the aft RP-1 tank. It extends from vehicle
</p>
<p>Page III A-59</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Station 414 to 436 and provides nesting support for the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle, which has its trailing edge at Station 421. The aft skirt is
</p>
<p>designed to provide hard points or fittings for actuator and ballast
</p>
<p>strut reactions.
</p>
<p>The skirt structure is stiffened skin, 0.5-in.
</p>
<p>total equivalent thickness, that attaches to a cylindrical extension
</p>
<p>on the RP-1 tank. The skirt is stiffened at its trailing edge with
</p>
<p>two deep frames designed to provide local circular stability. Gimbal
</p>
<p>actuator and ballast strut reactions are input to these circular frames
</p>
<p>through fittings.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation of Problems and Potential Improve-
ments
</p>
<p>The aft skirt distributes concentrated loads
</p>
<p>from the gimbal actuators and from the ballast struts to the tank
</p>
<p>structure. The skirt provides a surface against which the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle is stowed. These structural functions could be provided by a
</p>
<p>Page III A-60</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>truss-type structure for lower weight by additional design development.
</p>
<p>Concentrated loads from the truss structure could be distributed in a
</p>
<p>very short cylindrical structure at the aft end of the RP-1 tank. Ex-
</p>
<p>pandable nozzle stowage could be provided at its trailing edge with a
</p>
<p>ring channel section with its open end forward. Upon staging separation,
</p>
<p>the convolutions of the trailing edge of the expandable nozzle are re-
</p>
<p>leased by the relative motion of the stages.
</p>
<p>Alternate thrust chamber design types and -
</p>
<p>approaches to thrust vector control applicable to the Sea Dragon would
</p>
<p>not require an aft skirt extension. Where the thrust chamber is not
</p>
<p>articulated (such as a rigid chamber using secondary injection), the
</p>
<p>expansion section of the nozzle can be made to counteract ballast loads
</p>
<p>that are input in a circularly continuous manner. The incremental
</p>
<p>weight of the thrust chamber for this additional structural function
</p>
<p>probably would be less than that for the separate skirt extension. If
</p>
<p>a plug nozzle were to be used, separate skirts would not be required.
</p>
<p>In this case, the ballast loads would be input to a truncated isentropic
</p>
<p>spike in a circularly continuous manner.
</p>
<p>Page III A-61</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>•III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The aft skirt structure shown on the Stage I
</p>
<p>drawing is considered to be adequate to provide the structural functions
</p>
<p>defined above. A detailed analysis has not been attempted. Applicable
</p>
<p>thrust chamber design types of other designs could remove the require-
</p>
<p>ment for a separate skirt.
</p>
<p>8. Fill, Vent, and Dump System
</p>
<p>a. Description
</p>
<p>The LO2 tank, the RP-1 tank, and the CH4 tank
</p>
<p>have filling, venting, and dumping capabilities. Each has one fill
</p>
<p>line, one horizontal vent line, and one vertical vent line. These
</p>
<p>lines are all routed from the second stage forward compartment to the
</p>
<p>internal umbilical or disconnect assembly and to the respective tanks.
</p>
<p>The Stage I and Stage II layout drawings illustrate the location and
</p>
<p>routing of all Stage I fill and vent lines. Additionally,. Appendix
</p>
<p>II-8 provides a flow schematic of the first-stage fill and vent system,
</p>
<p>a functional description of all hardware used in filling a venting,
</p>
<p>Page III A-62</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>minimum pressures required during filling operations, and the positions
</p>
<p>that each component assumes during loading, towing erection, launch,
</p>
<p>staging, recovery, and dumping.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation
</p>
<p>The proposed vent system is flexible with many
</p>
<p>redundant features. Additionally, all filling and venting is conducted
</p>
<p>in a region that is accessible to the ground support equipment.
</p>
<p>9. Internal Umbilical Provisions
</p>
<p>a. General Configuration
</p>
<p>The internal umbilical interface is located
</p>
<p>in the center of the Stage II injector. Physically, it consists of
</p>
<p>a 6 ft dia recessed hold into which, the male portion of the connector
</p>
<p>(located on the Stage I nose cone) will slide and engage. Directly
</p>
<p>behind this recess is a tunnel that provides routing of the instrumen-
</p>
<p>tation and control lines from Stage I to the outer periphery of the
</p>
<p>Page III A-63</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III5 A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Stage II injector from which the various lines are rerouted to the
</p>
<p>vehicle GSE interfaces.
</p>
<p>b. Hydraulic Disconnect Couplings
</p>
<p>All the hydraulic couplings are identical in
</p>
<p>basic design and consist of two specially-designed check valves, back
</p>
<p>to back, which are held open by a compression rod that actuates the
</p>
<p>valves through a linkage system and provides an open circuit on all
</p>
<p>lines when Stage I and Stage II are coupled.
</p>
<p>Upon separation, the compression rods auto-
</p>
<p>matically are released allowing the check valves to resume a closed
</p>
<p>position isolating all systems from external contaminants.
</p>
<p>c. Electrical Disconnect Couplings
</p>
<p>All control and instrumentation lines will be
</p>
<p>connected by use of common-type bulkhead connectors and arranged by
</p>
<p>Page LiLA-64</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>function into various banks. Separation is simplified by use of this
</p>
<p>pin and socket combination.
</p>
<p>d. Disconnects (number and relative sizes)
</p>
<p>Hydraulic

<p>10. Propulsion System Performance
</p>
<p>a. Summary
</p>
<p>The thermochemical properties of the LOX/RP-1
</p>
<p>propellants for the first-stage vehicle were given in Figure III-A-2
</p>
<p>where the ISp versus mixture ratio curve is found to be very flat at
</p>
<p>the design mixture ratio. Because the engine is regeneratively-
</p>
<p>cooled with the propellant, the heat loss in the combustion chamber
</p>
<p>represents virtually no net loss. For the first stage vehicle,
</p>
<p>Figure III-A-14 gives a plot of chamber pressure versus specific im-
</p>
<p>pulse at sea level for various mixture ratios showing the small per-
</p>
<p>formance advantage when using chamber pressures much greater than 300.
</p>
<p>Page III A-66</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The conservative value of 93% of theoretical
</p>
<p>specific impulse assumed should be readily achievable. It is expected
</p>
<p>that the chamber can be cooled by convection techniques only and there-
</p>
<p>fore no loss because of film cooling need be considered. The large
</p>
<p>chamber volume will allow the reaction to attain a high degree of com-
</p>
<p>pletion while using stable injector designs.
</p>
<p>11. Propulsion System Weights, Stage I
</p>
<p>a. Summary
</p>
<p>The weights of the first-stage propulsion
</p>
<p>system are given in a weight breakdown accompanying this section. The
</p>
<p>results are given in Table III-A-3.
</p>
<p>The weights of all components have been cal-
</p>
<p>culated on the basis of the configurations defined. Component definition
</p>
<p>Page III A-67</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>has been on the basis of estimates, where necessary, as is discussed
</p>
<p>in other sections.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The total propulsion stage weight is felt to
</p>
<p>be conservative in terms of stage performance. It appears that through
</p>
<p>application of alternate component design concepts and materials
</p>
<p>significant stage performance improvement could be achieved. Utili-
</p>
<p>zation of higher strength structural materials for tankage and other
</p>
<p>components will provide an improvement in stage mass fraction. Applica-
</p>
<p>tion of alternate thrust chamber design types may offer weight ad-
</p>
<p>vantages .
</p>
<p>The weight breakdown, Table III-A-3, indicates
</p>
<p>a miscellaneous weight of 5% of the tankage weight. This allowance is
</p>
<p>made for weld joint reinforcement - This allowance accounts for
</p>
<p>thickening at the weld joints to allow for approximately 50% reduction
</p>
<p>in the strength of the as-welded joint. The reinforcement will be
</p>
<p>Page III A-68</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, A, Stage I Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>applied to longitudinal welds. The 5% will account for this reinforce-
</p>
<p>ment in the thinner sheets; a separate allowance is made for the thicker
</p>
<p>tankage elements. The RP-1 tank weight includes 70,000 Ib for integral
</p>
<p>stiffening of the conical aft closure to counteract the concentrated
</p>
<p>loads input by the gimbal bearing. Weld joints thickening will form
</p>
<p>the integral frame and stringer reinforcing element.
</p>
<p>Bulkhead insulation weights are on the basis
</p>
<p>of estimated thickness requirements of 4 in. Subsequent analysis has
</p>
<p>shown this to be very conservative.
</p>
<p>The weight savings achievable for an expandable
</p>
<p>vehicle are shown in Table II-A-2.
</p>

</p>
<p>Ill, Vehicle Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>B. STAGE II PROPULSION SYSTEM
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>The Stage II vehicle utilizes a bi-propellant pressure-
</p>
<p>fed rocket propulsion system. The general arrangement is shown in Figure
</p>
<p>III-B-1-. Basic Stage II characteristics are given in the following table:
</p>
<p>Stage Diameter 75 ft
</p>
<p>Stage Length to Separation Joint 188 ft
</p>
<p>Stage Weight, Total 10.6 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>Usable Propellants 9.5 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>Propulsion System Mass Fraction 0.89O5
</p>
<p>Propellants LO /LH
^ £
</p>
<p>Mixture Ratio 5
</p>
<p>Thrust Chamber Type DeLaval, Fixed
Expandable Nozzle
</p>
<p>Throat Diameter 31 ft
</p>
<p>Expansion Area Ratio 20
</p>
<p>Thrust, Nominal 14 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>Page III B-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Chamber Pressure, Nominal 75 psia
</p>
<p>Thrust Vector Control 4 aux engines
</p>
<p>Propellant Tank Pressures, Nominal
</p>
<p>LO2 30 psia
</p>
<p>LH2 100 psia
</p>
<p>Propellants are pressure fed from integral tankage.
</p>
<p>Injector inlet pressure is developed by a combination of ullage
</p>
<p>pressurization and acceleration head. The forward LO tank is self-
£
</p>
<p>pressurized and loaded at temperature equilibrium with vapor pressure
</p>
<p>at 30 psia. The required injector inlet pressure of 100 psia results
</p>
<p>from the additive acceleration head. The aft located LH tank is
£r
</p>
<p>separated from the LO tank by an insulted bulkhead. Pressurization
</p>
<p>of the LH_ tank is autogenous; liquid hydrogen is admitted to a heat
</p>
<p>exchanger on the cooled section of the thrust chamber where it is
</p>
<p>vaporized. Vapor is ducted to the propellant tank ullage through a
</p>
<p>low pressure drop line.
</p>
<p>Page III B-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Auxiliary thrust vector control engines use propellant
</p>
<p>from the main LH^ tank; LO is provided from separate spherical tanks
</p>
<p>that are pressurized from a regulated high pressure gas source.
</p>
<p>Main stage and auxiliary engines are ignited by
</p>
<p>injection of blowdown gas pressurized TEA.
</p>
<p>The main propellant valves are poppet types that are
</p>
<p>sealed in the closed position with clamped diaphragms. The valves are
</p>
<p>actuated by propellant-fed line pressure controlled with mechanical
</p>
<p>detents. The diaphragms are sheared by poppet motion.
</p>
<p>Propellant tanks are designed by the propellant feed
</p>
<p>requirements and are stabilized by pressure for all handling and flight
</p>
<p>loads. Skirts are made of stiffened skin.
</p>
<p>Page III B-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>Ill, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>2. Propellants
</p>
<p>a. Selection
</p>
<p>A comparison and evaluation of several propellant
</p>
<p>combinations is given in Section III-A-2. A decision to use LO /RP-1 in
</p>
<p>the first stage resulted. On a similar basis, LO /LH was selected as
2 2
</p>
<p>the propellant combination for Stage II. Previous studies at Aerojet-
</p>
<p>General on other sea-launched vehicles similar to Sea Dragon indicated
</p>
<p>that high performance coupled with reasonable cost and the ability to
</p>
<p>operate at low chamber pressures in upper-stage applications led to
</p>
<p>selection of LQ2/LH2. The performance of this propellant combination is
</p>
<p>given in Figures III-B-2, -3, -4, and -5.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>Further optimization of propellant selection on
</p>
<p>a cost effectiveness basis was not incorporated in this study but is
</p>
<p>recommended for future effort.
</p>
<p>Page III B-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>

<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>3. Thrust .Chamber Assembly
</p>
<p>a. General
</p>
<p>One rigidly mounted thrust chamber assembly is
</p>
<p>used in the second-stage propulsion system. The configuration is
</p>
<p>conventional (DeLaval nozzle, single combustion chamber, injector at
</p>
<p>forward end of combustion chamber, and one fuel and one oxidizer valve)
</p>
<p>The nozzle subsystem incorporates an expandable nozzle. The nozzle
</p>
<p>expansion section is a cooled, conventional rigid section with an
</p>
<p>expansion area ratio slightly greater than 6.0:1. Downstream of this
</p>
<p>point the nozzle is radiation-cooled, expandable, and is stowed in a
</p>
<p>contracted position on the Stage I tanks until second-stage firing.
</p>
<p>The following table gives the important engine
</p>
<p>parameters:
</p>
<p>Design and Performance Parameters
</p>
<p>Design thrust, vac = 14.12 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>Chamber pressure, P = 75 psia
</p>

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Design Basis
</p>
<p>A single thrust chamber was chosen because (1)
</p>
<p>the second-stage feed system is pressure-fed for system simplicity, which
</p>
<p>is expected to result in ease of development and flight reliability, (2)
</p>
<p>to realize a high stage mass fraction, especially for the low bulk density
</p>
<p>propellants, the system pressure levels must be kept low, and (3) to
</p>
<p>realize a high specific impulse, an expansion area ratio of 20 or higher
</p>
<p>is necessary. The scale effect (chamber and tank diameter vary as square
</p>
<p>and cube root respectively) was already mentioned in the discussion of
</p>
<p>the first-stage chamber. The net effect of the above factors is that a
</p>
<p>conventional clustered arrangement of chambers cannot be installed with-
</p>
<p>in the envelope above the first stage. The expandable nozzle in combi-
</p>
<p>nation with the single chamber provides the necessary expansion area
</p>
<p>ratio without the drag and weight penalty of a conventionally cooled
</p>
<p>expansion section.
</p>
<p>The single chamber is mounted rigidly to the
</p>
<p>second-stage tankage and thrust chamber compartment skirt. The first-
</p>
<p>stage tanks rest within the second-stage thrust chamber, thereby
</p>
<p>minimizing interstage length.
</p>
<p>Page III B-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, BJ Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>c. Chamber and Injector
</p>
<p>The combustion chamber is of conventional design,
</p>
<p>The 30 cone half angle and contraction area ratio of 1.8:1 allows more
</p>
<p>than enough combustion chamber volume. The conical chamber tends to
</p>
<p>inhibit longitudinal pressure fluctuations. The large chamber volume
</p>
<p>provides efficient combustion and flexibility in injector design. A
</p>
<p>possible injector configuration utilizes a shower head pattern which
</p>
<p>produces a combustion flow field over a relatively large portion of the
</p>
<p>chamber volume. Thus, the combustion reactions would not be as
</p>
<p>concentrated as for other injector types, and large perturbations that
</p>
<p>trigger combustion instability are less likely to occur. A discussion
</p>
<p>of combustion stability is included in Appendix II-2 for a Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>engine.
</p>
<p>Flow turning in the subsonic region upstream
</p>
<p>of the throa't is gradual; turning of a supersonic gas stream can be
</p>
<p>made at a sharper angle. The chamber contour upstream of the throat is
</p>
<p>O.5R (throat radius); the downstream radius of.curvature is 0.2Rt&lt;
</p>
<p>The gases are expanded around this downstream throat radius until a
</p>
<p>Page III B-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>Ill, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>half angle of 40 is reached at which point a contoured shape is attached.
</p>
<p>The expansion section contour is tangent to a half angle of 20° at an
</p>
<p>area ratio of 6.17:1. At this point the radiation cooled expandable
</p>
<p>metal nozzle section is attached. A 20° half angle was selected for the
</p>
<p>expandable nozzle. Appendix II-6 gives the results of the study performed
</p>
<p>to choose this expansion angle.
</p>
<p>The second-stage engine is cooled (nonregenera-
</p>
<p>tively) with hydrogen. Results of a computer heat transfer analysis
</p>
<p>indicate that hydrogen coolant flow rates of 260 Ib/sec or 4% of the
</p>
<p>total hydrogen flow can cool the chamber to the f= 6.17 station. The
</p>
<p>maximum flux is 1.91 Btu/in. sec; maximum wall temperature is 1,51O°F,
</p>
<p>coolant bulk temperature increase is 1,520°F and total pressure drop is
</p>
<p>93 psi. No loss in engine Is need be considered because the coolant is
</p>
<p>exhausted supersonically from the end of each tube with high I because
s
</p>
<p>of the low molecular weight of the gas. A more detailed discussion of
</p>
<p>engine cooling is given in Appendix II-3.
</p>
<p>Page III B-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>A large volume, circumferential, low pressure
</p>
<p>drop heat exchanger is applied to the exterior of the hydrogen coolant
</p>
<p>passages of the thrust chamber near the aft end of the rigid portion of
</p>
<p>the nozzle. The heat exchanger provides structural reinforcement for
</p>
<p>the thrust chamber and a heat source for pressurization of the second-
</p>
<p>stage LH tank during second-stage operation. A heat transfer analysis
^
</p>
<p>was made that proved the feasibility of the autogenous hydrogen
</p>
<p>pressurization system. The results of this investigation are given
</p>
<p>in Appendix II-5.
</p>
<p>The expandable nozzle is radiation cooled.
</p>
<p>Basically, the nozzle section is a thin wall, conical section in which
</p>
<p>convolutions or corrugations are formed. The depth of corrugations
</p>
<p>increases from the attachment point at Station 328 to the trailing edge,
</p>
<p>Station 420. A large nozzle currently undergoing altitude firing tests
</p>
<p>is shown in Figure III-B-10. The expandable element is nested on foam
</p>
<p>material and retained by several external straps. These straps provide
</p>
<p>mechanical retention of the expandable nozzle for prelaunch handling
</p>
<p>and first-stage flight modes. During the staging sequence, the straps
</p>
<p>Page III B-10</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>counteract pressure forces from an expanding gas charge that expels the
</p>
<p>expended first stage from the breach formed by the expandable nozzle.
</p>
<p>When adequate relative velocities of first and second stage have been
</p>
<p>-achieved, the straps are separated by shaped explosive devices, and the
</p>
<p>expandable nozzle is free to inflate at second-stage ignition. The straps
</p>
<p>and nesting materials provide mechanical protection of the expandable
</p>
<p>element, which varies in metal thickness from approximately 0.2 in. at
</p>
<p>its attachment point at Station 328 to approximately. 0.02 in. at its
</p>
<p>trailing edge.
</p>
<p>d. Flow Control and Lines
</p>
<p>(1) Flow of LO
</p>
<p>Once the second stage is clear of the first
</p>
<p>stage, the main LX&gt;2 valve can be opened. This valve is a poppett valve
</p>
<p>that has a shear diaphragm for a positive storage seal. An LO flow
£r
</p>
<p>control throttle is installed downstream of the poppett valve. The
</p>
<p>throttling feature allows slaving of the LO flow to the LH0 flow to
2 . 2
</p>
<p>maintain mixture ratio and propellant utilization control. One insulated
</p>
<p>Page III B-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>propellant line is installed on the outside of the fuel tank. The
</p>
<p>LO valve is located close to the injector head to minimize shutdown
</p>
<p>volume downstream of the valve. The pressure forcing the LO out of
</p>
<p>the tank is the sum of the ullage gas pressure in the tank and the
</p>
<p>acceleration head pressure of the LO . The gas pressure in the tank is
£r
</p>
<p>the result of pressurizing the LO with its own vapor pressure.
2
</p>
<p>(2) Flow of Ignition Fluid
</p>
<p>A pressure transducer in the head of
</p>
<p>the injector or timer senses the presence of the LO in the chamber and
</p>
<p>initiates an injection of hypergolic ignition fluid, triethylaluminum
</p>
<p>(see Appendix II-7). The TEA (triethylaluminum) is stored in a tank
</p>
<p>and is pressure fed by N gas stored in a high pressure tank nearby.
</p>
<p>The high pressure gas is simply allowed to blow down without use of a
</p>
<p>pressure regulator. This mechanical simplification is possible since
</p>
<p>the TEA can react with LO over a wide mixture ratio range with very
</p>
<p>small ignition delays.
</p>
<p>Page III B-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>

<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(3) Main LH Flowv ' 2
</p>
<p>After the LO /TEA. sequence, the LH valve
^ 2,
</p>
<p>is opened. The flow leaves the LH2 tank through a line running from the
</p>
<p>tank to the plane of the injector. The flow goes through a valve that is
</p>
<p>mounted in this line and then proceeds to the injector. The main LH
</p>
<p>valve is of the poppett type, having a shear diaphragm for sealing
</p>
<p>purposes as does the LO2 valve. The LH2 main feed line standpipe (in
</p>
<p>the tank) allows a small amount of LH_ to be trapped in the tank when
</p>
<p>the main engine burns out. This .propellant is used by the thrust
</p>
<p>vector control engines for orbital injection of the second stage and
</p>
<p>payload. The LH , after flowing through the valve, goes into a split
</p>
<p>manifold where approximately 4% of the total hydrogen flow is used for
</p>
<p>non-regenerative cooling of the engine. The engine walls are small
</p>
<p>tubes having openings at the top. These holes are sized so that they
</p>
<p>act as orifices and meter the flow into the cooling jacket. The main
</p>
<p>part of the hydrogen flow goes into the tapered manifold around the
</p>
<p>top of the engine. The flow is fed into the injector head by radial
</p>
<p>tubes that run from the manifold to the unbilical tunnel in the center
</p>
<p>Page III B-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>of the injector. These radial tubes are tapered toward the center of the
</p>
<p>injector. Every other tube is used for the LO and the remaining tubes
2
</p>
<p>are used for LH2. A schematic diagram of the flow in the injector head
</p>
<p>is given in Figure III-B-3.
</p>
<p>The pressure forcing the LH from the tank
^
</p>
<p>is the sum of the ullage gas pressure and the acceleration head of the
</p>
<p>fluid. The ullage pressure in the tank is the result of autogenous
</p>
<p>pressurization. Part of the flow that goes through the main LH valve
</p>
<p>goes into a. line that carries it to the heat exchanger located on the
</p>
<p>diverging section of the engine. The poppett valve stem actuates a
</p>
<p>valve that opens this line at the time the main LH2 valve is opened.
</p>
<p>The flow entering this line goes to the heat exchanger on the diverging
</p>
<p>part of the nozzle where the hydrogen is superheated. A line located
</p>
<p>on the outside of the LH2 tank carries this gas back to the forward
</p>
<p>end of the LH tank. As long as the hydrogen valve is open a
</p>
<p>continuous flow of pressurization gas is available. This system is
</p>
<p>stable. The ullage pressure in the tank will tend to match the
</p>
<p>propellant feed line pressure minus the pressure drop in the heat
</p>
<p>exchanger and vapor feed lines.
</p>

<p></p>

<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(4) Coolant H2 Flow
</p>
<p>The expandable nozzle is radiation cooled
</p>
<p>during flight. The chamber coolant hydrogen gas flows longitudinally
</p>
<p>down the cooling tubes. At the point of attachment of the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle ( C - 6.17), the hydrogen gas is injected at supersonic velocity
</p>
<p>into the hot-gas stream close to the wall. The dumping of the relatively
</p>
<p>cool hydrogen gas into the boundary layer at the pbint of attachment
</p>
<p>will provide a small amount of thermal protection for the upstream
</p>
<p>portion of the expandable nozzle.
</p>
<p>e. Ignition
</p>
<p>The ignition is accomplished using triethyl-
</p>
<p>aluminum (TEA.) that is hypergolic with LO . The ignition systems are
</p>
<p>discussed in detail in Appendix II-7.
</p>
<p>Page III B-15</p>
<p></p>

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>f. Structural Loads
</p>
<p>The second-stage thrust chamber assembly is
</p>
<p>rigidly mounted to the vehicle structure. The injector is integral with
</p>
<p>the LH_ tank. The injector is a double wall semisandwich structure as
</p>
<p>necessary to withstand tank pressure. Radial ribs and stressed skin
</p>
<p>construction will be used to integrate the tank and injector walls.
</p>
<p>The trailing edge of the cooled portion is rigidly supported by the
</p>
<p>transition skirt that extends from the aft end of the pressurized tank
</p>
<p>arid that counteracts loads from the expandable section of the nozzle.
</p>
<p>The heat exchanger located on the diverging section of the chamber gives
</p>
<p>added hoop strength to the nozzle. This structure will counteract the
</p>
<p>engine compartment pressure of 45 psia. This compartment is pressurized
</p>
<p>prior to ignition to counteract submersion loads and to provide staging
</p>
<p>by expelling the burned out Stage I booster from the second stage. A
</p>
<p>design chamber pressure of 1.25 x 75 psia (nominal) was used to allow
</p>
<p>for unpredictable pressure surges. The materials used for the various
</p>
<p>parts of the TCA are given in the following table:
</p>

<p>g. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The cooled elements of the Stage II thrust
</p>
<p>chamber assembly are considered to be of conventional design, which in
</p>
<p>application to Sea Dragon .does not introduce problems because of large
</p>
<p>Page III B-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>size. The chamber is conventionally shaped and advantages in combustion
</p>
<p>efficiency and stability may result from scaling. When requirements for
</p>
<p>contraction ratios (injector to throat area) and contraction angles are
</p>
<p>met, the resulting characteristic length is large. This factor is of
</p>
<p>importance because stable injector patterns, which produce performance
</p>
<p>loss in short chambers, can be used in Sea Dragon.
</p>
<p>An interaction between the expandable nozzle
</p>
<p>and flow from the auxiliary thrust vector control engines may be
</p>
<p>important. The interaction involves all propulsive operational modes.
</p>
<p>This interaction will require additional study to define severity and
</p>
<p>establish design requirements for affected components. The influence
</p>
<p>of flow from the auxiliary engines on the expandable nozzle can be
</p>
<p>modified by changing the mounting location or exhaust angle of the
</p>
<p>auxiliary engines.
</p>
<p>The effect of the sea on the second stage is
</p>
<p>in water handling and erection loadings and the ability of the tanks
</p>
<p>to sustain asymetric loading. Corrosion of the components is
</p>

<p>minimized by the sealed interstage compartment. Most of the exposed
</p>
<p>surface of the second stage will be covered with sealed layers of
</p>
<p>insulation for cryogenic storage.
</p>

</p>
<p>drawing.

</p>
<p>d. Stage II Structural Loading
</p>
<p>Pressure and body bending moments developed as
</p>
<p>a result of handling and flight environments are reacted by the
</p>
<p>pressurized propellant tank structure as for the first stage. The
</p>
<p>second-stage transition section and bulkhead section react flight
</p>
<p>body axial and bending loads as an unpressurized stiffened shell.
</p>
<p>During water handling operations, the compartments are pressurized to
</p>
<p>balance the external pressures encountered in the floating or submerged
</p>
<p>sea handling operation.
</p>
<p>The mechanical or concentrated loads applied
</p>
<p>to the second stage are the following:
</p>
<p>The first-stage thrust is reacted by the
</p>
<p>second stage at a structural (staging) separation joint located on
</p>
<p>the 75 ft cylindrical diameter at the aft end of the second-stage
</p>
<p>transition section at Station 328. The load path is along the
</p>
<p>cylinder; second-stage thrust chamber and expandable nozzle support
</p>
<p>loads are added at the same point.
</p>
<p>Page III B-27</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The second-stage thrust chamber is supported by
</p>
<p>the aft closure of the LH tank. Concentrated loads are not developed
£
</p>
<p>as the thrust chamber is primarily pressure supported. The cooled
</p>
<p>expansion portion of the nozzle (inside the 75 ft dia) and expandable
</p>
<p>section of the second-stage thrust chamber assembly are supported at
</p>
<p>attachments provided at the aft end of the second-stage structural
</p>
<p>transition section.
</p>
<p>Auxiliary engines (TVC) are mounted on the
</p>
<p>second-stage transition section. All longitudinal components of
</p>
<p>force reactions are distributed in the skin; all radial components
</p>
<p>are taken by circular frames.
</p>
<p>All structural load inputs to the payload
</p>
<p>are through the forward cylindrical skin. The payload is assumed to
</p>
<p>be designed to interface with a short cylindrical extension on the
</p>
<p>forward end of the second-stage LO2 tank.
</p>
<p>Page III B-28</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>e. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>A general evaluation and description of alternate
</p>
<p>approaches applicable to Sea Dragon tankage is given in Section III, A.
</p>
<p>Items of specific applicability to Stage II are shown in the stage
</p>
<p>layout drawing. Special loading effects are discussed as follows:
</p>
<p>(1) Hydrodynamic Effects
</p>
<p>The arrangement of Stage II tankage is
</p>
<p>favorable from vehicle center of gravity and pressurant gas weight
</p>
<p>aspects. The arrangement produces, in the light, pressure-stablized
</p>
<p>structure, some effects resulting from hydrodynamic loading that are
</p>
<p>not well understood. As an example, for a one-gravity transverse
</p>
<p>acceleration, the LO pressure on the cylindrical tank wall will be
</p>
<p>37 psi higher than on the opposite side. This pressure difference
</p>
<p>distributed around the surfaces of the pressure vessel may produce a
</p>
<p>general distortion of the structure. If the effect is dominant,
</p>
<p>bulkhead or closure failure may result. The analytical prediction of
</p>
<p>Page III B-29</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>this behavior is very complex and has not been attempted. Differential
</p>
<p>pressure loading on the pressure stabilized tank structure results from
</p>
<p>other environmental factors such as the variable depths of submergence
</p>
<p>at vehicle erection.
</p>
<p>During Stage II operation for a vehicle
</p>
<p>acceleration of 5g, the LO2 feed line pressure at the throttle valve is
</p>
<p>3.5 times the required pressure. This results from the hydrodynamic
</p>
<p>head in the long feed line and must be throttled to required levels
</p>
<p>for injection.
</p>
<p>Both hydrodynamic effects could be
</p>
<p>minimized by alteration of the tankage arrangement. Preliminary
</p>
<p>evaluation of a concentric tankage arrangement has been made, indicating
</p>
<p>that for a central LO tank the enclosing surface area is the same as
</p>
<p>the insulated common bulkhead. Differential pressure resulting from
</p>
<p>transverse accelerations would be reduced by a factor of 2.9; excess
</p>
<p>feed line pressure would be reduced because the stand pipe height
</p>
<p>decreases as a function of burn time. Final ullage pressures would
</p>
<p>Page III B-30</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>be higher with resultant higher outage gas weights; the transverse
</p>
<p>structural support of the central tank at its forward end would
</p>
<p>probably cause an inert weight penalty as compared to the present design.
</p>
<p>(2) Slosh Baffles
</p>
<p>No slosh baffling is shown for the Stage II
</p>
<p>tankage. It is expected that during Stage II operation the external
</p>
<p>purturbing influences will be small. Additional analysis will be
</p>
<p>required to confirm this conclusion.
</p>
<p>5. Pressurization and Feed Systems
</p>
<p>a. General Concept
</p>
<p>The general pressurization concepts used on
</p>
<p>Stage I are also used on Stage II. These concepts include autogenous
</p>
<p>pressurization, vapor pressurization, and acceleration head effects..
</p>
<p>Page III B-31</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. LOp Tank Pressurization System
</p>
<p>Pressurization of the Stage II LO tank is
</p>
<p>accomplished by a combination of the acceleration head effect and the
</p>
<p>oxygen vapor pressure (see Figure III-B-4). The liquid oxygen
</p>
<p>is stored at a temperature of -283°F with a corresponding vapor
</p>
<p>pressure of 30 psia. The final oxygen vapor pressure is 25 psia.
</p>
<p>The total pressure felt at the throttle valve
</p>
<p>varies from 107 psia at second-stage ignition (1.23g) to 305 psia at
</p>
<p>second-stage burnout (6.4g). The pressures are throttled down to the
</p>
<p>desired 100 psia injector inlet pressure. The ullage pressure of the
</p>
<p>gaseous oxygen at shutdown is 25 psi with a corresponding vapor density
</p>
<p>2
of O.44 Ib/ft . The residual gaseous pressurant weight is 52,000 Ib.
</p>
<p>c. LH Tank Pressurization System
</p>
<p>The second-stage fuel tank is autogenously
</p>
<p>pressurized in a manner similar to the first-stage oxidizer tank.
</p>
<p>A complele flow schematic illustrating the second-stage pressurization
</p>
<p>Page III B-32</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>system is included as Figure III-B-4. Liquid hydrogen is taken from
</p>
<p>the bottom of the tank, sent through a heat exchanger where it is
</p>
<p>vaporized and superheated, and fed back to the top of the LH tank to
£r
</p>
<p>supply its ullage pressure. The driving force needed for this
</p>
<p>pressurization process is provided by the acceleration head of the LH .
2
</p>
<p>An analysis of the system is included in Appendix II-5.
</p>
<p>The gaseous hydrogen pressure at shutdown is
</p>
<p>^considered to be 100 Ib/in. at a temperature of 300°R. A very detailed
</p>
<p>analysis would be required to obtain the exact pressure at the time of
</p>
<p>shutdown. No serious difficulty is anticipated in regard to the final
</p>
<p>tank pressure. The weight of the pressurizing gas at Stage II burnout
</p>
<p>is 18,500 Ib. The heat exchanger consists of large diameter tubes
</p>
<p>wrapped around the coolant tubes of the second-stage thrust chamber
</p>
<p>assembly. The nearly constant ullage pressure ensures a constant
</p>
<p>chamber pressure because the liquid acceleration head only contributes
</p>
<p>a maximum of 4 psi to the total pressure available.
</p>
<p>Page III B-33</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>—- AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Design criteria for the second-stage fuel
</p>
<p>system include:
</p>
<p>Initial gaseous hydrogen tank pressure 100 psi
</p>
<p>Final gaseous hydrogen tank pressure 100 psi (approx)
</p>
<p>Final gaseous hydrogen tank temperature -160°F
</p>
<p>Weight of gaseous hydrogen flowing 22,200 Ib
</p>
<p>through the heat exchanger (accounts for condensation)
</p>
<p>d. Ullage Pressure Characteristics
</p>
<p>Initial tank ullages are 3% and zero for the
</p>
<p>second-stage fuel and oxidizer tanks respectively. The tank volume,
</p>
<p>and hence, the total tank weight of the second-stage oxidizer tank is
</p>
<p>reduced because of the condition of zero ullage. An initial condition
</p>
<p>of zero ullage in the LO2 tank is possible because of the VaPak (vapor
</p>
<p>pressurization) system.
</p>
<p>No initial decay in LO ullage pressure is
</p>
<p>expected during liquid expulsion. As the liquid propellant is expelled,
</p>
<p>flashing occurs, creating an equilibrium condition between the liquid
</p>
<p>Page III B-34</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>and the gas. The gas then maintains the ullage pressure. Tests have
</p>
<p>been conducted on the vapor pressurization concept using many laboratory
</p>
<p>models with initial tank ullages varying from 0 to 30%. The tank pressure
</p>
<p>versus time curves are very similar in all cases thus establishing the
</p>
<p>fact that the initial pressure drop is independent of ullage. Figure
</p>
<p>III-A-11 illustrates the initial ullage conditions and the pressure
</p>
<p>drop profile for the second stage at ignition and burnout for that stage.
</p>
<p>e. Ignition
</p>
<p>Ignition will be accomplished through use of
</p>
<p>a triethyaluminum (TEA) reaction with LO . See Appendix II-7 for
</p>
<p>further details.
</p>
<p>f. Overall Operational Characteristics
</p>
<p>Hydrogen propellant is ducted from short stand
</p>
<p>pipes near the bottom of the hydrogen tank to the main propellant valve.
</p>
<p>The stand pipes limit the thrust chamber propellant utilization to
</p>
<p>Page III B-35</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>the liquid level adjacent to the stand pipes. Liquid below the stand
</p>
<p>pipes permits continuous flow to the TVC engines.
</p>
<p>With the main propellant valve in the open
</p>
<p>position, flow is admitted to a line leading to the heat exchanger
</p>
<p>located near the aft of the cooled portion of the second-stage thrust
</p>
<p>chamber assembly. Heated hydrogen from the exchanger is ducted to
</p>
<p>the ullage space by a large line that is routed external to the
</p>
<p>second-stage tankage. The coolant tubes are orificed at the top so
</p>
<p>that a small portion of the main hydrogen flow is admitted into the
</p>
<p>tubes. The bulk hydrogen flow goes directly into the injector.
</p>
<p>The second-stage fuel thrust chamber valve
</p>
<p>is very similar to the first-stage thrust chamber valves that have
</p>
<p>been previously discussed. This valve is also a three position valve
</p>
<p>(normally closed, fully open, and closed). Two pins allow the valve
</p>
<p>to attain positions two and three. The pintle is in the bottom of
</p>
<p>the valve housing in position three.
</p>
<p>Page III B-36</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The second stage LO flow is ducted from the aft
</p>
<p>end of the oxygen tank to the main propellant valve through a line
</p>
<p>supported on the outside of the LH2 tank. The main propellant valve
</p>
<p>is similar to the other propellant valves except for the throttling
</p>
<p>device that is located downstream of the main valve housing. The
</p>
<p>throttling device consists of a half hemisphere which is fully open
</p>
<p>prior to second-stage ignititon. The half hemisphere gradually closes
</p>
<p>during second-stage operation in order to maintain a constant oxidizer
</p>
<p>injector manifold pressure compatible with LH injector inlet pressure.
</p>
<p>g. Evaluations and Recommendations
</p>
<p>Liquid hydrogen, necessary to feed the TVC
</p>
<p>engines, is expelled from the fuel tank during first-stage operation.
</p>
<p>A loss of ullage pressure will result from expansion of the ullage
</p>
<p>gas or possibly from sloshing during Stage I burning since the Stage II
</p>
<p>pressurizing system is not operative at this time. Therefore, some
</p>
<p>method of maintaining or supplementing the ullage pressure is required.
</p>
<p>Page III B-37</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Prooulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Several methods are available. One method would
</p>
<p>be to allow the LH tank to decay in pressure. If bulk temperature of
</p>
<p>the LH is controlled to -413°F at liftoff the pressure cannot be
</p>
<p>dropped below 56 psia, its equilibrium vapor pressure. Therefore, the
</p>
<p>LH flow rate at ignition will be less than full flow; however, it will
2
</p>
<p>increase to full flow once the heat exchanger supplies the heated
</p>
<p>hydrogen to increase the ullage pressure to 100 psia.
</p>
<p>Helium could be used as the initial pressurization
</p>
<p>fluid if condensation of the gaseous hydrogen becomes a problem. There-
</p>
<p>fore, a helium charge could be inserted into the tank prior to launch.
</p>
<p>A floating barrier between the gaseous hydrogen and the liquid hydrogen
</p>
<p>would minimize condensation.
</p>
<p>Approximately one third of the total ullage
</p>
<p>volume is enclosed in the LH heat exchanger and is not in contact with
£
</p>
<p>the LH liquid surface. It is expected that this isolation will enable
</p>
<p>adequate control of ullage pressure prior to Stage II main engine
</p>
<p>operation. Additional study of this area is required to establish
</p>
<p>system adequacy.
</p>
<p>Page III B-38</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>6. Fill, Vent, and Dump System
</p>
<p>a. Description
</p>
<p>Both fuel and oxidizer systems contain a fill
</p>
<p>line, a horizontal vent line, and a vertical vent line. Dumping is
</p>
<p>achieved by reversing the flow in the fill line and in the vertical
</p>
<p>vent line. All external fill and vent line connection points are
</p>
<p>located around the periphery of the forward compartment of the second-
</p>
<p>stage tankage, Station 180. The second-stage layout drawing illustrates
</p>
<p>the line routing of all fill and vent lines from the forward compartment
</p>
<p>to various tanks. Appendix II-8 provides a more complete functional
</p>
<p>description of the second-stage fill and vent system. Included in this
</p>
<p>appendix is a flow schematic and the operational features of the system.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation
</p>
<p>The components installed for management of
</p>
<p>pressurized compartments are felt to afford adequate flexibility for
</p>
<p>Page III B-39</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>operation of the Sea Dragon vehicle. In all functions it appears
</p>
<p>possible to provide adequate functional checkout of components and
</p>
<p>redundancy.
</p>
<p>7. Auxiliary Thrust Chambers
</p>
<p>a. Description
</p>
<p>The TVC function for the second-stage vehicle
</p>
<p>is accomplished by use of four auxiliary LCU/LH- engines located on the
</p>
<p>exterior of the vehicle, Station 299. These four engines are located
</p>
<p>90 degrees apart on the exterior of the vehicle and can be actuated to
</p>
<p>rotate around an axis which is at 20° to a Station plane. The
</p>
<p>actuation stroke is 170 degrees. The propellant feed for the TVC
</p>
<p>engines is shown in schematic on Figure III-B-9-1;gimbal angle is half
</p>
<p>of this angle. The design performance data is given below:
</p>
<p>TVC Design Performance Parameters
</p>
<p>Design Thrust: 212,800 Ib, 4 engines at 53,200 Ib per engine
</p>
<p>Thrust at Sea Level: 26,700 Ib per engine
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

<p>Page III B-41</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The proposed auxiliary chambers can meet all
</p>
<p>vehicle control force requirements except pitch and yaw of the first
</p>
<p>stage. They provide:
</p>
<p>(1) Roll control for first stage
</p>
<p>(2) Propellant settling acceleration during
</p>
<p>staging
</p>
<p>(3) Pitch, yaw, and roll for second stage
</p>
<p>(4) Pitch, yaw and roll coast period
</p>
<p>(5) Orbital injection of Stage II and spacecraft
</p>
<p>(6) Other Possibilities:
</p>
<p>(a) Retrothrust
</p>
<p>(b) Rendezvous manuevers
</p>
<p>The chambers are of conventional De Laval design
</p>
<p>either ablatively cooled or nonregeneratively cooled with hydrogen.
</p>
<p>Ignition of the small engines occurs underwater at a depth of about
</p>
<p>70 ft. The ignition sequence consists of the following: LO flow is
£
</p>
<p>admitted to the chamber and the ignition fluid (TEA) is fed directly
</p>
<p>Page III 3-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>Ill, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>into the chamber a fraction of a second after the LO^, and combustion
</p>
<p>is initiated. LH? flow is admitted when chamber conditions are satis-
</p>
<p>factory. A more detailed discussion of the ignition system is given in
</p>
<p>Appendix II-7.
</p>
<p>b. Actuation System, TVC Engines
</p>
<p>Vectoring of the engines is accomplished by use
</p>
<p>of electrical actuators. The actuators can rotate the engines to
</p>
<p>various angles so that in combination the four engines provide pitch, yaw,
</p>
<p>and roll control throughout the vehicle flight operations.
</p>
<p>c. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>It appears that the overall vehicle simplicity
</p>
<p>and reliability is enhanced by the auxiliary system because rigid
</p>
<p>mounting of the Stage II main chamber is made possible. Propellant
</p>
<p>used during first-stage burning penalizes second-stage mass fraction.
</p>
<p>Also, the injection of the expended second stage into orbit requires
</p>
<p>greater energy than for the spacecraft and payload alone. These penalties
</p>
<p>are relatively minor and are offset by the operational simplicity
</p>
<p>obtainable.
Page III B-43</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-QENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>There are certain flow interaction problems which
</p>
<p>arise when an external gas source is mounted on the exterior of the
</p>
<p>vehicle. When the gas stream is directed away from the axis of the
</p>
<p>vehicle, the skin heating due to hot-gas impingement is minimized, but
</p>
<p>the axial thrust is decreased. One inherent disadvantage of having the
</p>
<p>TVC engines mounted on the exterior of the vehicle upstream of the
</p>
<p>expandable nozzle is that the structure to support the engines and
</p>
<p>the gas stream, directed into the external flow field, produces shocks
</p>
<p>which alter the flow field around the expandable nozzle. This has the
</p>
<p>effect of causing the expandable nozzle to distort, depending on the
</p>
<p>strength of the shock. These are possible problems which have not been
</p>
<p>investigated. Future work on this vehicle should determine the
</p>
<p>importance of these interactions. It may be possible to move the
</p>
<p>engines forward on the vehicle and alleviate the situation. Heating
</p>
<p>on the missile skin may be controlled by addition of a small patch of
</p>
<p>ablative insulation. It is felt that additional analysis of these areas
</p>
<p>is required to fully define the system.
</p>
<p>Page III B-44</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>8- Performance of Second-Stage Propulsion System
</p>
<p>a. Summary of Characteristics
</p>
<p>The second-stage propulsion system consists of
</p>
<p>the main engine and the 4 auxiliary thrust chambers. The design perform-
</p>
<p>ance of these engines can be found in Sections III, B, 3 and III, B, 7.
</p>
<p>Heat transfer studies of the second-stage engine indicate that the coolant
</p>
<p>flow rate required to cool the engine (260 Ib/sec of hydrogen) results
</p>
<p>in conditions at the end of the tubes as follows: pressure of 20.78
</p>
<p>psia, temperature of 1,092°F, and velocity of 1,525 ft/sec. The conditions
</p>
<p>in the main exhaust stream are as foJLlowsi velocity of 11,773 ft/sec,
</p>
<p>pressure of 2.01 psia, and temperature of 3,460°R. The conditions of
</p>
<p>the coolant gas flow in the tubes is different in addition to a molecular
</p>
<p>weight difference. If the tips of the tubes are pinched to form a
</p>
<p>converging diverging nozzle, then the expansion of the coolant gases
</p>
<p>will produce a velocity of 11,663 ft/sec. It is concluded that the
</p>
<p>theoretical Ig will not be penalized by non-regenerative cooling when
</p>
<p>the coolant is dumped into the main gas stream. The resulting propulsion
</p>
<p>Page III B-45</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>system mass fraction is 0.8905. The specific impulse of the main gas
</p>
<p>stream is given in Figure III-B-9. The performance of the TVC engines
</p>
<p>with altitude are given in Figure III-B-6, -7, and -8. The I of the
s
</p>
<p>TVC engines is virtually constant after fi^st-stage burnout. The I
o
</p>
<p>and thrust are given as they vary with altitude for different mixture
</p>
<p>ratios.
</p>
<p>b. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The performance assumed in the study is
</p>
<p>conservative. Another 1% in specific impulse might easily be claimed.
</p>
<p>9. Weights, Stage II
</p>
<p>a. Summary
</p>
<p>The weights of Stage II are given in tabular
</p>
<p>form in Table III-B-1.
</p>
<p>Page III B-46</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, B, Stage II Propulsion System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The weights of all components have been
</p>
<p>calculated based on the configurations defined. Component definition
</p>
<p>has been based on estimates, where necessary, as is discussed other
</p>
<p>sections.
</p>
<p>b. Evluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The total stage weight is felt to be conservative
</p>
<p>in terms of stage performance. Higher strength to density ratio materials
</p>
<p>such as 18% maraging steel for the LO tank and titanium for LH2 tank will
</p>
<p>improve mass fraction. Such a material combination would increase the
</p>
<p>stage mass fraction to 0.90 from its present value of 0.89.
</p>
<p>The common bulkheads and intertank structure
</p>
<p>should be reviewed for possible weight saving. A substantial portion
</p>
<p>of the Stage II weight exists in the transition skirt on the aft end
</p>
<p>of the LH tank. It is possible that incorporation of pressurized
</p>
<p>tanks in this area, serving the double purpose of taking first-stage
</p>
<p>thrust and storing propellant, would save weight. The low density for
</p>
<p>the LH2 used for design should be reviewed operationally to determine
</p>
<p>if a higher value could be used.
</p>
<p>(End III, B)

</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>C. INTERSTAGE STRUCTURE ASSEMBLIES
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>The interstage structure assemblies of the vehicle
</p>
<p>are the interconnecting structures that transmit the loads between the
</p>
<p>major sections of the vehicle. These structures are:
</p>
<p>a. The cylindrical structure supporting the
</p>
<p>command module and enclosing the service module
</p>
<p>b. The conical structure at the forward end of the
</p>
<p>payload that encloses the payload service module
</p>
<p>c. The cylindrical structure between the payload
</p>
<p>and second stage
</p>
<p>d. The cylindrical structure between the first
</p>
<p>and second stages
</p>
<p>The latter structure, between the first and second
</p>
<p>stages, incorporates provisions for the separation or staging operation
</p>
<p>between the stages as shown in Figures III-C-1 and III-C-2. A more
</p>
<p>Page III C-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>detailed presentation of the structural assembly and staging provisions
</p>
<p>is given later in this section and is representative of the other
</p>
<p>interstage structures and separation provisions.
</p>
<p>Except in the case discussed above, the interstage
</p>
<p>structures do not contain staging provisions because the entire
</p>
<p>vehicle forward of the first stage remains connected through orbit
</p>
<p>attainment. However, there are separation devices for the command module
</p>
<p>for abort and re-entry operations. All interstage structures will be
</p>
<p>designed to provide access as required for assembly and maintenance
</p>
<p>operations. When these structures are pressurized, and emergency
</p>
<p>access is required, airlock provisions will be made for manned entry
</p>
<p>and operations using a pressurized suit.
</p>
<p>2. Interstage Assembly (Stage 1 to Stage 2)
</p>
<p>a. Description and Operation
</p>
<p>This assembly is shown in Figure III-C-1 and
</p>
<p>its basic function is to connect the two stages and to provide for the
</p>
<p>Page III C-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>staging function. It is pressurized up to 40 psi for structural
</p>
<p>stability and to provide a positive separation force during staging.
</p>
<p>A linear shaped charge assembly is located slightly aft of the second-
</p>
<p>stage joint to effect separation of the structure upon command.
</p>
<p>Additional shaped charge assemblies will be used to jettison the
</p>
<p>remaining structure after complete separation to leave a nose
</p>
<p>configuration suited to recovery. The sequence of staging operations
</p>
<p>is shown on Figure III-C-2 and these operations are described in more
</p>
<p>detail later in this section.
</p>
<p>b. Structural Configuration and Material
</p>
<p>t.
</p>
<p>The main interstage structure will be maae of
</p>
<p>i
</p>
<p>18% nickel, maraging steel. Longitudinal stiffeners are provided to
</p>
<p>resist buckling in the unpressurized condition and to assist in
</p>
<p>maintaining structural stability under maximum loading conditions
</p>
<p>when pressurized. This structure, although expendable, will be coated
</p>
<p>to resist salt water corrosion. The expandable nozzle is attached to
</p>
<p>the forward end of this structure that will continue to support the
</p>
<p>nozzle after the staging operation and during the second-stage main
</p>
<p>Page III C-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>engine firing. Therefore, this forward section will be subjected to
</p>
<p>temperatures of 1,000°F from the gas injected just upstream of the joint.
</p>
<p>The use of aluminum was considered, but was dropped because of the
</p>
<p>temperature environment.
</p>
<p>c. Structural Load Path and Pressurization
</p>
<p>The load path is directly along the cylindrical
</p>
<p>structure between the first and second stages. The flanges provide
</p>
<p>full development of the compressive strength and nearly full development
</p>
<p>of the tensile strength.
</p>
<p>The main second-stage engine and the space
</p>
<p>enclosed in the interstage structure will be pressurized to approxi-
</p>
<p>mately 10 psig at the conclusion of the vehicle assembly operation.
</p>
<p>This pressure will stabilize the interstage structure and will prevent
</p>
<p>water entry during the towing to the launch point. The pressure will
</p>
<p>be controllable and may be increased to as much as 40 psia prior to
</p>
<p>launch. This pressure is held by seals at the joints and the flexible
</p>
<p>seal and nozzle support at the aft end of the second-stage nozzle.
</p>
<p>Page III C-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>•III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>d. Assembly
</p>
<p>The main interstage structure is bolted to the
</p>
<p>stub skirt at the forward end of the first stage. This structure will
</p>
<p>be fabricated at the manufacturing plant and attached to the first
</p>
<p>stage before it is towed to the vehicle assembly site. The remainder
</p>
<p>of the interstage assembly will be assembled at the assembly site when
</p>
<p>the first and second stages are mated. The bolted joints provide
</p>
<p>flexibility of operations and ease of replacement of this structure
</p>
<p>after recovery of the first stage.
</p>
<p>The linear shaped charge assemblies are
</p>
<p>located within the structure and, therefore, will be installed before
</p>
<p>mating of the stages. Safety will be provided by remotely-monitored
</p>
<p>safe and arm mechanisms with positive mechanical shutters that are
</p>
<p>reversible to arm or safe the ordnance items by remote control. The
</p>
<p>linear shaped charge assemblies and safe and arm mechanisms are
</p>
<p>enclosed in housings pressurized with dry nitrogen gas, and can with-
</p>
<p>stand submersion during operations.
</p>
<p>Page III C-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>The last stages of assembly will be the mating
</p>
<p>of the first and second stages followed by the assembly of the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle and the segmented straps holding the expandable nozzle. These
</p>
<p>steps are shown in detail in the Operational Plan Section.
</p>
<p>e. Staging Sequence
</p>
<p>The staging sequence between Stages I and II,
</p>
<p>is started by command of the guidance system after the first-stage
</p>
<p>engine has been shut down Figure III-C-2 illustrates the operation.
</p>
<p>The first step is the cutting of the interstage
</p>
<p>structure circumferentially with the forward linear shaped charge
</p>
<p>assembly. Because of the air pressure in the interstage structure,
</p>
<p>separation forces exist between the stages that are immediately
</p>
<p>released when the interstage structure is cut. Because the gas
</p>
<p>pressure acts on the entire cross-sectional area of both stages there
</p>
<p>are equal and opposite forces of large magnitude developed that give
</p>
<p>relative separation accelerations between the stages. A gas
</p>
<p>Page III C-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Stucture Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>obturating seal between the interstage structure and the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle provides capability to the expandable nozzle to withstand this
</p>
<p>pressure. Thirdly, after the first stage has moved aft relative to
</p>
<p>the second stage about 5 - 1 0 ft, the segmented straps on the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle will be jettisoned by means of explosive bolts at each joint.
</p>
<p>These bolts incorporate a piston and cylinder arrangement that provides
</p>
<p>positive outward impulse to the segments so they will move away from
</p>
<p>the nozzle. The nozzle is then free to expand because of the remaining
</p>
<p>gas pressure, and hard contract with the first stage is prevented.
</p>
<p>After the first stage has moved aft, so there
</p>
<p>is considerable space between stages, the fourth operation is
</p>
<p>accomplished, which is the jettisoning of the remaining portion of
</p>
<p>the interstage structure forward of the joint connecting it to the
</p>
<p>first stage. This is accomplished by simuitaneously firing the aft
</p>
<p>linear charge assembly and 16 longitudinal linear charge assemblies
</p>
<p>so that the structure is cut into 16 pieces without damaging the
</p>
<p>joint of the recoverable first stage. This operation removes the
</p>
<p>greatest portion of the structure for improved aerodynamic stability
</p>
<p>Page III C-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>and reduced water impact loading during the subsequent recovery phase.
</p>
<p>The first-stage recovery system includes the sequencing and power
</p>
<p>systems required for this and subsequent operations.
</p>
<p>3. Other Interstage Assemblies
</p>
<p>The other interstage assemblies are less complex
</p>
<p>than the one described above. Their main function-is to provide
</p>
<p>structural interconnections as described below.
</p>
<p>The interstage structure that supports the command
</p>
<p>module is cylindrical and reinforced to provide adequate strength as
</p>
<p>well as sea handling. At the forward end of the interstage structure,
</p>
<p>there is a separation device that is capable of separating the command
</p>
<p>module from the service module upon abort of the flight so the abort
</p>
<p>propulsion system will carrry the command module away from the vehicle,
</p>
<p>At the aft end of the interstage structure, there is a similar separa-
</p>
<p>tion device that operates to separate the command module for re-entry
</p>
<p>under normal operating conditions. Simultaneously, the posigrade
</p>
<p>rocket propulsion system will accelerate the command module away from
</p>
<p>the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III C-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, C, Interstage Structure Assemblies (cont.)
</p>
<p>The second assembly is the interstage structure
</p>
<p>between the payload and the command module. This is a continuation of
</p>
<p>the forward conical section of the payload. Thus, the interstage
</p>
<p>structure of the payload supports the command module and the interstage
</p>
<p>and service module for the command module.
</p>
<p>The interstage structure is between the payload
</p>
<p>and the second stage and is a stiffened aluminum cylinder. This
</p>
<p>section contains the battery power supply and other equipment for
</p>
<p>the vehicle that requires service access. There will be access
</p>
<p>manholes in this portion of the structure so that the servicing and
</p>
<p>assembly can be accomplished. This interstage structure is bolted to
</p>
<p>the payload and to the second stage.
</p>
