</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>D. PAYLOAD
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>The basic payload is assumed to be a tank containing
</p>
<p>one million pounds of liquid hydrogen with a service module attached to
</p>
<p>provide those functions that must be performed by the payload after
</p>
<p>orbit has been achieved and the command module has separated to return
</p>
<p>to earth. Because the second stage and command module are attached to
</p>
<p>this payload, the payload that is placed in circular orbit includes
</p>
<p>the entire vehicle forward of the second stage as show in the Flight
</p>
<p>Sequence illustration, Figure II-3. Details of the vehicle are shown
</p>
<p>in Figure II-l.
</p>
<p>The payload could be varied for other missions to
</p>
<p>provide additional propulsion stages or to provide variations in
</p>
<p>configuration, density and function.
</p>
<p>Page III D-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>The command module is a manned Apollo spacecraft
</p>
<p>that provides the basic guidance, control, and communications functions
</p>
<p>during flight. The module will return to earth using its service module
</p>
<p>to provide separation and recovery functions. An abort propulsion system
</p>
<p>is also provided. If the only function of the command module is to
</p>
<p>ensure attainment of orbit by the LH payload, a considerably smaller
£
</p>
<p>and simpler module similar to Mercury or Gemini could be utilized.
</p>
<p>The payload fuel tank will be constructed of aluminum.
</p>
<p>Provisions are made for fueling and venting in the same manner as the
</p>
<p>liquid hydrogen tank in the second stage. The tank will be insulated
</p>
<p>as necessary to limit heat transfer during transportation, flight, and
</p>
<p>the orbital environment.
</p>
<p>After orbit has been attained and the command module
•»•
</p>
<p>has separated and returned to earth, the remaining payload will continue
</p>
<p>to orbit until rendezvous with a spacecraft. The service module of the
</p>
<p>payload will provide communications and attitude control functions to
</p>
<p>assist in location, identification and docking. Fuel transfer functions
</p>
<p>will also be provided.
</p>
<p>Page III D-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>2. Tankage
</p>
<p>The payload tankage for the liquid hydrogen will be
</p>
<p>a welded aluminum structure with features similar to those of the second-
</p>
<p>stage liquid hydrogen fuel tank. The tank is supported at both ends by
</p>
<p>the respective interstage structures. Provisions will be made for
</p>
<p>loading the payload in a horizontal position and for topping in both
</p>
<p>a horizontal and vertical position.
</p>
<p>The forward end of the payload will be conical to
</p>
<p>provide efficient aerodynamic performance and strength for supporting
</p>
<p>the command module at the forward end. The aft portion of the pressure
</p>
<p>vessel will be hemispherical in shape with a transition section at the
</p>
<p>outer diameter between the spherical and conical sections.
</p>
<p>This configuration has not been optimized for the
</p>
<p>liquid hydrogen payload.
</p>
<p>Page III D-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>The insulation of the tank will be external and of the
</p>
<p>same type provided for the second-stage liquid hydrogen tank. The exact
</p>
<p>thickness used depends on the mission profile, time in orbit, and other
</p>
<p>parameters not investigated in this study.
</p>
<p>3. Service Module for Payload
</p>
<p>The payload service module will contain those functions
</p>
<p>essential to orbit, rendezvous, and fuel transfer of the payload. These
</p>
<p>are:
</p>
<p>a. Command receiving and transmission functions
</p>
<p>for rendezvous
</p>
<p>b. Radio transmission for location and identification
</p>
<p>c. Telemetry system for payload
</p>
<p>d. Attitude control system
</p>
<p>e. Fuel transfer system
</p>
<p>Page III D-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>The attitude control system will be controlled by
</p>
<p>command instructions from the vehicle that is attempting to rendezvous
</p>
<p>with the payload. It is considered feasible to use the ullage pressure
</p>
<p>of hydrogen in the payload to orient the payload on command by the use
</p>
<p>of payload attitude control rockets. The hydrogen will provide consider-
</p>
<p>able specific impulse because of its low molecular weight, even though
</p>
<p>it is quite cold.
</p>
<p>The power supply for the payload service module will
</p>
<p>be the main power supply for the vehicle. It is located between the
</p>
<p>second stage and the payload, because the second stage will remain
</p>
<p>with the payload in this version of the vehicle mission.
</p>
<p>The design of the attitude control system and the
</p>
<p>fuel transfer system for rendezvous operation involves equipment and
</p>
<p>techniques that are considered beyond the scope of this study.
</p>
<p>Page III D-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-eENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>4. Command Module
</p>
<p>The command module contains the main vehicle guidance
</p>
<p>and control systems as well as the communeiations system for communicating
</p>
<p>with ground control stations. The command module is located at the
</p>
<p>forward end of the vehicle and will be attached at the assembly site
</p>
<p>before towing to the launching site. All checkout of the equipment
</p>
<p>will be made prior to this towing, operation.
</p>
<p>For the purposes of the present study, it was assumed
</p>
<p>that the command module is a manned module of the Apollo type. Depending
</p>
<p>on further mission definition, a considerably simpler spacecraft such as
</p>
<p>Mercury or Gemini may be adequate. All of the applicable equipment and
</p>
<p>operational techniques for the Apollo program are assumed to be
</p>
<p>developed and available, including the life support system, at the time
•̂
</p>
<p>of the first flight. The manned concept does not preclude the use of
</p>
<p>an unmanned command module that would be physically interchangeable with
</p>
<p>the manned module.
</p>
<p>Page III D-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>Primary equipment that will be in the command module
</p>
<p>for vehicle operations will be as follows:
</p>
<p>a. Main Vehicle Guidance and Control System
</p>
<p>The main vehicle guidance and control system
</p>
<p>including all computers, autopilots, sequencing equipment, and related
</p>
<p>checkout equipment will be in the command module. This system will alsp
</p>
<p>provide for the separation, propulsion, and control and guidance functions
</p>
<p>necessary for recovery of the module. A further description of the main
</p>
<p>vehicle guidance and control system is contained in another part of this
</p>
<p>report.
/
</p>
<p>b. Electrical Power Supply
</p>
<p>The electrical power supply for the' command
</p>
<p>module will be a battery pack that is activated after the vehicle has
</p>
<p>been erected, or it will be the type of power supply available from
</p>
<p>the Apollo program if applicable.
</p>
<p>Page III D-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont.)
</p>
<p>It will have the capability for operating the
</p>
<p>essential functions for the command module throughout the flight and
</p>
<p>recovery operations. Additional power will be provided by the main
</p>
<p>• vehicle electrical power supply system where command module weight can
</p>
<p>be saved.
</p>
<p>c. Communications Receivers and Transmitters
</p>
<p>Provisions for two-way communications will be
</p>
<p>made. The total extent of the communications will depend on further
</p>
<p>studies of the operational requirements for the mission and will
</p>
<p>include all aspects of voice, command functions, telemetry, and
</p>
<p>tracking. Additional telemetry transmitters may be used in the
</p>
<p>vehicle outside of the command module to save weight and complexity
</p>
<p>of this module.
</p>
<p>d. Instrumentation and Observation Equipment
</p>
<p>Instrumentation and observation equipment
</p>
<p>will be provided in the module as required to provide maximum safety
</p>
<p>Page III D-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, D, Payload (cont . )
</p>
<p>for the crew and monitoring for the entire operation so that information
</p>
<p>can be transmitted to the command ship or action taken aboard the command
</p>
<p>module to correct performance or to abort the mission. Automatic
</p>
<p>recording and telemetry will be provided for all critical data as an
</p>
<p>integral part of the checkout and operation of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>e. Abort System
</p>
<p>For the manned command module, an abort system
</p>
<p>will provide automatic abort with manual override under some situations
</p>
<p>to provide as a whole maximum manned safety and maximum probability of
</p>
<p>mission success.
</p>
<p>Abort provisions will be made for the entire
</p>
<p>vehicle at launch during an initial period of a few seconds after the
</p>
<p>start of the first-stage engines and before the flight is committed.
</p>
<p>That is, if it is evident that the first-stage engine firing or other
</p>
<p>critical operations are not satisfactory, it is possible to shut
</p>
<p>down the engines and abort the mission without destroying the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III D-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>I I I , D, Payload ( c o n t . )
</p>
<p>This is possible at this time because .the vehicle will not be seriously
</p>
<p>damaged by allowing it to settle back into the sea.
</p>
<p>5. Service Module for Command Module
</p>
<p>The service module for the command module will be
</p>
<p>essentially the same type that will be provided for the Apollo earth
</p>
<p>orbit mission command module. The propulsion systems for earth re-entry
</p>
<p>are the major elements of this service module. In addition, however,
</p>
<p>there will be included any support equipment that will reduce the total
</p>
<p>weight and complexity of the command module.
</p>
<p>The service module will contain:
</p>
<p>a. Posigrade rocket system for separation
</p>
<p>b. Retrograde rocket system for de-orbit
v
</p>
<p>c. Stage separation systems for abort and for
</p>
<p>re-entry operations
</p>
<p>d. Support equipment for the command and
</p>
<p>service modules.
</p>
<p>Page III D-10 (End III, D)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>E. CONTROL SYSTEM
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>In the present design, all of Sea Dragon attitude
</p>
<p>control is achieved by jet forces; neither moveable nor fixed fins
</p>
<p>are employed. Directional control moments for the vehicle during
</p>
<p>first-stage operation are developed by rotation of the entire first-
</p>
<p>stage engine, which is gimbaled. Movement of the system is obtained
</p>
<p>by means of piston-type actuators and an open-loop hydraulic system
</p>
<p>fed by the main fuel tank at 4OO psi. An estimated 40,000 lb of RP-1
</p>
<p>is dumped after use by the control system. Second-stage directional
</p>
<p>control is accomplished by four auxiliary L0_-hydrogen engines, each
</p>
<p>pivoted and electrically actuated to provide a maximum side thrust
</p>
<p>component of - 53,20O lb - or an equivalent deflection of the main
</p>
<p>engine thrust equal to 0.43 degrees. These engines provide roll con-
</p>
<p>trol throughout all stages of flight in addition to directional con-
</p>
<p>trol after completion of first-stage operation.
</p>
<p>Page III E-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Analyses of the control system have consisted mainly
</p>
<p>of evaluating performance capabilities of the preliminary design,
</p>
<p>which was based on estimates, while also defining a more complete set
</p>
<p>of requirements for the final design. In this process, consideration
</p>
<p>was given to the effects of imperfections in the vehicle itself and of
</p>
<p>perturbations caused by its environment and its program. Wind and sea
</p>
<p>conditions, thrust and aerodynamic misalignments, maneuver programs,
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing, and body bending, therefore, were analyzed.
</p>
<p>Excepting the two latter effects, it was found that the original
</p>
<p>estimated -3 rotation of the first-stage main engine provides more than
</p>
<p>adequate control capability. Propellant sloshing and body bending
</p>
<p>influence mainly the control loop stability,, which was not completely
</p>
<p>analyzed. Preliminary results indicated that sloshing will not be
</p>
<p>particularly troublesome because the first body bending mode frequency
</p>
<p>is at least three times the first slosh mode frequency. However, body
</p>
<p>bending coupling possibilities will require careful selection of loop
</p>
<p>gain, instrument location, and actuation system lag. For this evalua-
</p>
<p>tion, a simplified autopilot design was selected on the basis of a
</p>
<p>single-plane, rigid-body analysis of first-stage attitude control.
</p>
<p>Page III E-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The second-stage system requirement is determined
</p>
<p>primarily by the effects of thrust misalignment, although staging,
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing, wind and maneuver program conditions were also
</p>
<p>examined. It was found that the combined effects of thrust misalign-
</p>
<p>ment and wind require a directional trimming moment corresponding to .
</p>
<p>o .
0.37 main thrust deflection. To obtain some performance margin above
</p>
<p>the 0.43 present design capability, the auxiliary engine size will be
</p>
<p>increased; however, a more complete, analysis of the system requirements
</p>
<p>will be made first. In particular, the effect and likelihood of con-
</p>
<p>trol system saturation because of propellant sloshing must be considered
</p>
<p>when specifying the required performance margin. The auxiliary engines
</p>
<p>provide the vehicle roll correction. In addition, they provide orbital
</p>
<p>injection without a restart requirement, a retrothrust capability if
</p>
<p>second-stage recovery is considered, and a capability that is difficult
</p>
<p>to achieve with the single first-stage gimbaled engine. For both
</p>
<p>stages, alternate TVC concepts have been, studied and will be reviewed
</p>
<p>in follow-on vehicle design phases.
</p>
<p>Page III E-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>2. TVC Requirements
</p>
<p>Of the many considerations that determine control
</p>
<p>system requirements, those that are discussed in this section pertain
</p>
<p>primarily to the thrust vector deflection characteristics. The most
</p>
<p>important considerations of physical properties, such as simplicity,
</p>
<p>reliability, state of development, adaptability for packaging, and
</p>
<p>suitability for use in the water environment are discussed briefly.
</p>
<p>A full description of the TVC system selected is given in Section III,A.
</p>
<p>a. First Stage
</p>
<p>At the outset of this study, it was estimated
</p>
<p>that the first-stage TVC requirement would be for 3 maximum deflection
</p>
<p>of the main engine thrust. Subsequent analyses have shown that a
•»•
</p>
<p>lesser requirement (closer to 1 1/2 ) probably will suffice; however,
</p>
<p>many aspects of the control system analysis remain to be done. Some
</p>
<p>influences on the control loop stability have been analyzed, and are
</p>
<p>Page III E-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AERO J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>discussed later in this section; however, most of these results are
</p>
<p>on the basis of uncoupled operation of the system under quasi-steady-
</p>
<p>state conditions. Discussion of first-stage control is confined to
</p>
<p>directional control requirements; roll control is included as part of
</p>
<p>the second-stage discussion because it is effected by second stage
</p>
<p>engines.
</p>
<p>The principal factors that determine the ..
</p>
<p>directional TVC requirements for first-stage operation are' thrust
</p>
<p>misalignment, aerodynamic misalignment, launch transients (wind and
</p>
<p>sea affects), pitch maneuver program, upper atmosphere wind and pro-
</p>
<p>pellant sloshing. Their effects are discussed separately below:
</p>
<p>Thrust misalignment - Although it is not
</p>
<p>known with certainty what degree of mechanical alignment can be main-
</p>
<p>tained in a. structure of Sea Dragon dimensions, it is estimated that
</p>
<p>the equivalent of 1/4 thrust alignment accuracy can be achieved, and
</p>
<p>this value has been used in all analyses to date. In reality, thrust
</p>
<p>misalignment is made up of many components such as flow misalignment
</p>
<p>Page III E-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>of the mechanical assemblies, bias in the actuation system, and
</p>
<p>lateral displacements of the combined center of gravity with respect
</p>
<p>to the reference center line of the vehicle. Most of these components
</p>
<p>vary with time as the propellant in the system is used and the struc-
</p>
<p>tures are exposed to changing loading and heating conditions. 'Also,
</p>
<p>these components are randomly distributed and a statistical analysis
</p>
<p>of their combined effect eventually is required to completely define
</p>
<p>the characteristic. Although the Sea Dragon scale difference is
</p>
<p>recognized, experience with the largest rocket motors to date would
</p>
<p>indicate that the value of 1/4° thrust misalignment is a reasonable
</p>
<p>assumption.
</p>
<p>Aerodynamic misalignment - An analysis has not
</p>
<p>been made to determine the magnitude of aerodynamic misalignment;
</p>
<p>however, because the vehicle is not equipped with predominant aero-
</p>
<p>dynamic surfaces, such as wings or fins, it is believed that the over-
</p>
<p>all aerodynamic misalignment effectiveness will be almost negligible.
</p>
<p>To further assure that it is minimized, every effort is made in the
</p>
<p>Page III E-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>vehicle configuration to retain features of symmetry. This is done not
</p>
<p>only to minimize aerodynamic contributions to directional moments, but
</p>
<p>also to avoid coupling with the roll characteristics.
</p>
<p>Launch transients - Associated with underwater
</p>
<p>launch are certain unique requirements for control. These are derived
</p>
<p>from the fact that the vehicle is in motion prior to ignition and
</p>
<p>"flies" through a high density medium wherein it can develop high q
</p>
<p>effects at low velocity. The vehicle's emergence into surface winds
</p>
<p>while its aft end is still immersed and somewhat constrained creates
</p>
<p>another unusual effect. In addition&gt; the water environment restricts
</p>
<p>the control surface (nozzle) motions; however, as velocity is reached,
</p>
<p>it also increases the gain of the system; that is, less deflection is
</p>
<p>needed to achieve a given moment.
</p>
<p>There are certain advantages in leaving the
</p>
<p>first-stage control system "locked-out" until the vehicle has cleared
</p>
<p>the water. These advantages relate primarily to ballast staging con-
</p>
<p>siderations. Because the locked control case would result in the
</p>
<p>Page III E-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>largest vehicle dispersion during launch, it was analyzed. The result
</p>
<p>o
showed that vehicle motion characterized by 5.8 pitch and 1.7 degree/
</p>
<p>sec pitch rate could be developed by the combined effects, considering
</p>
<p>wind, sea condition, and thrust misalignment. This analysis, and
</p>
<p>others, are presented in Section V,B. Subsequently, it was determined
</p>
<p>that the control system can overcome these effects by 1 ̂  deflection
</p>
<p>for1 5 sec.
</p>
<p>Kick maneuver - The vehicle pitch maneuver
</p>
<p>required to program into the gravity turn has been estimated to require
</p>
<p>thrust deflection of O.75 for approximately 8 sec, which in turn
</p>
<p>achieves a pitch rotation of six to eight degrees, as discussed in
</p>
<p>Section III, F, 3. Further trajectory studies will determine a
</p>
<p>definite command pitch rate program for trajectory optimization.
</p>
<p>Wind - The influence of surface winds was
</p>
<p>included in the analysis of launch transients as discussed previously.
</p>
<p>Subsequently, in the atmospheric flight, wind effects continue through-
</p>
<p>out most of the first-phase flight. A discussion of the wind-produced
</p>
<p>angle of attack is given in Appendix VI-Q. A typical expected TVC
</p>
<p>Page III E-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>deflection history because of wind conditions is included in this
</p>
<p>appendix. The wind condition that exists at the time when the
</p>
<p>vehicle reaches its peak value is of most interest. It was found
</p>
<p>that the most severe requirement because of wind occurs 25 sec after
</p>
<p>launch and leads to a TVC requirement of 0.95 . This was on the basis
</p>
<p>of conditions produced by zero drift.
</p>
<p>For the Sea Dragon vehicle wind conditions may
</p>
<p>not have to be treated as having completely random direction; for a
</p>
<p>particular launch location, such as Cape Canaveral,. prevailing winds
</p>
<p>can be assumed to pertain since the launch direction is always the
</p>
<p>same.
</p>
<p>Sloshing - Propellant sloshing affects the
</p>
<p>control system as more of a transient influence than the factors cited
</p>
<p>above; however, it is not expected to have a strong effect on the
</p>
<p>required thrust vector deflection. In Section V,B,11, sloshing con-
</p>
<p>siderations are further discussed.
</p>
<p>Page III E-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N -—
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Conclusion - Although further work must be done
</p>
<p>to fully define the combinations of effects on TVC requirements during
</p>
<p>first stage, indications at present are that a thrust vector deflec-
</p>
<p>tion of less than 3 will be sufficient. Because the present system
</p>
<p>o
is designed to provide the 3 requirement, it is believed to be con-
</p>
<p>servative in this regard.
</p>
<p>b. Second Stage
</p>
<p>For auxiliary engines suitably pivoted are
</p>
<p>used for deriving the control moments necessary for second-stage
</p>
<p>steering. In addition, these auxiliary motors, operating in a mode of
</p>
<p>differential deflection, provide the roll control capability of the
</p>
<p>vehicle throughout the first stage of flight. At the time of staging,
</p>
<p>the auxiliary motors are switched to a mode of. operation in which they
</p>
<p>can provide combined directional and roll control, which continues to
</p>
<p>the point where orbit injection is achieved for the entire upper-stage
</p>
<p>system. An evaluation of roll moment requirements has not been made;
</p>
<p>Page III E-1O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>however, it is conservatively estimated that auxiliary engines suit-
</p>
<p>ably sized for directional control of the second stage will have ample
</p>
<p>capability for roll stabilization and maneuver of the entire vehicle
</p>
<p>during its first stage of flight.
</p>
<p>During second-stage operation, the principal
</p>
<p>considerations in deriving thrust vector control requirements are the
</p>
<p>influences of the staging process, thrust misalignment, propellant
</p>
<p>sloshing, and the pitch program requirement. Because the system is
</p>
<p>designed for staging at low-q condition (100 psf maximum), the
</p>
<p>aerodynamic effects will be negligibly small. A staging altitude of
d
</p>
<p>more than 125,000 ft also precludes the encounter of any seriously
</p>
<p>high-q wind effects. If it is conservatively assumed, however, that
</p>
<p>o
an angle of attack as high as 1 occurs because of any effects at the
</p>
<p>time of staging, the corresponding aerodynamic moment will require a
</p>
<p>o
thrust vector deflection of 0.12 . (As in subsequent discussion, the
</p>
<p>quoted figures are equivalent deflections of the main engine thrust,
</p>
<p>not deflection of auxiliary engine thrust.) As discussed previously,
</p>
<p>Page III E-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>it is estimated that the Sea Dragon engines will be characterized by
</p>
<p>a thrust misalignment of no more than ^ . Using this value, the com-
</p>
<p>bined aerodynamic and thrust misalignment effects call for a thrust
</p>
<p>o
vector deflection of 0.37 . As in the first-stage case, the control
</p>
<p>requirements uniquely associated with propellant sloshing in the
</p>
<p>second-stage system have not been determined, but is is believed that
</p>
<p>they will be of a transient nature. The second-stage TVC auxiliary
</p>
<p>o
engines as presently designed will provide 0.43 equivalent deflection
</p>
<p>o
in the plane of one pair of engines or 0.6 in a plane half-way
</p>
<p>between the pairs of engines, which is the combined plane. It is
</p>
<p>evident from these considerations that additional capability must be
</p>
<p>added to the second-stage TVG system because little margin exists
</p>
<p>above presently known demands. The most straightforward way of doing
</p>
<p>this is to increase the size; however, this and other alternatives,
</p>
<p>such as Canard systems will be examined in the next study phase.
</p>
<p>Page III E-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>3. Selection of TVC System Type
</p>
<p>The selection of a gimbaled engine system for the
</p>
<p>first-stage TVC was made primarily because of its simplicity and suc-
</p>
<p>cessful use in other applications. Historically, its use imposes the
</p>
<p>least propulsion penalty of all candidate systems. The concept is
</p>
<p>well understood and thoroughly proven and may be regarded as being
</p>
<p>within current state-of-the-art. For these reasons, its reliability
</p>
<p>is considered to be excellent; however, it imposes problems in
</p>
<p>actuation, flex line requirements, and in transmitting large loads
</p>
<p>through a relatively restricted structure. The latter consideration
</p>
<p>is particularly important in Sea Dragon as regards the load conditions
</p>
<p>at water re-entry. Also, the use of the gimbaled single engine
</p>
<p>requires an auxiliary system to provide roll control capability.
</p>
<p>Other systems under consideration are those involving
</p>
<p>secondary injection, which is attractive from a structural standpoint,
</p>
<p>but which has greater complexity and perhaps greater limitation for
</p>
<p>underwater operations. By comparison to the gimbaled system, however,
</p>
<p>its most attractive feature is in the simplicity of actuation requirements
</p>
<p>Page III E-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>i
</p>
<p>Another applicable concept that has been given
</p>
<p>limited study employs auxiliary engines. Aside from the additional
</p>
<p>complexity of having four engines instead of one, the auxiliary engine
</p>
<p>system has many of the attractive features of the simple gimbaled
</p>
<p>engine. In addition, and perhaps 'the most outstanding attraction of
</p>
<p>this concept, is the fact that it can be developed and checked out
</p>
<p>independently of the main engine.
</p>
<p>For the second-stage TVC system, improved auxiliary
</p>
<p>engines are being given attention as previously indicated. This
</p>
<p>initial selection was on the basis of consideration of the expandable
</p>
<p>nozzle, which tends to preclude the use of secondary injection and
</p>
<p>gimbaling.
</p>
<p>In Section III,A, the physical and mechanical
</p>
<p>characteristics of the TVC systems and alternates under consideration
</p>
<p>are discussed in detail.
</p>
<p>Page III E-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>Only the case of rigid-body pitch control at
</p>
<p>maximum dynamic pressure has been included in this discussion. This
</p>
<p>analysis should be repeated for all flight conditions and should also
</p>
<p>be applied to pitch control of the second-stage vehicle and roll con-
</p>
<p>trol of the system in all phases of flight.
</p>
<p>b. Propellant Sloshing Effects
</p>
<p>No attempt is made here to present a detailed
</p>
<p>stability analysis of the control system with the combined effects of
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing and body bending. The stability of a vehicle such
</p>
<p>as the Sea Dragon, which is controlled by an autopilot control system,
</p>
<p>is dependent on the interaction of the elastic fuselage, liquid pro-
</p>
<p>pellant, and feedback control system.
</p>
<p>The fundamental frequencies for the propellant
</p>
<p>slosh modes, as derived in Section V, B, 9, may be seen to be in the
</p>
<p>same range of frequencies as the control frequency. However, this
</p>
<p>Page III E-20</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>situation does not appear to cause instability when one considers the
</p>
<p>approximate root locus of the control loop with undamped sloshing
</p>
<p>dipoles as shown in Figure III-E-3. This analysis assumes no inter-
</p>
<p>action between body bending and slosh modes, which is reasonable for
</p>
<p>a preliminary analysis because the body bending frequencies are at
</p>
<p>least three times the first sloshing mode frequency. The particular
</p>
<p>case shown is for a rigid vehicle at t = 33 sec with the assumption
</p>
<p>made that the second-stage tanks are so full that only the sloshing
</p>
<p>of the first-stage propellants need be considered. In conclusion,
</p>
<p>it may be said that stability problems caused by sloshing during
</p>
<p>first-stage firing do not appear troublesome; however, a more detailed
</p>
<p>study of all flight conditions is necessary.
</p>
<p>c. Body Bending
</p>
<p>(1) The body mode frequencies are derived in
Section IV.
</p>
<p>(2) For this example, the body mode fre-
</p>
<p>quencies are approximately: first mode = 14 rad/sec, second mode =
</p>
<p>24 rad/sec, third mode = 33 rad/sec.
</p>
<p>Page III E-21</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The above discussion assumed a rigid body.
</p>
<p>However, missile flexibility induces adverse effects upon vehicle
</p>
<p>loads, vehicle stability, and bending mode coupling through instruments
</p>
<p>in the missile control system. Airloads are induced by local in-
</p>
<p>creases in angle of attack caused by missile bending which in turn
</p>
<p>results in additional deflection and loads. Because Sea Dragon is
</p>
<p>aerodynamically unstable, gusts will produce attitude perturbations
</p>
<p>that amplify the aerodynamic loading. To minimize the loading, a
</p>
<p>fast.control system is needed. This implies high loop gains that
</p>
<p>almost assure a coupling problem with one or more elastic modes.
</p>
<p>The presence of bending makes it impossible to
</p>
<p>sense pure rigid body motion with the position and particularly the
</p>
<p>rate gyros. The first mode is the most pronounced and is generally
</p>
<p>compensated for by placing the rate gyro slightly aft of the antinode,
</p>
<p>resulting in phase stabilization. Amplitude compensation may be
</p>
<p>required for the higher modes, since the instrument placement is fixed
</p>
<p>by the first mode requirements.
</p>
<p>Page III E-22</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>In any case, a detailed study is required of
</p>
<p>the many structural modes to ensure the stability of the control
</p>
<p>loop. This must also include details of the hydraulic actuation
</p>
<p>system since its characteristics at body mode frequencies are very
</p>
<p>important.
</p>
<p>Detailed simulations are required to determine
</p>
<p>the effect of nonlinearities, coupling between sloshing modes and
</p>
<p>bending body modes, and many other complicating details.
</p>
<p>d. Steering Loop Dynamics
</p>
<p>Several factors effect the stability of the
</p>
<p>steering loop and must be considered in the system design. The
</p>
<p>inertial platform accelerometers respond to the missile pitching rate
</p>
<p>if it is not located on the e.g. A location forward of the e.g. is
</p>
<p>preferable to an aft location but in any case its effect should be
</p>
<p>considered. Sampling rates and quantitization errors also effect the
</p>
<p>steering loop stability. The autopilot response and variations in its
</p>
<p>response must be considered along with the above mentioned factors.
</p>
<p>Page III E-23</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, E, Control System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Various sources of errors can be evaluated
</p>
<p>within the context of the steering loop such as rate sensor null
</p>
<p>errors, thrust misalignments, center of gravity notions, etc., that
</p>
<p>can cause actual or effective attitude errors.
</p>
<p>5. Measurement of Gimbal Angle
</p>
<p>Several concepts have been considered to provide an
</p>
<p>.effective gimbal action for the first stage engine. Whatever con-
</p>
<p>figuration is used it is important that a direct accurate measure of
</p>
<p>the engine orientation with respect to the missile be provided. If
</p>
<p>the actuator displacement were used as an indirect measure of the engine
</p>
<p>orientation then the actuator linkage compliance would have a desta-
</p>
<p>bilizing effect on the gimbal actuation servo. Furthermore, steady
</p>
<p>state errors could prohibit this approach.
</p>

</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>F. GUIDANCE SYSTEM
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>a. System Performance and General Characteristics
</p>
<p>The fully inertial guidance system proposed
</p>
<p>for the Sea Dragon launch and orbital injection vehicle has been
</p>
<p>specially adapted for vehicles launched at a sea site sufficiently
</p>
<p>close to shore Loran installations. The system is characterized by:
</p>
<p>(1) capability for full self-alignment at sea, both in azimuth and
</p>
<p>vertical directions, through gyrocompass and stable-vertical search
</p>
<p>modes, under the conditions of existing roll and pitch while afloat;
</p>
<p>(2) necessity for a prelaunch insertion of initial position and veloc-
</p>
<p>ity conditions obtained from shore installations, momitored continuously
</p>
<p>and mechanized in the vehicle guidance system; (3) a fully self-
</p>
<p>contained inertial performance during the flight mode of the vehicle
</p>
<p>from launce to injection into orbit.
</p>
<p>Page III F-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The analyzed accuracy of the guidance system
</p>
<p>is expected to be such that the injection of the payload into a
</p>
<p>nominal circular orbit of 300-nm altitude will be achieved with a
</p>
<p>confidence of 98% that the following tolerances will not exceeded:
</p>
<p>(a) Orbit altitude at inection: 300 t 4.27 nm
</p>
<p>(b) Eccentricity established orbit: 0.005 max
</p>
<p>(c) Difference between altitude at apogee
</p>
<p>and perigee; that is maximum change in altitude of injected satellite
</p>
<p>for one full orbital circle is 37.5 nm
</p>
<p>The injection into orbit will be accomplished
</p>
<p>at a point about 4,100 nm downrange from the launch site, 22.4 min
</p>
<p>after countdown 0.
</p>
<p>The error budget drawn up by the performance
</p>
<p>analysis has assigned the following component and parameter tolerances,
</p>
<p>which are based on the foregoing injection requirements:
</p>
<p>Page III F-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Uncompensated gyro drift rate = 0.022 /hr
</p>
<p>Initial platform misalignment = 1/3 min of arc
</p>
<p>-4
Accelerometer error =10 g
</p>
<p>Initial position determined error = 2,000 ft =
0.33 nm
</p>
<p>Initial velocity determination error = 3.16
knots
</p>
<p>These values are within the state-of-the-art,
</p>
<p>except for the initial alignment accuracy, in a self-aligning mode and
</p>
<p>at sea. Consequently, this mode of operation will require a rather
</p>
<p>heavy gyrocompass and stable-vertical erection system, more of a naval-
</p>
<p>ordnance type than of an avionics type.
</p>
<p>Systems growth capability will include the
</p>
<p>addition of radio-command guidance and possibly stellar monitoring as
</p>
<p>a further improvement in system accuracy and reliability and for mission
</p>
<p>growth. Further, in the orbital mode, the addition of a horizontal
</p>
<p>scanner and other auxiliary equipment is anticipated as a necessity for
</p>
<p>adequate attitude control.
</p>
<p>Page III F-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>A significant factor influencing the study of
</p>
<p>guidance concepts is that there may be a human crew aboard. Accord-
</p>
<p>ingly, the system reliability must be man-rated, and provisions must
</p>
<p>be made for the crew to become an important link in the orbital mode
</p>
<p>of operation.
</p>
<p>While an entirely automatic operation is
</p>
<p>envisioned, the crew must be able to provide certain overrides, to
</p>
<p>insert radioed guidance information and to take action in possible
</p>
<p>mission abort situations.
</p>
<p>b. Physical Characteristics
</p>
<p>The guidance system proposed for the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>consists of two main assemblies, one containing the miniaturized
</p>
<p>guidance computer and associated electronics, and the other, a rela-
</p>
<p>tively heavy stable platform. This equipment will be located in the
</p>
<p>command modules of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III F-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>'III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The computer, which uses transistor components
</p>
<p>3
and solid wiring, weighs 100 Ib, occupies l.O ft , and consumes 100
</p>
<p>watts of electric power, and has a storage capacity of about 8,000
</p>
<p>words. This may be compared to an existing prototype digital computer
</p>
<p>3
using printed circuitry weighing 35 Ib, occupying 0.3 ft , and consuming
</p>
<p>30 watts of electric power. The actual choice will be primarily
</p>
<p>determined by reliability considerations. .The entire computer and
</p>
<p>electronics package is thermal- and shock-insulated, and its estimated
</p>
<p>physical characteristics are as follows: weight, 200 Ib; and volume,
</p>
<p>3
7 ft . The estimated power requirements are: 300 watts at 26v,
</p>
<p>400 cps. The design of the package will be such as to allow operation
</p>
<p>in the space environment.
</p>
<p>The accuracy requirements for self-alignment
</p>
<p>of the stable platform call for gyros with very high angular momentum,
</p>
<p>and therefore high moments of inertia. This requirement will lead to
</p>
<p>a considerably increased size and weight of stable platform by com-
</p>
<p>parison with existing units for avionics applications. Anticipated
</p>
<p>Page III F-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>physical characteristics for a stable platform unit located within
</p>
<p>3
the pressurized capsule are: weight, 400 Ib, volume 6 ft , and 1 kw
</p>
<p>peak power demand. By comparison, a typical fully developed min-
</p>
<p>iature platform for avionics applications weight 33--| Ib, occupies
</p>
<p>3
0.65 ft of space, and consumes 230 watts peak power.
</p>
<p>In addition, .a certain allowance for radio
</p>
<p>equipment and for a telesextant to be used by the astronauts may be
</p>
<p>included. A horizon scanner, probably mounted on a secondary stable
</p>
<p>platform slaved to the master unit, will be used in the orbital mode
</p>
<p>to establish the local vertical.
</p>
<p>c. Special Features
</p>
<p>The rather special conditions existing at the
</p>
<p>launch of the vehicle floating at sea in the vertical position impose
</p>
<p>the requirements of an accurate self-alignment of the stable platform
</p>
<p>Page III F-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>in the required azimuth and vertical directions. Such an alignment
</p>
<p>cannot be performed in a conventional manner through an optical
</p>
<p>alignment system based outside of the vehicle. Instead, the highly
</p>
<p>accurate gyropendulum self-erecting system of the vehicle stable
</p>
<p>platform will operate during this mode as a self-contained north and
</p>
<p>vertical seeking element. A simplified block diagram of the self-
</p>
<p>alignment mode is shown in Figure III-F-1.
</p>
<p>During the flight-navigation mode of the
</p>
<p>inertial guidance system after launch, which is diagrammed in
</p>
<p>Figure III-F-2, the use of the all-inertial system will obviate the
</p>
<p>necessity for a network of ground stations along the ephemeris of the
</p>
<p>inertial 4,100 nm downrange flight of the vehicle till its injection
</p>
<p>into orbit. Moreover, the guidance system of the type proposed here
</p>
<p>will provide complete freedom of selection of orbital planes of the
</p>
<p>vehicle and of the injection trajectory. In fact, it is expected to be
</p>
<p>sufficiently flexible to accommodate future growth or expansion to the
</p>
<p>required modes of operation, mission and maneuverability requirements,
</p>
<p>orbital navigation with full attitude control, and finally a re-entry
</p>
<p>with or without active participation of the crew.
</p>
<p>Page III F-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AERO J E T - G E N E R A L CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The location of the guidance system, i.e., of
</p>
<p>the stable platform, the associated electronics and digital computer
</p>
<p>packages, the horizon scanner and radio transponders, in the command
</p>
<p>module of the Sea Dragon vehicle will ensure a complete recoverability
</p>
<p>of this expensive precision equipment, because the command module will
</p>
<p>be designed with full capability for controlled re-entry.
</p>
<p>An essential simplification of the overall
</p>
<p>system is expected to be achieved through a judicious application of
</p>
<p>the multifunction principle to various sytem elements, networks, sub-
</p>
<p>systems, etc., so that they could be switched sequentially into different
</p>
<p>modes of operation, as required. On the other hand, the demand for a
</p>
<p>very high level of reliability may, in general, require an emphasis on
</p>
<p>overdesign in some areas as well as a planned redundancy of the most
</p>
<p>critical subsystems and channels of operation. For example, marked
</p>
<p>simplification of the guidance and control equipment is achieved by
</p>
<p>using the vehicle-borne equipment to perform in sequential modes of
</p>
<p>Page III F-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>operation the various prelaunch checkout and alignment functions and
</p>
<p>the airborne guidance. Test and checkout equipment is simplified by
</p>
<p>using the airborne digital computer to check itself functionally and
</p>
<p>to monitor the performance of the guidance system during all modes
</p>
<p>of operation.
</p>
<p>2. General Considerations of Guidance Problems
</p>
<p>a. Introduction
</p>
<p>By guidance is generally meant the totality of
</p>
<p>operations required to: (1) locate the position of a vehicle during
</p>
<p>flight; (2) determine the magnitude and direction of its velocity;
</p>
<p>(3) compute from this data, and from the known location of the desti-
</p>
<p>nation, what corrective steering action, if any, is required.
</p>
<p>The major phases into which these, operations
</p>
<p>can be grouped are: (1) prelaunch mod.e including self-alignment; (2)
</p>
<p>injection mode during which the principal rocket engines of the vehicle
</p>
<p>Page III F-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>are in operation; (3) orbital mode,carried out by on-board propulsion
</p>
<p>system; and (4) terminal phase consisting of re-entry and touchdown
</p>
<p>of the command module.
</p>
<p>Even though the Sea Dragon is an ascent vehicle,
</p>
<p>the guidance system specifications must reflect requirements generated
</p>
<p>for all these phases.
</p>
<p>Devices able to generate guidance information
</p>
<p>fall into two classes: those that make the measurements from within
</p>
<p>the vehicle itself (self-contained guidance); and those that make the
</p>
<p>measurements with the aid of guidance stations external to the vehicle.
</p>
<p>As with navigation in the past, different methods are not competitive
</p>
<p>but complementary. Classification by different guidance techniques
</p>
<p>such as "zero-draft" or adaptive guidance may also be used.
</p>
<p>Special computer techniques are required for
</p>
<p>coordinate conversion of the basic input information into coordinates
</p>
<p>more useful for later computations, or into coordinates more appropriate
</p>
<p>Page III F-10</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>for referencing missile steering commands. Computers are also re-
</p>
<p>quired to compare the actual behavior with the standard program,
</p>
<p>calculate the correct time for initiation of such actions as rocket
</p>
<p>shutoff, calculate the direction and magnitude of command corrective
</p>
<p>maneuvers, and perform stability and attitude control computation
</p>
<p>for the autopilot. The computer also performs noise-reduction signal
</p>
<p>shaping for continuous signals used within a closed-guidance loop.
</p>
<p>Analog and digital computers have been used in the past. The heart
</p>
<p>of the guidance scheme may be thought of as the set of highly
</p>
<p>specialized equations of motions that must be resolved to fit the
</p>
<p>idiosyncrasies of the particular mission. The beam-riding and pro-
</p>
<p>portional-navigation techniques developed for ground to air guided
</p>
<p>missile applications and the rendezvous and orbital correction guidance
</p>
<p>schemes may be cited as examples. For ballistic missile and ascent
</p>
<p>vehicles, three schemes are generally recognized, viz: Q, A and
</p>
<p>explicit, and of these the explicit guidance scheme has been chosen
</p>
<p>(Section III, F, 5).
</p>
<p>Page III F-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N . —
</p>
<p>Ill, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>In general, much of the computation, being of
</p>
<p>a nonlinear variety on noise contaminated signals, requires that very
</p>
<p>careful consideration be given to the order of performing filtering
</p>
<p>and computing. However, if small deviations of a vehicle from its
</p>
<p>nominal kinematic conditions can be assumed; it is often possible to
</p>
<p>avoid noise-filtering problems by using only the first order on linear
</p>
<p>terms.
</p>
<p>From a system cost effectiveness point of view,
</p>
<p>a definite trend exists at present to use major guidance subsystems
</p>
<p>for many prelaunch checkout and control systems operations. In addition,
</p>
<p>the choice of technique used to determine, the initial kinematic con-
</p>
<p>ditions of the launch vehicle affects the requirements for the guidance
</p>
<p>system of the vehicle, and vice-versa.
</p>
<p>Page III F-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Selection Criteria and Advantages of Inertial
Guidance
</p>
<p>First it should be mentioned that a variety
</p>
<p>of different guidance schemes such as radio-inertial, fully inertial,
</p>
<p>and possible stellar-monitored inertial guidance systems have been
</p>
<p>found conceptually feasible for application to the Sea Dragon. The
</p>
<p>selection then proceeded on the basis of possible extensions needed
</p>
<p>in proven techniques, and development of new components, particularly
</p>
<p>in the event that required accuracies or speeds of response would
</p>
<p>exceed present-day capability. In addition a rudimentary cost evalua-
</p>
<p>tion study, especially taking into account ground-support equipment
</p>
<p>and checkout needs for accommodating a variety of orbital missions,
</p>
<p>served as one of the criteria for recommendation of a particular
</p>
<p>injection guidance scheme for the Sea Dragon vehicle.
</p>
<p>The missions for a large sea-launch orgital
</p>
<p>space vehicle may require changes in flight profiles and trajectories
</p>
<p>as a function or launch time, particularly in the case of a rendezvous
</p>
<p>Page III F-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>requirement. Correspondingly, flexibility in the mode of operation
</p>
<p>and capability to execute a wide spectrum of flight programs is
</p>
<p>desirable. Because of the large size and cost of the vehicle and its
</p>
<p>probable application to manned missions, a high degree of reliability
</p>
<p>is required in the guidance and control systems.
</p>
<p>The guidance and control system selected is a
</p>
<p>fully inertial one with growth potential to include stellar inertial
</p>
<p>or radio command inertial capabilities. As in the Saturn vehicle, the
</p>
<p>guidance system is located in the payload and will be available to the
</p>
<p>command module after second-stage separation.
</p>
<p>The necessary degree of accuracy during the
</p>
<p>ascent flight and orbit injection can be achieved by this self-con-
</p>
<p>tained system independent of ground station support. Continuous ratio-
</p>
<p>inertial guidance, which can in general achieve a higher accuracy with
</p>
<p>given equipment tolerances, is not required but does demand a large
</p>
<p>number of ground tracking station if various launch directions are
</p>
<p>Page III F-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>considered. Additionally, and especially in the case of a sea-launch
</p>
<p>operation like the Sea Dragon, a sizeable portion of the guidance
</p>
<p>stations would have to be provided in the form of tracking and
</p>
<p>guidance ships. These ships must be very accurately located on the
</p>
<p>geographical grid and with respect to velocity, or their tracking
</p>
<p>measurements will be meaningless from the guidance standpoint. Never-
</p>
<p>theless, even in the case of all-inertial guidance, provisions in the
</p>
<p>system can be made for discrete corrections in later guidance phases,
</p>
<p>necessary to compensate for errors in the inertial sensors in the form
</p>
<p>of RF commands through a guidance processor to the initial guidance
</p>
<p>system. These commands can be based on radar, interferometer, and
</p>
<p>radio-doppler systems as processed by a ground based computer. Alter-
</p>
<p>natively, star-track monitoring on the motion of the vehicle in inertial
</p>
<p>space can be used.
</p>
<p>3. Nominal Trajectory Concerning Guidance Requirements
</p>
<p>The following table summarizes those characteristics
</p>
<p>of the nominal trajectory that have a direct bearing on the performance
</p>
<p>analysis of the guidance system. This study was based on preliminary
</p>
<p>data but is essentially unchanged by the new trajectory.
</p>

<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The vehicle is launched at sea in a vertical direc-
</p>
<p>tion, and during the initial phase of the flight, a very coarse type
</p>
<p>of guidance is in operation. The autopilot receives preprogramed
</p>
<p>command singnals based on a predetermined time and attitude program.
</p>
<p>Thus, during the vertical ascent of the vehicle, after clearing the
</p>
<p>water, the vehicle is rotated about its roll axis to ensure that the
</p>
<p>pitch axis of the vehicle becomes perpendicular to the plane of the
</p>
<p>trajectory. The maneuver is performed by means of four auxiliary
</p>
<p>control rockets, while the attitude control in yaw and pitch is per-
</p>
<p>formed -by the main thrust vector control system of the first stage.
</p>
<p>Even so, the coarse adjustment in roll is done by rotation in the
</p>
<p>water, before launch. This is in contradistinction to the conditions
</p>
<p>for a land launch, which by necessity, must launch with a fixed roll
</p>
<p>attitude with respect to the ground site installations.
</p>
<p>After the performance of the rotational trim maneuver,
</p>
<p>and upon obtaining the "kick-over velocity" as determined by the
</p>
<p>guidance system, a command signal is sent to the autopilot to initiate
</p>
<p>Page III F-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>the pitch-over program, which is a series of commanded pitch rates
</p>
<p>approximating the kick angle and subsequent gravity turn (near zero
</p>
<p>angle of attack, autopilot stabilized flight).
</p>
<p>The magnitude of the kick angle should be held to
</p>
<p>about six or eight degrees for structural reasons. This means that
</p>
<p>the initiation of the kick angle should be at low velocities, allowing
</p>
<p>less time for roll maneuvering. Subsequent trajectory analysis will
</p>
<p>be performed using the more realistic profile, but it is not expected
</p>
<p>to markedly change the vehicle performance.
</p>
<p>Guidance system output is next used by the autopilot
</p>
<p>at a certain instant during the operation of the second stage, typically
</p>
<p>about 180 sec after launch, when guidance command signals based on
</p>
<p>vehicle altitude samplings are sent (Section III, F, 5, C).
</p>
<p>Page III F-18</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>4. Definition of System Design
</p>

<p>initially defined as that of injecting payload into an essentially

</p>
<p>spectrum has been completely specified, comparable analyses for the

<p>other parameters (inclination, position of the nodes and apsides,
</p>
<p>epoch, and period) will be made.

<p>radius and horizontal velocity, it is shown in Appendix 11-12 that

<p>b. Operational Airborne Equipment
</p>
<p>A general block diagram of the inertial
</p>
<p>guidance and control system is shown in Figure III-F-2. The main
</p>
<p>physical units of the system are as follows:
</p>
<p>Page III F-21</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(1) Inertial Measurement Unit
</p>
<p>A four-gimbal gyro-stabilized platform
</p>
<p>provides the necessary freedom of motion for any kind of flight
</p>
<p>maneuvers and delivers three-axes attitude information to the guidance
</p>
<p>and control computer. Also, mounted on the platform are three pro-
</p>
<p>perly aligned accelerometers. Provisions are also made for later
</p>
<p>installation of a horizon scanner and possibly star scanners.
</p>
<p>(2) Airborne Electronics
</p>
<p>This block includes gimbal servo ampli-
</p>
<p>fiers and signal-shaping networks, integrators, power supplies,
</p>
<p>accelerometer electronics and gimbal-angle resolver signal conversion
</p>
<p>electronics, with provision for inclusion of horizon and star scanner
</p>
<p>electronics as a growth future.
</p>
<p>Page III F-22</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(3) Vehicle-Borne Digital
</p>
<p>A high-speed, high-capacity generalized
</p>
<p>digital computer needed for the wide range of guidance and control
</p>
<p>operations in the pre-launch, ascent, as well as orbital modes is
</p>
<p>represented by this block. A tentative selection of the computer
</p>
<p>now in prototype development at Librascope, designated L-9O, was made
</p>
<p>on the basis of a preliminary needs and availability evaluation. The
</p>
<p>only other computer whose announced specifications were compared
</p>
<p>directly with the L-90 unit is the AC Spark Plug product using
</p>
<p>Fairchild semiconductor microcircuits. In addition, specifications for
</p>
<p>somewhat less advanced computers by IBM and Texas Instruments were
</p>
<p>available. This tentative selection was made on the basis of announced
</p>
<p>memory capacity, presumed expansion capabilities, word time, power con-
</p>
<p>sumption, and component count. Naturally, this tentative selection
</p>
<p>will be carefully reviewed and reconsidered in the follow-on phases of
</p>
<p>the Sea Dragon design and program definition study.
</p>
<p>Page III F-23</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(4) Airborne Power Supply
</p>
<p>In unmanned ballistic vehicles, on-
</p>
<p>board requirements are largely related to the guidance and control
</p>
<p>needs so that it is usual to provide a power supply as a component part of
</p>
<p>the guidance and control system. However, in the case of a manned
</p>
<p>vehicle, the presence of life support systems aboard will dictate an
</p>
<p>additional large and still undefined electric power requirement.
</p>
<p>Section F,6,a summarizes the guidance and control power requirements.
</p>
<p>(5) Rate Gyro Assembly
</p>
<p>This assembly consists of a package of
</p>
<p>three low threshold, miniature, spring-constrained rate gyros in each
</p>
<p>stage, which provide an anticipation signal for attitude stabilization.
</p>
<p>The stable platform should not be used as the attitude control refer-
</p>
<p>ence for the first stage because flexure and bending of the missile
</p>
<p>would produce erroneous pitch and yaw axis attitude indications at the
</p>
<p>stabilized platform location.
</p>
<p>Page III F-24</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-QENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>5. Inertial Guidance Performance Analysis
</p>
<p>a. Discussion of Operation of Guidance System
</p>
<p>(1) General Description of Guidance System
</p>
<p>The fully inertial guidance system to be
</p>
<p>used for injection of the Sea Dragon payload into a nominal 300-nm
</p>
<p>circular orbit will consist of the following three major subsystems:
</p>
<p>(a) The stable platform
</p>
<p>(b) The stable-platform electronics
</p>
<p>(c) The guidance-equations computer
</p>
<p>The purpose of the stable platform is to
</p>
<p>establish within the vehicle an inertial rectangular reference system
</p>
<p>properly aligned during the prelaunch period, and maintaining its
</p>
<p>initial alignment during the powered flight through the operation of
</p>
<p>platform gyros and gimbal system properly fitted with servo-mechanisms
</p>
<p>Page III F-25</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cpnt.)
</p>
<p>Briefly, the stable platform, which is the heart of an inertial
</p>
<p>guidance system, will consist of a structural element gimbaled by
</p>
<p>means of four gimbals to the frame of the vehicle. Four gimbals
</p>
<p>(three basic plus one "redundant") are necessary to avoid any possible
</p>
<p>gimbal lock particularly during the orbital operations of the system
</p>
<p>after the injection. Two two-degree-of-freedom or three single-degree-
</p>
<p>of-freedom, high-precision floating gyros will be mounted on the plat-
</p>
<p>form that constitutes the inner gimbal of the system.
</p>
<p>The gyros will be capable of being
</p>
<p>torqued so that they could be erected through precession in the re-
</p>
<p>quired direction in space. The platform will be always slaved to the
</p>
<p>gyros through high accuracy gimbal servos operating each gimbal through
</p>
<p>properly resolved gyro pickoff signals.
</p>
<p>In addition to the gyros, three precision
</p>
<p>accelerometers are mounted on the stable platform so that each of the
</p>
<p>accelerometers has its sensitive axis oriented precisely along the
</p>
<p>corresponding axis of the platform.
</p>
<p>Page III F-26</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Additional electronic equipment and sub-
</p>
<p>systems essential to the operation of the stable platform and the in-
</p>
<p>ertial guidance system are:
</p>
<p>(a) Platform gimbal servo amplifiers
</p>
<p>and shaping networks
</p>
<p>(b) Integrators of accelerometer outputs
</p>
<p>(c) Gravity terms computer which closes
</p>
<p>the inertial guidance loop
</p>
<p>(d) Analog-to-digital converters.
</p>
<p>These elements and subsystems are located outside the platform and form
</p>
<p>what might be called the stable-platform electronics system.
</p>
<p>Further, the third major subsystem of the
</p>
<p>inertial guidance is the guidance-equations computer. This computer
</p>
<p>performs the coordinate transformation operation. The position and
</p>
<p>velocity components (time functions) in the x-y-z inertial Cartesian
</p>
<p>coordinate system, obtained from the inertial system, are translated
</p>
<p>into polar coordinates so that these polar equations can be input to
</p>
<p>the autopilot of the vehicle. The guidance-equations computer may be
</p>
<p>Page III F-27</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>combined with the inertial-guidance computer (platform electronics) in-
</p>
<p>to one integral physical unit.
</p>
<p>The general simplified block diagram of
</p>
<p>the guidance and control system is shown in Figure III-F-2.
</p>
<p>The overall operation of the guidance
</p>
<p>system of the Sea Dragon vehicle can be divided into three basic modes:
</p>
<p>(a) the prelaunch mode
</p>
<p>(b) the injection flight mode
</p>
<p>(c) the orbital and re-entry mode
</p>
<p>(2) Prelaunch Mode
</p>
<p>During the prelaunch mode, while the vehi-
</p>
<p>cle is being towed, fueled, and erected at sea, the inertial guidance
</p>
<p>system operates continuously in properly damped stable-vertical and
</p>
<p>gyrocompass mode. The gyros of the stable platform are continuously
</p>
<p>torqued by proper signals obtained from pendulous accelerometers and
</p>
<p>Page III F-28</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>from the platform-erection computer so that the three platform axes are
</p>
<p>to be aligned continuously with a high degree of accuracy with the di-
</p>
<p>rections north, east, and vertical at any location of the vehicle. Thus,
</p>
<p>a self-alignment of the stable platform is obtained and continuously
</p>
<p>maintained during the entire prelaunch period.
</p>
<p>Another variant of the self-aligning,
</p>
<p>prelaunch mode of the operation is the alignment in which one horizontal
</p>
<p>axis, x, of the platform forms a given angle Q in azimuth (with respect
</p>
<p>to north) such that this axis lies in the plane of the planned trajectory
</p>
<p>The other horizontal axis, y, is perpendicular to x, and the third
</p>
<p>platform axis, z, is vertical at the point of launch. This alignment
</p>
<p>has the advantage of leading to a considerable simplification of the
</p>
<p>coordinate transformation equations during the flight mode of operation
</p>
<p>of the guidance system.
</p>
<p>It must be remembered that the self-align-
</p>
<p>ing mode of operation may become a relatively lengthy procedure requir-
</p>
<p>ing several hours if sufficient accuracy is to be obtained. On the
</p>
<p>Page III F-29</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>other hand, the time of self-alignment can be appreciably reduced if,
</p>
<p>before initiation of the self-aligning mode, a coarse alignment of the
</p>
<p>platform through external means (such as magnetic compass, for example)
</p>
<p>is performed.
</p>
<p>During the prelaunch mode, the data per-
</p>
<p>taining to initial conditions of position and velocity of the vehicle
</p>
<p>at the instant preceding the actual launch must be introduced and mech-
</p>
<p>anized in the inertial guidance computer. This data must be obtained
</p>
<p>from outside the vehicle with the information on the direction of launch
</p>
<p>in azimuth and other pertinent data on the nominal injection trajectory
</p>
<p>of the vehicle. It is envisioned that the initial conditions data for
</p>
<p>the sea launch of the Sea Dragon will be continuously monitored from
</p>
<p>Loran shore installations, so that the exact instantaneous location
</p>
<p>of the launch site at sea, as well as the exact horizontal velocity
</p>
<p>components at launch are measured, properly transformed, and introduced
</p>
<p>into the vehicle guidance system. The horizontal vectors of the vehi-
</p>
<p>cle inertial velocity just before launch are primarily results of the
</p>
<p>rotation of the earth, and in part caused by any drifting of the vehicle
</p>
<p>Page III F-30</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>at sea currents. The water velocity in Gulf Stream may have a magnitude
</p>
<p>of 2 to 4 knots depending upon the location.
</p>
<p>The self-alignment mode of the vehicle
</p>
<p>guidance system is illustrated in Figure III-F-1, which represents a
</p>
<p>general block diagram of the prelaunch, self-alignment mode.
</p>
<p>(3) Flight Navigation Mode
</p>
<p>The flight-navigation mode of operation
</p>
<p>of the guidance system is the actual active mode during which the in-
</p>
<p>ertial guidance system generates continuous, instantaneous data on the
</p>
<p>position and velocity of the vehicle in inertial space and computes
</p>
<p>kinematic equations that are to be satisfied at injection, through the
</p>
<p>operation of autopilot, TVC actuators, and the gradual response of the
</p>
<p>vehicle in space of these control functions (Figure III-F-6).
</p>
<p>The flight-navigation mode is automat-
</p>
<p>ically initiated at the instant of vertical launch of the vehicle, and
</p>
<p>Page III F-31</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>is characterized by free (nontorqaed) operation of the previously align-
</p>
<p>ed gyros. The stable platform is slaved to the gyros through its gimbal
</p>
<p>servos and, therefore, maintains its inertial (nonrotating) orientation
</p>
<p>in space with an accuracy nearly equal to that of the gyros themselves.
</p>
<p>The three accelerometers with their sensi-
</p>
<p>tive axes oriented respectively along the x, y, z axes of the platform
</p>
<p>are sensing the total acceleration in each direction in space. These
</p>
<p>accelerations, of course, contain gravity terms that must be subtracted
</p>
<p>by means of gravity computer operating in a. closed-loop fashion on the
</p>
<p>basis position data of the vehicle obtained from the guidance system.
</p>
<p>Each accelerometer output is integrated- once to obtain velocity,* and
</p>
<p>the second time to obtain position. The data on position is used to
</p>
<p>compute the corresponding gravity term that is subtracted from the ac-
</p>
<p>celerometer output (Figure III-F-5). As a result, continuous signals
#'
</p>
<p>representing x, y, z, x, y, z are produced by the inertial guidance
</p>
<p>This first integration may be performed within the accelerometer.
Such an accelerometer is called integrating accelerometer or velocity
meter.
</p>
<p>Page III F-32</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>system, providing continuous data on the instantaneous position and
</p>
<p>velocity of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>These inertial position and velocity sig-
</p>
<p>nals are fed into the guidance-equations computer that performs all the
</p>
<p>required coordinate translations and generates basic guidance equations
</p>
<p>as well as the input signals to the autopilot. Then, through the oper-
</p>
<p>ation of the autopilot and the TVC system of the vehicle, the guidance
</p>
<p>equations must be satisfied at the instant of injection of the vehicle
</p>
<p>into orbit.
</p>
<p>A guidance and control system that oper-
</p>
<p>ates in a general manner discussed here, so that it assures the satis-
</p>
<p>faction of the guidance equations at the crucial injection (or cutoff)
</p>
<p>point of the powered trajectory, while permitting, in general, trajectory
</p>
<p>deviations from the nominal pattern at the intermediate points, is
</p>
<p>called an adaptive guidance and control system. The more rigid "zero-
</p>
<p>drift" system cannot be regarded as superior in operation, while being
</p>
<p>much more difficult to realize in practice.
</p>
<p>Page III F-33</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>A more detailed discussion of the flight-
</p>
<p>guidance problems, injection dispersion and guidance equations is given
</p>
<p>in the subsequent sections.
</p>
<p>(4) Orbital and Re-Entry Guidance
</p>
<p>Although the present phase of the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon study is not concerned with detail investigation of the orbital
</p>
<p>and re-entry guidance of the service and command module of the vehicle,
</p>
<p>it is necessary to mention several significant points relating to the
</p>
<p>required modifications of the system operation and to some auxiliary
</p>
<p>equipment necessary for proper orbital navigation and ultimate re-entry,
</p>
<p>Once the payload (with the service and
</p>
<p>command module) is injected into the orbit, the most significant pro-
</p>
<p>blem is to maintain the attitude of the orbital vehicle with a suffi-
</p>
<p>cient degree of accuracy. As far as the command module is concerned,
</p>
<p>a proper attitude maintained during its orbital mode is necessary to
</p>
<p>assure the required direction of retro-thrust for de-orbiting at the
</p>
<p>Page III F-34</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>beginning of re-entry, and for other functions such as observation,
</p>
<p>photography optical and infrared, communications, telemetering, etc.
</p>
<p>It is apparent that the payload space station itself must be attitude
</p>
<p>stabilized for the purpose of any future rendezvous with it by other
</p>
<p>orbital vehicles coming in for refueling, etc.
</p>
<p>Normally, the attitude of an orbiting
</p>
<p>vehicle will be maintained so that its yaw axis is always colinear
</p>
<p>with the local vertical; its roll axis will always lie in the orbital
</p>
<p>plane, and its pitch axis will be perpendicular to the plane of the orbit
</p>
<p>It must be recognized that an inertial
</p>
<p>guidance system of either Schuler-tuned or fully inertial type cannot
</p>
<p>be used successfully during the orbital mode, during which the outputs
</p>
<p>of accelerometers will always be zero. Therefore, independent sensing
</p>
<p>devices must be used in addition to the gyro-stabilized platform.
</p>
<p>Specifically, an optical or infrared
</p>
<p>horizon scanner must be used to establish the direction of local verti-
</p>
<p>cal at each instant of time, whereas the stable platform will be
</p>
<p>Page III F-35</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL. CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>slaved, through the appropriate torquing of its gyros, to the local
</p>
<p>vertical realized by the horizon scanner. Further, the vehicle frame
</p>
<p>will be slaved to thi platform through pitch and roll attitude-control
</p>
<p>jets.
</p>
<p>While the horizon scanner and the yaw
</p>
<p>axes of both the platform and the vehicle frame xvill thus continously
</p>
<p>track the local vertical, a definite angular velocity about the pitch
</p>
<p>axis must exist. Under these conditions, if the roll axis is exactly
</p>
<p>in the plane of the orbit, the average angular velocity of the vehicle
</p>
<p>about the roll axis should be zero. If however, there is some angular
</p>
<p>displacement of the roll axis of the vehicle about the yaw axis, a de-
</p>
<p>finite mean angular velocity in roll will be registered by a high-sensi-
</p>
<p>tivity roll rate gyro properly mounted on the vehicle frame. The out-
</p>
<p>put signal of this rate gyro will be fed into the yaw attitude control
/
</p>
<p>channel which will operate the yaw-control jets to return the roll axis
</p>
<p>to the orbital plane of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III F-36</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cpnt.)
</p>
<p>Further, radio command and communication
</p>
<p>equipment will be used in the command module to maintain continuous
</p>
<p>communications and possibly telemetering links with the ground stations
</p>
<p>and to provide the capability of initiating the de-orbiting operation
</p>
<p>for final re-entry by ground radio command.
</p>
<p>If the orbiting module is to be provided
</p>
<p>with any space maneuvering capability such as trimming and corrections
</p>
<p>of the existing orbit, transferring to different coplanar orbits,-or
</p>
<p>changing the orbital-plane inclination angle, additional guidance and
</p>
<p>control and space propulsion equipment will be required.
</p>

<p>c. Guidance Equations
</p>
<p>During the vertical and programed pitch-over
</p>

</p>

<p>F,5,a,(3). After the initiation of the first stage, the actual vehicle

</p>
<p>11-12) and compared with the nominal altitude at that instant, to gen-
</p>

</p>
<p>a change in pitch attitude. An additional criteria (Equation 23, Ap-
</p>
<p>pendix 11-12) will ensure that the flight path angle becomes small, a
</p>
<p>degree or two. When the actual altitude of the vehicle approaches the
</p>
<p>nominal altitude by several miles, the guidance will be switched to
</p>
<p>the flight path angle mode, Equation 1.
