<p>6. Airborne Guidance Design Considerations and Sub-
system Description
</p>
<p>a. Evaluation of Physical Characteristics
</p>
<p>Page III F-42</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(1) Packaging and Location
</p>
<p>A guidance compartment structure is needed
</p>
<p>to contain the airborne equipment, providing mechanical support and en-
</p>
<p>vironmental conditioning. Provision for windows to provide the star
</p>
<p>scanner a free line of sight to selected stars should be incorporated
</p>
<p>for future growth in system capability.
</p>
<p>In addition, special airborne test equip-
</p>
<p>ment such as signal conditioners, necessary for the evaluation of the
</p>
<p>guidance and control system during flight tests will be mountable on
</p>
<p>the structure. It is anticipated that the flight tests with guidance
</p>
<p>evaluation missiles, as well as sled tests, will be necessary to check
</p>
<p>out the system. This is particularly true in the case of the star-
</p>
<p>tracking component.
</p>
<p>The entire guidance package is insulated
</p>
<p>with glass fiber and placed in a specially coated monocoque enclosure,
</p>
<p>which will be designed to have the stiffness and damping coefficients
</p>
<p>Page III F-43</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>Ill, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>to aid the serious vibration problems connected with missile guidance
</p>
<p>computer design.
</p>
<p>To provide cooling for the avionics equip-
</p>
<p>ment, the sealed container is filled with helium. It also contains a
</p>
<p>circulating fan and a liquid-type heat exchanger in its base.
</p>
<p>The exact location in the command and serv-
</p>
<p>ice module is not critical, and in fact from the producibility point of
</p>
<p>view may be in the shirt-sleeve environment or vacuum outer space portion,
</p>
<p>depending on detailed capsule design and operational considerations not
</p>
<p>yet resolved. Placement of flight control system sensors is separately
</p>
<p>discussed.
</p>
<p>(2) Weights, Dimensions, and Power Requirements
</p>
<p>The Litton L-90 digital computer weighs
</p>
<p>3
35 Ib, occupies about 0.3 ft , and consumes 30 watts. The Litton L-
</p>
<p>30O9 platform \veighs 33.5 Ib, occupies 0.65 ft3 and consumes 230 watts
</p>
<p>Page III F-44</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>under high torque conditions. If an astro-inertial platform is not se-
</p>
<p>lected, radio trim commands would probably be supplemented by telesex-
</p>
<p>tant data in orbital and, especially, possible lunar modes. Possible
</p>
<p>telesextant characteristics would be 2O-lb weight, 0.65-ft3 volume, and
</p>
<p>20-watt power requirements. An astro-inertial platform may be expect-
</p>
<p>3
ed to weigh about 55 Ib, need 1.3 ft of space and 250 watts of power.
</p>
<p>The average power consumption, including associated airborne electronics
</p>
<p>is estimated at 500 watts, weight (including compartment) about 200 Ib,
</p>
<p>and compartment volume about 10 ft3, of which perhaps 3 ft3 is actually
</p>
<p>taken up by pertinent guidance equipment. Power is furnished at 26v,
</p>
<p>400 cps ̂  1% rms.
</p>
<p>However, the extreme accuracy requirements
</p>
<p>of alignment in the vertical mode when no radio or astro corrections
</p>
<p>are made during ascent leads to a relatively heavy, naval type, gyro
</p>
<p>compassing system. In this case, which is selected as the preferred
</p>
<p>method to date, the guidance and control system will weigh an extra
</p>
<p>200 to 300 Ib, with corresponding increase in space and power required.
</p>
<p>It will be recalled that an increase of this magnitude is not a signi-
</p>
<p>ficant event in the Sea Dragon weight budget.
</p>
<p>Page III F-45</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Stable Platform
</p>
<p>The unaided inertial guidance scheme selected
</p>
<p>will require development of a platform featuring high inertia gyros
</p>
<p>delivering high angular momentum. Further advances in powdered metal-
</p>
<p>lurgy, including magnetic high density materials of high tensile
</p>
<p>strength, are expected to aid this effort. If, however, the alternative
</p>
<p>guidance treatment involving stellar monitoring during the ascent phase
</p>
<p>is used, platforms presently under development for avionics applications
</p>
<p>would be suitable. In this mode of operations the errors in align-
</p>
<p>ment, which if uncompensated would be unacceptably large, are ob-
</p>
<p>tained from the star'tracking fix and appropriate steering corrections
</p>
<p>are generated in the guidance computer.
</p>
<p>A number of miniature inertial platforms were
</p>
<p>considered, and the Litton P-300G has been selected as typical and
</p>
<p>capable of fulfilling Sea Dragon requirements under these conditions.
</p>
<p>Much of the discussion which follows, based on Air Force miniature
</p>
<p>platforms, is applicable also to the heavier platform, but detailed
</p>
<p>Page III F-46</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>design analysis affecting the large units will not be available for the
</p>
<p>study. A detailed component description of the P-300G platform is
</p>
<p>given in Appendix 11-13.
</p>
<p>(1) General Description
</p>
<p>The inertial subsystem consists of an
</p>
<p>inertial reference unit and an inertial reference electronics unit.
</p>
<p>These are the major units of the subsystem.
</p>
<p>A summary of P-300G subsystem physical
</p>
<p>characteristics is presented in Table 1 of Appendix 11-13. The environ-
</p>
<p>mental capabilities of the subsystem are summarized in Table 2 of the
</p>
<p>same appendix.
</p>
<p>(2) Inertial Reference Unit
</p>
<p>The inertial reference unit consists of
</p>
<p>a stable element employing two floated, two-degree-of-freedom gas bear-
</p>
<p>ing gyros and three floated, torque-balance accelerometers supported by
</p>
<p>Page III F-47</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>a set of gimbals permitting vehicle angular freedom in azimuth, pitch,
</p>
<p>and roll. The stable element can be maintained in a prescribed orien-
</p>
<p>tation by torquing the gimbals with angular error signals derived from
</p>
<p>the gyro pickoffs. A redundant inner roll gimbal is provided to prevent
</p>
<p>gimbal lock; this allows the vehicle complete maneuverability without
</p>
<p>affecting the stable element. A summary of P-300G characteristics is
</p>
<p>found in Table 3 of Appendix 11-13.
</p>
<p>(a) Gyros
</p>
<p>In a stabilized platform employing two-degree-
</p>
<p>of-freedom gyros, the gyro instrument errors are the limiting factors
</p>
<p>in stabilization accuracy (since there are no cross-coupling errors
</p>
<p>caused by rotor precession). It is therefore of extreme importance
</p>
<p>that the design configuration be highly accurate. The development of
t
</p>
<p>self-acting, gas-lubricated bearings makes it possible to improve the
</p>
<p>performance of the precision gyro by approximately one order of magni-
</p>
<p>tude. Basically, the gas-lubricated bearing assembly consists of
</p>
<p>simple sleeve bearings and thrust pads similar to the oil-lubricated
</p>
<p>Page III F-48</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-eENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>bearing used on large machinery. The difference between gas and oil-
</p>
<p>lubricated bearings is that a gaseous lubricant requires a much smaller
</p>
<p>clearance between the bearing surfaces. About one-twentieth the clear-
</p>
<p>ance commonly used in oil-lubricated bearings is used in the gas bearing
</p>
<p>(b) Accelerometers
</p>
<p>Typically, a miniature, single axis, torque
</p>
<p>balance, pendulous accelerometer is used. The sensitive element is a
</p>
<p>floated pendulum mounted on a pair of jewel-and-pivot bearings and equip-
</p>
<p>ped with an electromagnetic pickoff and torquer. The sensitive axis of
</p>
<p>the instrument is perpendicular to both the pivot axis and to the line
</p>
<p>joining the center of mass and the center of buoyancy; hence, an ac-
</p>
<p>celeration along the sensitive axis results in a torque acting upon
</p>
<p>the floated pendulum, which tends to rotate the pendulum about the
</p>
<p>pivot axis. This rotation, however, is immediately sensed by the pick-
</p>
<p>off that generates a signal to control the accelerometer torquer current
</p>
<p>by means of an external amplifier so that the pickoff signal is contin-
</p>
<p>uously maintained at null. As a result, the current through the torquer
</p>
<p>coils becomes a precise measure of the applied acceleration. The high
</p>
<p>Page III F-49</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>pickoff sensitivity and restoring amplifier gain keep the relative ro-
</p>
<p>tation of the pendulum with respect to the case limited to a small
</p>
<p>fraction of a milliradian even in the presence of maximum acceleration.
</p>
<p>"As a result, the directional accuracy of the sensitive axis is main-
</p>
<p>tained, and cross-coupling effects are negligible. A calibrated preci-
</p>
<p>sion resistor, mounted externally to the instrument, converts the torquer
</p>
<p>current to a voltage that is a precise measure of acceleration. This
</p>
<p>voltage is applied to the input of the digital conversion circuit.
</p>
<p>(c) Gimbal System
</p>
<p>The gimbal system for the inertial reference
</p>
<p>unit employs a four-gimbal, three-axis configuration, including one
</p>
<p>redundant gimbal to eliminate the possibility of gimbal lock during
</p>
<p>vehicle maneuvers. The azimuth, pitch, and outer roll gimbals have un-
</p>
<p>limited freedom about their respective axes, but the inner roll gimbal
</p>
<p>is limited to ̂  20° of freedom. The resultant system, when servo-
</p>
<p>stabilized, allows unlimited maneuverability of the carrier vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III F-50</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Typically, the signal generators and torquer motors are direct drive,
</p>
<p>gearless units. This arrangement permits higher angular output accu-
</p>
<p>racy within minimum size and without the need for special, low-backlash
</p>
<p>gearing in the case of the signal generators. The torquer motors ap-
</p>
<p>proach the high efficiency of geared units by proper design and appli-
</p>
<p>cation of the direct drive components.
</p>
<p>(3) Electronics
</p>
<p>The inertial data subsystem electronics
</p>
<p>unit contains all the circuitry necessary for operation of the inertial
</p>
<p>reference unit and for communication with a digital computer. The in-
</p>
<p>ertial reference electronics unit contains the gimbal servo amplifiers,
</p>
<p>accelerometer restoring amplifiers, redundant azimuth gyro axis caging
</p>
<p>amplifier, the various power supplies for the subsystem, platform tem-
</p>
<p>perature control circuits, the digital converter, and various switching
</p>
<p>functions.
</p>
<p>Page III F-51</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III. F5 Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The digital converter contains the analog-
</p>
<p>to-digital conversion circuits for converting accelerometer analog out-
</p>
<p>puts to binary incremental velocity data for a digital computer, the
</p>
<p>precision gyro pulse torquing circuits that are controlled by torquing
</p>
<p>commands from a digital computer, and the emergency pendulous leveling
</p>
<p>circuits for the platform.
</p>
<p>c. On-Board Digital Computer
</p>
<p>The predesign study described here is intended
</p>
<p>to result in a computer that will have the capability to navigate and
</p>
<p>guide the Sea Dragon vehicle from launch to landing completely auto-
</p>
<p>matically (Figure III-F-7.) Also, in case of abort, it has the capa-
</p>
<p>bility of guiding the vehicle to a safe landing at any of several pre-
</p>
<p>selected sites. Other functions to be performed by the computer are to
</p>
<p>provide information for various guidance displays, to make decisions
</p>
<p>regarding fuel management or the need to abort, and to assist1 in the
</p>
<p>telemetry link. In addition, the crew must be able to manually insert
</p>
<p>data and commands into the computer.
</p>
<p>Page III F-52</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The operation of the computer is generalized by
</p>
<p>discussing the four operational phases: preflight, launch, orbital, and
</p>
<p>re-entry. On the basis of previous experience, the numbers of words of
</p>
<p>computer memory required has been estimated at 8,000. This includes
</p>
<p>4,500 for equations of motion and launch corrections, and 1,250 words
</p>
<p>for re-entry subroutines (target selection; navigation computations to
</p>
<p>determine h, v, x, ̂  path angle, q; energy management; landing commands).
</p>
<p>The memory capacity is conserved by the assumption that a judicious or-
</p>
<p>ganization of instruction words has resulted in using two instructions
</p>
<p>per word.
</p>
<p>The initial phase of the computer operation is
</p>
<p>preflight and is combined with the launch phase since the computations
</p>
<p>are the same. Such computations as gravity corrections, position and
</p>
<p>velocity are performed during these phases. This portion of the mission
</p>
<p>requires a high solution rate for accurate determination of position
</p>
<p>and velocity. The incremental portion of the computer is ideally suited
</p>
<p>for this purpose.
</p>
<p>At cutoff of the rocket booster, the computer
</p>
<p>Page III F-53</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>automatically switches to the programs associated with orbital mode guid-
</p>
<p>ance .
</p>
<p>The computer selected for Sea Dragon is designed
</p>
<p>around a delay line memory. In Appendix 11-13 the computer requirements
</p>
<p>are discussed and followed by a description of a computer with a delay
</p>
<p>line memory that will fulfill these requirements. The many advantages
</p>
<p>of delay lines are discussed in detail.
</p>
<p>Alternatively, for comparative purposes, a
</p>
<p>computer design using a wired fixed program-core memory with a 512
</p>
<p>word erasable core memory was considered. An advantage is that only
</p>
<p>one core is needed for each instruction word.
</p>
<p>7. Checkout and Ground Service Equipment (GSE) Summary
</p>
<p>The GSE consists of operational and maintenance units
</p>
<p>The operational unit includes flight checkout equipment to prepare and
</p>
<p>maintain the airborne equipment ready for launch and a guidance control
</p>
<p>Page III F-54</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>panel required on the ship during the earth-fixed modes of system oper-
</p>
<p>ation. While the in-flight guidance computer performs many system
</p>
<p>checks, the ship-board equipment monitors the computer for malfunctions.
</p>
<p>Maintenance ground equipment includes:
</p>
<p>a. Handling equipment
</p>
<p>b. Interface and isolation console
</p>
<p>c. Component test equipment
</p>
<p>d. Computer load-test console
</p>
<p>Detailed descriptions and methodology of the support
</p>
<p>equipment required by the guidance (and other) systems is included in
</p>
<p>the section on support operations of this volume. In addition, the re-
</p>
<p>sults of the preliminary study mode specifically for guidance system
</p>
<p>ground support and checkout is presented as Appendix 11-14. Section B
</p>
<p>of this appendix, it should be noted, presents a method and justification
</p>
<p>of finding the initial position data needed for orbital injection com-
</p>
<p>putations.
</p>
<p>The requisite telemetry and associated antenna systems
</p>
<p>are discussed in Section III of Appendix 11-14.
</p>
<p>Page III F-55</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F. Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>8. Comment on Reliability and Redundancy
</p>
<p>a. General Discussion
</p>
<p>The development of the Sea Dragon design will be
</p>
<p>based on the results of a comprehensive reliability design analysis.
</p>
<p>The reliability design analysis approach proposed for the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>program will be reflected primarily by analytical techniques that will
</p>
<p>ensure that reliability becomes inherent in the design. Concurrent with
</p>
<p>design efforts, analysis will be done. In logical sequence these steps
</p>
<p>are: functional analysis, analysis of environments, malfunction mode
</p>
<p>review, system analysis, alternative analysis, reliability prediction
</p>
<p>and establishment, and allocation of system goals.
</p>
<p>b. Electronics Reliability Factors
</p>
<p>The design analysis procedure selected for the
</p>
<p>Sea Dragon guidance computer development will in general follow the
</p>
<p>worst case analysis concept using the tolerance analysis briefly out-
</p>
<p>lined in the following: (1) specify in complete detail and with
</p>
<p>Page III F-56</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>tolerances all the basic functions that the circuit is required to per-
</p>
<p>form; (2) determine all conditions (primarily tolerances, damaging
</p>
<p>stresses, noise, and handling) that tend to prohibit the circuit from
</p>
<p>performing each function as required; (3) prove that the circuit win
</p>
<p>perform each function as required, even though all ±ailure-cansing tend-
</p>
<p>encies occur simultaneously at their maximum values (unless some are
</p>
<p>mutually prohibitive; in this case each set of failure-causing tend-
</p>
<p>encies is considered separately).
</p>
<p>It can be proved that the necessary and suffi-
</p>
<p>cient condition for a component never to fail is that the tolerance
</p>
<p>analysis criteria above be met, assuming zero intrinsic parts failure.
</p>
<p>The engineer performing the analysis has the
</p>
<p>responsibility for determining all the required functions of the com-
</p>
<p>ponent (states, input-output, errors, tolerances, internal functions,
</p>
<p>etc.) and all failure-causing tendencies. The effectiveness of a
</p>
<p>tolerance analysis is dependent on the accuracy, completeness, and
</p>
<p>Page III F-57</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>reasonableness with which these items are specified. If the procedure
</p>
<p>given here is not completely adequate for all circuits, the analyzing
</p>
<p>engineer should add a procedure that proves the tolerance analysis cri-
</p>
<p>teria are met in full.
</p>
<p>Maximum consideration will be given to the use
</p>
<p>of semiconductor microcircuits for logic and gating functions. The
</p>
<p>potentially high reliability of these devices made possible partly by
</p>
<p>the decrease in the number of hand made connections among parts, and
</p>
<p>partly because of the inherent potential advantages of semiconductor
</p>
<p>technology make this approach highly desirable. In some documented
</p>
<p>cases the parts count in guidance computer applications has been reduced
</p>
<p>by as much as 80% through the use of semiconductor microcircuits. Thus,
</p>
<p>reliability should be improved by the attempt to minimize the number
</p>
<p>of components and by the introduction of microcircuits.
</p>
<p>Prelaunch testing of guidance and flight con-
</p>
<p>trol systems have generally required extensive ground-based equipment.
</p>
<p>This equipment, usually digital in operation, also had an important
</p>
<p>but brief role that ends when the missile is launched.
</p>
<p>Page III F-58</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Recent advances have designed the guidance com-
</p>
<p>puter so it could serve a dual function. The computer can be used also
</p>
<p>to test the complete guidance system before launch. This technique,
</p>
<p>which is used in the Sea Dragon design, enables the missile computer to
</p>
<p>be continuously exercised independently of the ground installation with
</p>
<p>an obvious increase in reliability.
</p>
<p>c. Inertial System Reliability Factors
</p>
<p>N.ot too many years ago in guidance planning,
</p>
<p>inertial guidance ran a poor third behind radio and radio inertia!
</p>
<p>systems as a system most likely to succeed. Today, however, this- system
</p>
<p>is the most generally accepted. The break-through of inertial guidance
</p>
<p>was made possible primarily as a result of inertial component perform-
</p>
<p>ance and reliability improvement.
</p>
<p>d. Redundancy Concepts
</p>
<p>Redundancy might be defined as the existence
</p>
<p>of more than one means of accomplishing a given task, where all means
</p>
<p>Page III F-59</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>must fail before there is an overall failure of the task. As applied
</p>
<p>to reliability, redundancy concerns the practice of paralleling two or
</p>
<p>more elements, such as parts, circuits, components, modules, equipments,
</p>
<p>or even complete systems, such that, ideally, they will independently
</p>
<p>perform the same function. Failure of any one or more of the elements
</p>
<p>should neither cause failure of the desired function nor affect the
</p>
<p>probability of successful operation of any of the remaining paralleled
</p>
<p>elements. It is obvious that the use of redundancy in design should
</p>
<p>improve the probability of accomplishing a given task beyond that which
</p>
<p>would be experienced with a single element, the failure of which would
</p>
<p>cause an immediate loss of the desired function. However, the use of
</p>
<p>redundancy does introduce certain practical problems, such as require-
</p>
<p>ments for additional weight, space, and power and the need for a deci-
</p>
<p>sion making process as a means of assigning the failure to the appro-
</p>
<p>priate redundant unit. This switching system must perform with an
</p>
<p>overall improvement in system reliability.
</p>
<p>Redundancy may be further classified as func-
</p>
<p>tional and standby, which are defined as follows:
</p>
<p>Page III F-60</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, F, Guidance System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(1) Functional redundancy exists when two or
</p>
<p>more means are operating simultaneously to accomplish a task, and less
</p>
<p>than the total number is capable of performing the function in the event
</p>
<p>of failure of less than all of the primary and alternate means.
</p>
<p>(2) Standby redundancy provides one or more
</p>
<p>alternative means of accomplishing a task. The task is switched or
</p>
<p>transferred into operation by a malfunction sensing device when the
</p>
<p>primary means fail.
</p>
<p>Page III F-61 (End III,F)</p>
<p></p>
</div>

<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>G. RECOVERY SYSTEM
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>A recovery system for the Sea Dragon Stage I vehicle
</p>
<p>has been selected on the basis of operational simplicity and reli-
</p>
<p>ability. In choosing the recovery method, advantage was taken of the
</p>
<p>increased strength of the pressurized booster in overcoming adverse
</p>
<p>scale effects that tend to make very large vehicles less adaptable to
</p>
<p>conventional recovery methods. Consideration of this size scale effect
</p>
<p>showed that for vehicles that are areodynamically decelerated to a
</p>
<p>given landing velocity, that the ratio of required "drag diameter"
</p>
<p>(size of accessory drag device) to vehicle diameter increases as vehicle
</p>
<p>size increases. Deceleration of the Sea Dragon vehicle to normal let-
</p>
<p>down velocities of 20 to 25 ft/sec would require a drag device, for
</p>
<p>example, a parachute 3,000 ft. in diameter. To overcome this problem
</p>
<p>use was made of the inherent structural strength of the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>(a result of using a pressure-fed propellant feed system).
</p>
<p>Page III G-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-8ENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>j. i j. , G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Since the booster tanks are in a pressurized
</p>
<p>condition (with ullage pressurant) they can react landing loads most
</p>
<p>efficiently in an axial direction. Therefore, final water impact of the
</p>
<p>vehicle, regardless of the method used to reach terminal velocity, was
</p>
<p>restricted to a nose-down, vertical position. Since final deceleration
</p>
<p>of the vehicle will be caused by water impact forces, analyses of the
</p>
<p>hydrodynamic pressures and decelerations were carried out so that
</p>
<p>limiting impact velocities could be established. Pressure distributions
</p>
<p>over the conical nose of the vehicle were quantitatively predicted and
</p>
<p>compared with experimental results (Appendix 11-22). Deceleration-time
</p>
<p>histories were developed. For an impact velocity of 600 ft/sec a
</p>
<p>maximum deceleration of 90g is encountered, and for 300 ft/sec the maximum
</p>
<p>is 22g (see Figure III-G-1). Assuming a cone half-angle ( ;;. ) = 30°
</p>
<p>analysis of the structural response to impact loads at various veloci-
</p>
<p>ties showed that for velocities less than 600 ft/sec no dynamic load
</p>
<p>amplification will occur. Using these facts an allowable impact
</p>
<p>velocity-tank pressure criterion was established, relating water entry
</p>
<p>velocity to tank pressure available for axial load reaction, i.e. for
</p>
<p>a LOX tank pressure of 400 psi an impact velocity of 600 ft/sec can be
</p>
<p>Page III G-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-6ENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>accepted. For 100 psi (which is the burnout pressure actually achieved
</p>
<p>with Sea Dragon) the allowable impact velocity is slightly less than
</p>
<p>300 ft/sec (Figure III-G-2).
</p>
<p>The basic recovery sequence consists of two main opera-
</p>
<p>tions: an atmospheric entry phase and a terminal or impact phase. At
</p>
<p>Stage I burnout the propellant valves in the booster are closed and the
</p>
<p>pressurant gases are trapped in the tank (somewhat in excess of 100 psi
</p>
<p>in the LO2 tank and approximately 290 psi in the RP-1 tank). After
</p>
<p>staging occurs the first stage coasts upward along its ballistic tra-
</p>
<p>jectory and then re-enters the atmosphere. During atmospheric flight
</p>
<p>drag and stablization forces are produced such that vertical impact at
</p>
<p>the proper terminal velocity is achieved.
</p>
<p>Three different aerodynamic deceleration methods were
</p>
<p>considered: parachutes, large first stage nozzle skirt, and inflatable
</p>
<p>aerodynamic decelerator. Parachutes were excluded because of potential
</p>
<p>deployment and attachment problems. The large nozzle skirt concept,
</p>
<p>which made use of a very large area ratio first-stage engine, had the
</p>
<p>Page III G-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>highest estimated reliability because of its passive operation as a
</p>
<p>decelerator. However, because of relatively high drag losses during
</p>
<p>ascent flight, possible nozzle vibration caused by the high degree of
</p>
<p>over-expansion and separation at low altitude, and adverse reaction to
</p>
<p>sea operation towing loads, this concept was discarded for the present
</p>
<p>time.
</p>
<p>The inflatable flare decelerator was selected for the
</p>
<p>Sea Dragon because it provides a high degree of reliability, ease of
</p>
<p>operation and minimizes the drag penalty during ascent. This device is
</p>
<p>attached, in deflated package form, to the first-stage thrust chamber.
</p>
<p>The device is in the form of a large conical flare 3OO ft in diameter
</p>
<p>with a flare half angle of 55° (Figure III-G-6).
</p>
<p>The flare is made up of a large, 30-ft diameter torus
</p>
<p>rigidized with smaller inflatable tubes 10 ft in diameter and covered
</p>
<p>with a surface generating outer skin. The torus and supporting tubes are
</p>
<p>constructed of rubberized nylon-dacron reinforced fabric and are pro-
</p>
<p>tected from any thermal environment by the outer skin. The outer skin
</p>
<p>Page III G-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>is an ablating rubberized asbestos fabric and is expendable (i.e., it is
</p>
<p>replaced for each flight). The outer skin is kept in tension and reacts
</p>
<p>the air loads (design aerodynamic external pressure was 5 psia) by the
</p>
<p>reaction of the tubes on the torus structure.
</p>
<p>The flare structure is inflated to a maximum of 30 psia
</p>
<p>with pressurant gas from the first-stage fuel tank (the pressurant gas is
</p>
<p>CH4). Thus, the need for separate gas supply to inflate the flare is
</p>
<p>eliminated. The volume of the torus and tubes is such that the fuel tank
</p>
<p>still has sufficient pressure to react the impact loads.
</p>
<p>Maximum temperature on the flare skin during re-entry
</p>
<p>is approximately 1,000°F; however, the asbestos cloth with its ablative
</p>
<p>covering can resist this temperature pulse without failure.
</p>
<p>The flare device represents a weight penalty of 3.8%
</p>
<p>of recovered weight. This weight increase corresponds to a payload
</p>
<p>penalty of approximately 1.7% . Total payload penalty due to recovery
</p>
<p>is 2.5%.
</p>
<p>Page III G-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The following sections provide discussion of Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>stage recovery in more detail.
</p>
<p>2. Introduction
</p>
<p>Since booster recovery and reuse can play an impor-
</p>
<p>tant role in achieving the low payload delivery costs required for the
</p>
<p>establishment of truly large scale space programs, emphasis has been
</p>
<p>placed on developing a recovery concept for Sea Dragon that is simple
</p>
<p>and reliable in operation and represents a low weight penalty for the
</p>
<p>vehicle.
</p>
<p>The size, weight, and, in part, the complexity of a
</p>
<p>vehicle recovery system are functions of the allowable touchdown or
</p>
<p>impact velocity. Conventional boosters require very "soft" landings
</p>
<p>because of their basic structural design. The Sea Dragon, by virtue of
</p>
<p>its pressurized tanks, has considerable more structural strength than
</p>
<p>other proposed vehicles. As a result, it can, in general, withstand
</p>
<p>higher impact velocities and associated load environments. Thus, the
</p>
<p>Page III G-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>complexity and weight of the Sea Dragon recovery system should be much
</p>
<p>lower relative to other systems.
</p>
<p>The ability of the Sea Dragon Structure to accept higher
</p>
<p>impact velocities is a fortunate one since the scale effect for very
</p>
<p>large boosters, in general, places much higher requirements on a given
</p>
<p>recovery system. This size-scale effect can be shown quite simply: for
</p>
<p>recovery methods, which depend on aerodynamic deceleration to final
</p>
<p>velocity, the ratio of accessory drag device diameter to the vehicle
</p>
<p>diameter .can be expressed as follows:
</p>
<p>Da _ / 8W Cdv
</p>
<p>Dv \/ Cda P Vi2 Dv
2 C da
</p>
<p>where p is the atmospheric density, v± is the impact velocity, Cd is
</p>
<p>the drag coefficient, W is the recoverable inert weight, and subscripts
</p>
<p>a and v refer to the accessory device and' the booster vehicle, respec-
</p>
<p>tively. For geometrically similar bodies with comparable structural
</p>
<p>efficiencies, we can now write the same diameter ratio as:
</p>
<p>D - Kv
</p>
<p>Page III G-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>Thus, the drag diameter to body diameter ratio, for a fixed impact
</p>
<p>velocity, goes up as vehicle size goes up. If the Sea Dragon used,
</p>
<p>conventional low pressure tanks whose inherent lower structural strength
</p>
<p>required an impact velocity of 20 to 30 ft/sec, the necessary diameter
</p>
<p>(e.g., a parachute) of the mechanism would be approximately 3,200 feet.
</p>
<p>Since the Sea Dragon operation is based on the sea
</p>
<p>launch concept, final vehicle recovery and retrieval will take place at
</p>
<p>sea. Thus, in general, final deceleration of the expended booster will
</p>
<p>be caused by water impact forces. Becuase the Sea Dragon booster
</p>
<p>vehicle will take advantage of its increased strength in accepting higher
</p>
<p>impact velocities, it is important to know quantitatively, the water
</p>
<p>impact pressures and decelerations.
</p>
<p>An analysis of the water entry load environment and
</p>
<p>vehicle structural response (assuming vertical nose first entry) was
</p>
<p>conducted. The following sections show a summary of this analysis.
</p>
<p>Page III G-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>

</p>

<p>b. Determination of Allowable Impact Velocities
</p>
<p>(1) Propellant Tank Stresses
</p>
<p>Using the deceleration-time data from
</p>
<p>Figure III-G-1, a criteria for determining allowable impact velocity
</p>
<p>was established: the structural response of the empty booster was
</p>
<p>analyzed, and it was found that the first mode longitudinal natural
</p>
<p>frequency was 30 cps and the first mode.bending frequency was 13 cps.
</p>
<p>Comparing these frequencies with the impact frequency of the water entry
</p>
<p>loading (Figure III-G-5) we see that no dynamic load amplification is
</p>
<p>probable for velocities less than 500 ft/sec (Section V,B,9 gives a
</p>
<p>more detailed analyses of the dynamic response of the booster structure
</p>
<p>Page III G-1O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>during water entry). Therefore, using a dynamic load amplification
</p>
<p>factor of one, we can establish a general criterion relating impact
</p>
<p>velocity with internal tank pressure required to react the associated
</p>
<p>water entry loads. This criterion is based on the assumption that
</p>
<p>the cylindrical tank wall cannot accept any compressive stresses. Thus,
</p>
<p>we equate the compressive stresses, caused by the maximum deceleration
</p>
<p>inertia loads to the longitudinal tension stresses due to internal
</p>
<p>pressure. Figure III-G-2 shows the results of this calculation. Using
</p>
<p>this graph we can select the proper impact velocity for the available
</p>
<p>internal pressure. For the pressurization system used in this design,
</p>
<p>the LO2 tank pressure at burnout is approximately 100 psia. From
</p>
<p>Figure III-G-2 it is seen that an impact velocity of 300 ft/sec must
</p>
<p>be obtained. Based on this velocity and the associated pressures and
</p>
<p>acceleration, the remaining vehicle structure (nose cone and components)
</p>
<p>were examined.
</p>

<p>(2) Conical Nose
</p>
<p>(a) Stresses
</p>
<p>Using Figure III-G-3 and reducing
</p>
<p>the pressure for an impact velocity of 300 fps, the dynamic stresses in
</p>
<p>the conical nose were analyzed. Preliminary results show that the stress
</p>
<p>levels in the tank wall during impact are below the yield value, except
</p>
<p>in an area near the cone apex.* Stresses at this point may exceed the
</p>
<p>failure point and a reinforced apex may be required. However, the
</p>
<p>extent of the area over which this condition exists is small (approx-
</p>
<p>imately 10 feet) and the weight penalty is negligible.
</p>
<p>(b) Stability
</p>
<p>Experimental results from the
</p>
<p>Aerojet-General water entry test facility show that material failure is
</p>
<p>* It was also shown that membrane stresses, rather than
bending stresses, are predominant.

</p>
<p>not the design criteria for nose cones such as used on Sea Dragon but
</p>
<p>rather that geometric or buckling instability is the mode of failure
</p>
<p>(as might be expected). While complete mathematical analysis of the
</p>
<p>buckling problem has not been completed, it has been shown by a simpli-
</p>
<p>fied analysis that some stiffening will be required to stabilize the
</p>
<p>cone under the transient pressure loading. However, the weight penalty
</p>
<p>due to installing circumferential T-type stiffeners is small; 8% of
</p>
<p>total cone shell weight and approximately 2.2% of the first-stage
</p>
<p>inert weight. For stability purposes, it was found that approximately
</p>
<p>ten stiffener rings were required. Spaced equally between a point
</p>
<p>approximately 65 ft from the apex and the cone base. The stffeners
</p>
<p>were of thickness t, equal to the shell skin thickness, and depth of
</p>
<p>10 t and flange width of 5 t. •
</p>
<p>(c) Vehicle.Components
</p>
<p>A major factor in the structural
</p>
<p>integrity of vehicle subassemblies is the amplification of the input
</p>
<p>acceleration pulse by vehicle resonances. For subassemblies whose
</p>
<p>Page III G-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>mass is small relative to the vehicle, the input is more easily described
</p>
<p>as an acceleration pulse at the base of their tie-down structure. This
</p>
<p>acceleration is a function of the amplification in the longitudinal
</p>
<p>mode and the location of the tie-down structure in the mode shape.
</p>
<p>Components and subassemblies that are highly sensitive to these loads
</p>
<p>will be located as close to the nodal point of this mode as possible.
</p>
<p>Preliminary study of the subassembly attachments under a 22g decelera-
</p>
<p>tion pulse showed that stiffening and beefup structure would be
</p>
<p>required, which would impose a weight penalty.of 2.0% of booster inert
</p>
<p>weight. Consideration of the thrust chamber dynamic interaction with
</p>
<p>the gimbal joint and tankage indicated that structural stiffening of
</p>
<p>the gimbal joint would be required. The weight penalty due to the
</p>
<p>required increased structural strength is approximately 0.5% of booster
</p>
<p>inert weight.
</p>
<p>4. Selection of Aerodynamic Deceleration Device
</p>
<p>After preliminary investigation, three basic recovery
</p>
<p>systems were selected for study: inflatable aerodynamic decelerator;
</p>
<p>Page III G-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>large nozzle flare; and parachute. All of the systems studied, with
•
</p>
<p>the exception of the large nozzle, were required to yield impact
</p>
<p>velocities of 300 ft/sec, the allowable velocity based on available
</p>
<p>tank pressure for structural stability (Figure III-G-2). In each case
</p>
<p>the same basic recovery operation was followed: the first-stage
</p>
<p>vehicle after burnout continues along its ballistic trajectory, re-enters
</p>
<p>the atmosphere and impacts on the ocean surface at terminal velocity.
</p>
<p>The basic objective in selecting a particular recovery system is to
</p>
<p>provide means for attaining the proper aerodynamic stabilization and
</p>
<p>drag for the given impact conditions. The aerodynamic decelerator was
</p>
<p>chosen as the most promising system based on overall reliability, weight,
</p>
<p>and performance. A brief discussion of each concept is presented be-
</p>
<p>low.
</p>
<p>a. Parachutes
</p>
<p>For recovery of the Sea Dragon first stage
</p>
<p>booster with a single parachute, a diameter of 270 feet would be required
</p>
<p>to attain the required impact velocity. Preliminary estimates indi-
</p>
<p>cate that a complete recovery system weight, including a 45 foot drogue
</p>
<p>Page III X3-15</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>parachute and tank reinforcement would be approximately 3.0% of the
</p>
<p>total recovered weight. Supersonic deployment, at Mach number 1.8 to
</p>
<p>2.0 of the drogue chute was assumed. Use of several smaller chutes
</p>
<p>was considered, but the total weight of a clustered system was approxi-
</p>
<p>mately the same as a single chute system. It was also concluded that
</p>
<p>the deployment reliability of a clustered system was lower than for a
</p>
<p>single chute.
</p>
<p>Even though the parachute system appeared very
</p>
<p>favorable from a weighr standpoint, some important disadvantages were
</p>
<p>found in the area of initial atmospheric entry and deployment: the
</p>
<p>Sea Dragon booster, without accessory devices, has poor aerodynamic
</p>
<p>stability and at best is neutrally stable. Thus with a tumbling re-
</p>
<p>entry, as must be the case because of staging tipoff torques, the
</p>
<p>problem of parachute deployment is a serious one. The prediction of
</p>
<p>tumbling rates during entry is very difficult. Whether or not stable
</p>
<p>equilibrium at some angle of attack is reached before impact also
</p>
<p>poses an involved analysis program. Thus, attitude and rate sensors
</p>
<p>must be used in the booster so that parachute ejection and deployment
</p>
<p>Page III G-16</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>i
</p>
<p>can be sequenced properly. If low drag fin type stabilization devices
</p>
<p>are used to provide sufficient stability for a stable flight path,
</p>
<p>the rapid descent of the booster allows insufficient time for parachute
</p>
<p>deployment, i.e., for a stabilized (with fin) descent, a Mach number
</p>
<p>of 1.8 is reached 105 sec after initial entry. At 113 sec impact occurs
</p>
<p>Thus, there is a very short time interval in which parachute deploy-
</p>
<p>ment, even at supersonic Mach numbers, can occur. Deployment at
/
</p>
<p>higher speeds presents severe problems in parachute stability and
</p>
<p>deployment (NASA TN D752). Therefore, based on overall ease of opera-
</p>
<p>tion and reliability, the parachute, for purposes of this effort, was
</p>
<p>not considered further.
</p>
<p>b. Large Nozzle Flare
</p>
<p>In seeking a recovery system with the highest
</p>
<p>reliability possible, a system with almost complete passive operation
</p>
<p>was investigated. The configuration studied was the basic Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>first-stage with a very large area ratio nozzle. The function of the
</p>
<p>large nozzle was to provide re-entry stabilization and drag forces that
</p>
<p>Page III G-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>would give the proper velocity and attitude at impact. For this partic-
</p>
<p>ular system, the exit diameter required for 300 ft/sec was impracti-
</p>
<p>cally large from a structural and thrust coefficient standpoint so a
</p>
<p>modification in pressurization system was assumed and a tank pressure
</p>
<p>of 400 psi was assumed to be available. Thus, from Figure III-G-2
</p>
<p>we see that a velocity of 600 ft/sec can be accepted. For this case,
</p>
<p>a nozzle diameter of 175 ft is required (for a 20° half angle conical
</p>
<p>nozzle). The payload penalty associated with the additional drag
</p>
<p>losses during ascent, increased nozzle weight, additional beefup of
</p>
<p>components, Is loss caused by over-expansion, and increased outage gas
</p>
<p>weight was approximately 18%. It was decided, for this study, that
</p>
<p>the payload penalty and the additional fabrication and other associated
</p>
<p>problems over-shadowed the high recovery reliability available with
</p>
<p>this system. However, a final selection of any recovery system will
</p>
<p>rest upon the results of a detailed cost effectiveness study for each
</p>
<p>recovery method.
</p>
<p>Page III G-18</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>

<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>c. Inflatable Aerodynamic Decelerator
</p>
<p>Based on (a) and (b) above, a recovery system
</p>
<p>was designed which attempts to incorporate a high degree of reliability
</p>
<p>with reasonable weights and deployment simplicity. As a result, an
</p>
<p>inflatable aerodynamic decelerator was chosen to provide the necessary
</p>
<p>drag and stability during atmospheric entry and still enable the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon to have a reasonably high performance capability. The detail
</p>
<p>design of this device is described in Appendix 11-15. Basically,_ it
</p>
<p>is an inflatable conical flare attached to the thrust chamber of the
</p>
<p>booster (see Figure III-G-6). The base diameter of the flare is 300 ft
</p>
<p>and provides an impact velocity of 3OO ft/sec. The flare is made up
</p>
<p>of a large, 30-ft diameter torus made rigid with smaller inflatable
</p>
<p>tubes 10 ft in diameter and covered with a surface generating outer
</p>
<p>skin. The torus and supporting tubes are constructed of rubberized
</p>
<p>nylon-dacron reinforced fabric and are protected from any adverse
</p>
<p>thermal environment by the outer skin. The outer skin is an ablating
</p>
<p>rubberized asbestos fabric that is replaced for each flight. The
</p>
<p>outer skin is kept in tension and reacts the air loads (design aero-
</p>
<p>dynamic external pressure was 5 psia, by the reaction of the tubes on
</p>
<p>the torus structure.
</p>
<p>Page III G-19</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>The flare structure is inflated to a maximum
</p>
<p>of 30 psia with pressurant gas from the first stage fuel tank (the
</p>
<p>pressurant gas is CH4). Thus, the need for separate gas supply to
</p>
<p>inflate the flare is eliminated. The volume of the torus and tubes
</p>
<p>is such that the fuel tank still has sufficient pressure to react the
</p>
<p>impact loads.
</p>
<p>This concept was chosen as the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>booster recovery system. It provides low terminal velocity and high
</p>
<p>aerodynamic stability yet presents no drag penalty during ascent. The
</p>
<p>weight of the complete flare system constitutes approximately 3.8%
</p>
<p>of the total recovered weight. The basic vehicle can withstand the
</p>
<p>impact loads without additional stiffening*, however, some stiffening
</p>
<p>will be required in component attachments and gimbal joint which
</p>
<p>should be charged to the recovery system. This weight amounts to
</p>
<p>approximately 2.5% of inert weight. Therefore, the percentage of total
</p>
<p>recovery system weight to recovered weight is approximately 8.5̂ .
</p>
<p>With the exception of the nose stiffeners which
constitute 2.2% of inert weight.
</p>
<p>Page III G-20</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>This is a lower figure than those reported for other types of recovery
</p>
<p>systems (15% of inert weight and higher) and derives from the fact that
</p>
<p>the requirement on impact velocity is drastically reduced here due to
</p>
<p>the structural design of the Sea Dragon. Thus, this recovery system
</p>
<p>will have an associated payload penalty (for a 300-min orbital mission)
</p>
<p>of 2.5%. The advantage in using a vehicle whose strength allows higher
</p>
<p>impact velocities is readily apparent. In addition, the use of a
</p>
<p>pressure fed fuel system eliminates the need of carrying separate flare
</p>
<p>inflation gas since, in the case of the Sea Dragon, the tank pressurant
</p>
<p>gas required for the propulsion phase is available for flare inflation.
</p>
<p>(1) Operational Sequence
</p>
<p>(a) Flare Deployment
</p>
<p>Upon attainment of first stage
</p>
<p>burnout conditions (velocity 5,800 ft/sec, altitude 125,000 ft), the
</p>
<p>staging sequence occurs and the expended booster coasts upward along
</p>
<p>Page III G-21</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>its ballistic trajectory. Immediately after staging the forward
</p>
<p>extended skirt is detached and the outer fabric cover which contains
</p>
<p>the deflated flare structure is ejected. When the dynamic pressure
</p>
<p>has reached a value of 10 lb/ft2 (17 sec after staging) inflation of
</p>
<p>the flare structure begins. Methane gas from the fuel tank is passed
</p>
<p>through a pressure regulating valve and into the flare. The structure
</p>
<p>is fully inflated, to a pressure of 1 psi, in approximately 100 sec.
</p>
<p>At this time, the vehicle has reached its apogee (335,000 ft). Pres-
</p>
<p>surization of the flare continues and at approximately 200 sec from
</p>
<p>inflation initiation, the flare is pressurized to its design value of
</p>
<p>20 psia. At this time, the altitude is 182,000 ft, the trajectory
</p>
<p>flight path angle is 33° below the local horizontal, the velocity is
</p>
<p>5,450 ft/sec and the dynamic pressure is 15 lb/ft2.
</p>
<p>(b) Atmospheric Entry
</p>
<p>Atmospheric entry of the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>booster stage "effectively" occurs at an altitude of 200,000 ft. At
</p>
<p>this point, the velocity, is 5,350 ft/sec and the flight path angle is
</p>
<p>Page III G-22</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, g, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>30° below the local horizontal. Tumbling of the vehicle, due to
</p>
<p>initial tipoff forces at staging will begin to affect the vehicle trajec-
</p>
<p>tory at this point; however, because of the high aerodynamic stability
</p>
<p>and damping of this configuration, the tumbling motion will subside
</p>
<p>rapidly and the vehicle will attain a stable flight path at, or around
</p>
<p>a zero angle of attack. As mentioned above, the flare structure is
</p>
<p>not fully pressurized to its design pressure of 20 psia until the
</p>
<p>vehicle reaches an altitude of 182,000 ft. The flare, however, is
</p>
<p>fully inflated at all times during entry and the internal pressure at
</p>
<p>all times far exceeds the external flow field static pressures.
</p>
<p>Durin.g the subsequent descent tra-
</p>
<p>jectory, the following maximum conditions are experienced by the
</p>
<p>vehicle. The maximum stagnation radiation equilibrium temperature
</p>
<p>(on the 7-ft diameter blunt nose cap) is 1,390°F. Temperatures on the
</p>
<p>flare structure, assuming no ablative materials, are not expected to
</p>
<p>exceed 900°F. If an ablative outer skin is used, temperatures within
</p>
<p>the flare on the torus and tubes will be in the order of 250°F maximum.
</p>
<p>The maximum dynamic pressure is 300 psf and the maximum deceleration
</p>
<p>is 6.5g's.
</p>
<p>Page III G-23</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>(c) Impact Conditions
</p>
<p>An impact velocity of 300/sec and
</p>
<p>a flight path angle of 89° from the horizontal (1° off vertical) are
</p>
<p>the end conditions for the atmospheric trajectory described above.
</p>
<p>Oscillatory motions of the booster are damped out*thus, for a still air
</p>
<p>condition, the water entry angle of attack is zero prior to impact.
</p>
<p>The effect of surface winds in the splash area could conceivably give
</p>
<p>rise to an angle of attack in the order of six degrees for a 30 ft/sec
</p>
<p>surface wind, however, due to the slow response of booster to such
</p>
<p>drift effects, the angle of attack will never be greater than 3.0
</p>
<p>degrees .
</p>
<p>(d) Maximum Underwater Penetration
</p>
<p>Since the effective density of the
</p>
<p>empty booster is quite low, the penetration of the vehicle for an
</p>
<p>initial velocity of 300 ft/sec will be relatively small, approximately
</p>
<p>45% of total first stage length'.
</p>
<p>Page III G-24</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>density is defined here as total weight divided by the cube of the
</p>
<p>tank radius times the density of water. Thus, the flare structure will
</p>
<p>not be subjected to hydrodynamic loads.
</p>
<p>The action of buoyant forces will
</p>
<p>eject the vehicle upward after maximum penetration. The cone apex
</p>
<p>will rise approximately 3O% of one body length above the surface.
</p>
<p>Reimpaction will occur at a
</p>
<p>velocity of 35 ft/sec. Structural loading due to reimpaction will be
</p>
<p>very low. Angular rotation of the booster during this "pop up" period
</p>
<p>will cause lateral as. well as axial loading; however, the combined
</p>
<p>effects are not expected to be nearly as severe as those at first
</p>
<p>impact.
</p>
<p>5. Second-Stage Recovery
</p>
<p>Recovery and reuse of the second stage vehicle has
</p>
<p>been studied. The general method that was used to effect recovery of
</p>
<p>Page III G-25</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>the first stage could be applied to upper stage recovery as well. In
</p>
<p>this case, a conical nose would be incorporated in the second stage.
</p>
<p>Since the second stage auxiliary engines provide
</p>
<p>orbital injection thrust for the payload, the second stage will go
</p>
<p>into orbit with the payload. During ascent into orbit and after the
</p>
<p>second stage main engine has ceased firing, the expandable nozzle skirt
</p>
<p>will be separated and ejected from the vehicle. After attaining
</p>
<p>orbital condition, the payload and expended second stage will be
</p>
<p>separated. When the second stage has reached the proper position,
</p>
<p>small retro rockets in the nose will be fired to eject the vehicle
</p>
<p>out of orbit. A velocity impulse of 480 ft/sec will be required to
</p>
<p>give an initial re-entry angle of 2.0° from the local horizontal.
</p>
<p>Aerodynamic stabilization and deceleration of the
</p>
<p>vehicle will be attained with an inflatable flare identical to the
</p>
<p>one used for first stage recovery. However, the size of the flare
</p>
<p>will be smaller than the device used on the first stage since, even
</p>
<p>Page III G-26</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-eENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, G, Recovery System (cont.)
</p>
<p>though the estimated available tank pressure is lower, 50 psi, the
</p>
<p>recovered weight is also lower, 1.2 x 106 Ib. with 50 psi tank
</p>
<p>pressure available for axial load reactions at impact a terminal velo-
</p>
<p>city of 210 ft/sec can be allowed. The flare diameter required is
</p>
<p>240 ft.
</p>
<p>Aerodynamic heating for second stage recovery will
</p>
<p>of course be more severe than for first stage. Use of material with
</p>
<p>higher thermal resistance, such as Rene 41 mesh and ablative coatings,
</p>
<p>will be necessary.
</p>

</p>

<div class="page"><p></p>

</p>
<p>AEROJET-QENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, Vehicle Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>H. SECONDARY SUBSYSTEMS
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>This section concerns power supplies and th,- ordnance
</p>
<p>systems because their functions extend throughout the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Batteries have been tentatively selected for use as
</p>
<p>the electrical power source for this vehicle; there is no need for a
</p>
<p>central hydraulic or pneumatic power supply. The first-stage engine
</p>
<p>gimbal actuator utilizes RP-1 fluid power and no separate hydraulic
</p>
<p>supply is required. The use of hydrogen-oxygen fuel cells or other
</p>
<p>electical power systems may be indicated after the total vehicle power
</p>
<p>requirements are determined. No attempt was made to develop this data
</p>
<p>for this study. The ordnance system will be integrated for optimum
</p>
<p>safety, check-out, and reliability. Positive mechanical safe-arm
</p>
<p>mechanisms will be provided for each ordnance item..
</p>
<p>Page III H-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, H, Secondary Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>It has been assumed that many of the minor subsystems
</p>
<p>and some of the major ones will have hardware and operational techniques
</p>
<p>developed and available from current space programs by the time the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon program starts, and that these will be adapted for this program.
</p>
<p>Therefore, some of the concepts portrayed in this section and through-
</p>
<p>out the report will later be influenced by future developments in the
</p>
<p>state of the art and will be improved accordingly. However, the
</p>
<p>currently available techniques can be adapted to make adequate and
</p>
<p>feasible subsystems without development of new techniques.
</p>
<p>2. Power Supplies
</p>
<p>The main power supplies for the vehicle during
</p>
<p>flight will be located in the compartment between the forward end of
</p>
<p>the second stage and the aft end of the payload. Power converters
</p>
<p>will be used to convert the direct current battery power into alterna-
</p>
<p>ting current as required for the various subsystems of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Separate power supplies will be provided in the command module and
</p>
<p>its service module for their operation after separation from the
</p>
<p>vehicles. Also a separate power supply will be provided on the first
</p>
<p>stage for recovery operations.
</p>
<p>Page III H-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, H, Secondary Subsystem (cont.)
</p>
<p>During the towing of each individual stage, electrical
</p>
<p>power will be supplied externally from the towing ship. After
</p>
<p>assembly into the complete vehicle, power will continue to be supplied
</p>
<p>from an external source until the service vessel leaves the erected
</p>
<p>vehicle late in the countdown.
</p>
<p>Before final prelaunch checkout, the vehicle power
</p>
<p>supply batteries will be activated and the external power supply discon-
</p>
<p>nected. This final checkout will be performed using vehicle flight
</p>
<p>power; the external power for vehicle functions will not be reconnected
</p>
<p>except during an extended delay in flight operations.
</p>
<p>The first-stage power supply for recovery purposes
</p>
<p>will be a battery pack that will be checked out prior to launch, but
</p>
<p>will not be used until after separation of the first stage. This power
</p>
<p>supply will provide power for all functions of the onboard recovery
</p>
<p>system.
</p>
<p>Page III H-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-3ENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, H, Secondary Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>The main functions that will be controlled by the
</p>
<p>sequencing equipment are jettisoning of the forward interstage struc-
</p>
<p>ture by means of explosive charges; inflation of the recovery flare
</p>
<p>bag; telemetry throughout the recovery phase including impact; and
</p>
<p>signalling of the location of the first stage after impact by active
</p>
<p>location aids.
</p>
<p>The onboard equipment for the first stage will be
</p>
<p>rugged enough to withstand the landing impact. The battery pack will
</p>
<p>be made of dry cells potted in plastic; the sequencing equipment will
</p>
<p>be especially designed for the purpose and will be shock mounted to
</p>
<p>attenuate the shock.
</p>
<p>All major external electrical wiring will be con-
</p>
<p>tained in conduits that are pressurized with dry nitrogen gas, and a
</p>
<p>replenishment system will maintain the pressure. The replenishment
</p>
<p>rate will be monitored to assure there are no leaks in the system.
</p>
<p>Page III H-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, H, Secondary Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>3. Ordnance System
</p>
<p>Several vehicle and propulsion systems functions that
</p>
<p>will be accomplished by means of various types of ordnance items are:
</p>
<p>a. Command module abort propulsion system
</p>
<p>b. Posigrade and retrograde propulsion systems
</p>
<p>in the service module for the command module.
</p>
<p>c. Command module abort separation system
</p>
<p>d. Command module re-entry separation system
</p>
<p>e. Interstage (Stage I/II) separation system
</p>
<p>f. Propulsion system valve operating devices
</p>
<p>g. Ballast staging devices
</p>
<p>h. First-stage recovery operation devices
</p>
<p>There will be an integrated ordnance system that
</p>
<p>includes provisions for checkout of all items and for operations as
</p>
<p>commanded by the guidance computer and sequencing equipment.
</p>
<p>Page III H-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, H, Secondary Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>Positive mechanical safe and arm provisions will be
</p>
<p>made for all ordnance items to prevent inadvertent firing. These
</p>
<p>safe and arm mechanisms will be operated remotely by electrical signals
</p>
<p>and will be monitored as to position. These mechanisms will be revers-
</p>
<p>ible to provide for operational checkout and for safety after checkout.
</p>
<p>The first-stage recovery operation devices will
</p>
<p>operate after separation from the vehicle, and therefore will operate
</p>
<p>separately with its own sequencing system and power supply. However,
</p>
<p>the checkout procedures and equipment will be integrated with those
</p>
<p>of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III H-6 (End III,H)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, Vehicles Subsystems (cont.)
</p>
<p>I. BALLAST UNIT
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>The ballast unit consists of a structural assembly
</p>
<p>of six cylindrical tanks and support struts. The forward structural
</p>
<p>element is an orifice that provides a control of thrust chamber environ-
</p>
<p>ment for underwater operation. The ballast unit as attached to the
</p>
<p>first-stage thrust chamber assembly and with struts to the aft skirt
</p>
<p>provide aft-end flotation for sea handling of the vehicle. The
</p>
<p>ballast tankage when filled with a heavy fluid (specific gravity =2)
</p>
<p>provide balancing mass that erects and stabilizes the vehicle in the
</p>
<p>vertical launch attitude. Provisions exist for staging the ballast
</p>
<p>during the ignition sequence as shown in Figure II A-2.
</p>
<p>2. Description and Function
</p>
<p>The ballast unit as shown in the Stage I drawing
</p>
<p>consists of a structure that interfaces with the aft end of Stage I
</p>
<p>thrust chamber assembly at Station 536. A structural separation
</p>

<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>point is provided. The forward support structure for the ballast
</p>
<p>tanks includes reaction points for the struts. The ballast support
</p>
<p>struts are four A-frame links that span from reaction points on the
</p>
<p>ballast structure to fittings on the aft skirt of the vehicle at
</p>
<p>Station 432. The function of the ballast and strut assembly is illus-
</p>
<p>trated in Figure II-A-2.
</p>
<p>a. Forward Structure
</p>
<p>The forward ring structure of the ballast
</p>
<p>interfaces with the internal surfaces of the thrust chamber assembly
</p>
<p>at Station 516 where it is sealed and aft to Station 536. The ring is
</p>
<p>ported at its center at a diameter of approximately 46 ft., which
</p>
<p>provides a port area ratio relative to the engine throat of 1.2. This
</p>
<p>port will be sized to produce pressures in the skirt area of the
</p>
<p>thrust chamber that are higher than the ambient pressure. The ring is
</p>
<p>ported at six places for duct access to each of six ballast tanks.
</p>
<p>The ducts admit chamber gases to the ballast tank ullage during ballast
</p>
<p>Page III 1-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>fluid expulsion. The forward port on the ballast tanks (aft end of
</p>
<p>the duct) will be sealed with a diaphragm that ruptures when exposed
</p>
<p>to pressures in excess of design values or when failure is initiated
</p>
<p>by explosive means.
</p>
<p>b. Ballast Tanks
</p>
<p>Six ballast tanks are attached at their forward end
</p>
<p>to the forward ring structure. The tanks are sized to contain a
</p>
<p>fluid of specific gravity of 2 and produce, when fully loaded, an
</p>
<p>underwater weight of 10 x 106 Ib. The 28 ft diameter cylindrical
</p>
<p>tanks are 24 ft long between tangents of the hemipherical end closures.
</p>
<p>The ballast tanks are designed to withstand an internal pressure of ISO
</p>
<p>psig and will be pressurized to avoid crushing pressure differentials.
</p>
<p>The aft closure on each ballast tank is ported and sealed with a dia-
</p>
<p>phragm that will rupture when design pressures are exceeded, or when
</p>
<p>failure is initiated by explosive means. The aft port will be sized
</p>
<p>to control heavy fluid expulsion at rates desired for the underwater
</p>
<p>trajectory. Each tank provides the mounting for structure that distri-
</p>
<p>butes concentrated loads from the ballast support struts. Attachment
</p>
<p>Page III 1-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>fitting are provided for plumbing lines as required to fill and vent
</p>
<p>the ballast tankage. Flotation devices will be installed that enable
</p>
<p>returning the ballast unit to a buoyant condition following vehicle
</p>
<p>launch. These devices can be flotation bags inflated with air from
</p>
<p>ground equipment.
</p>
<p>c. Ballast Support Struts
</p>
<p>The ballast support struts are A frames con-
</p>
<p>structed of tubular elements interconnected by webs. The base of aft
</p>
<p>end of the A frames attach to pins on the ballast assembly; the for-
</p>
<p>ward ends attach to fi'ttings on the aft skirt of the vehicle. The
</p>
<p>struts transmit all axial and shear forces developed during all hand-
</p>
<p>ling modes to the aft skirt. The A frames are provided with hydro-
</p>
<p>dynamic plates inclined to the longitudinal axis of the vehicle, which
</p>
<p>rotate the struts outboard during the underwater trajectory after
</p>
<p>separation of the struts at their forward end from the fittings on
</p>
<p>the aft skirt of the vehicle.
</p>
<p>Page III 1-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>3. Evaluation and Recommendations
</p>
<p>The functions provided by the ballast unit are
</p>
<p>necessary for the handling and launching of the Sea Dragon Configura-
</p>
<p>tion 135. Additional functions that are felt to be desirable if not
</p>
<p>necessary, have not been attainable at this point in the design develop-
</p>
<p>ment of Sea Dragon. Ballasting, in flotation and in weighing are
</p>
<p>enabled by the unit; hard points for towing and protection for acces-
</p>
<p>sory flotation devices are provided. It is estimated that 50% of the
</p>
<p>aft skirt weight is necessary to provide the structural reactions for
</p>
<p>the ballast unit. This-is equivalent to a payload penalty of 30OO Ib.
</p>
<p>Although the present design allows several seconds
</p>
<p>between main engine ignition and commitment to flight, it would be
</p>
<p>desirable to add a thrust spoiling capability. During the ignition
</p>
<p>transient, it is considered desirable to maintain the vehicle in a
</p>
<p>stable condition at zero velocity until engine performance quality is
</p>
<p>established. Thrust spoiling would enable shutdown with the vehicle
</p>
<p>in a manageable condition if ignition transient criteria are not met.
</p>
<p>Page III 1-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>a. ' Alternative Approaches
</p>
<p>The design of the ballast unit is on the basis
</p>
<p>of requirements derived from the vehicle design and its environment;
</p>
<p>flexibility appears to exist only in its operational sequence. Alter-
</p>
<p>nate ballast designs exist primarily for different vehicle designs.
</p>
<p>b. Struts and Strut Staging
</p>
<p>The ballast unit design for Configuration 135
</p>
<p>includes struts that serve the purpose of avoiding a weight penalty
</p>
<p>on the vehicle for the structure required, to react the gimbal loads
</p>
<p>associated with handling. The flight gimbal moments are estimated to
</p>
<p>be 62 x 10° ft Ib. The gimbal moments resulting from handling of the
</p>
<p>vehicle with the ballast attached to the trailing edge of the thrust
i
</p>
<p>chamber assembly only (no struts) will be 100O x 10^ ft. Ibs. This
</p>
<p>moment would have to be reacted with the vehicle structure and actua-
</p>
<p>tion components at a significant weight penalty. The requirement for
</p>
<p>struts exists only where the thrust chamber is articulated. The
</p>
<p>Page III 1-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>current operational concept for the ballast staging is illustrated in
</p>
<p>Figure II-2. The struts are retained in structural interface with the
</p>
<p>ballast and vehicle to the point in the ignition sequence where an
</p>
<p>orderly shutdown could be accomplished. The attached struts prevent
</p>
<p>structural damage to the chamber on such a shutdown.
</p>
<p>•An alternate possibility involves staging the
</p>
<p>struts prior to the initiation of the first-stage main engine ignition
</p>
<p>sequence. Staging of the struts early simplifies the underwater tra-
</p>
<p>jectory, but affords limited potential for orderly shutdown after the
</p>
<p>main engine ignition sequence has started.
</p>
<p>c. Ballast Design and Thrust Chamber Loads
</p>
<p>The current ballast concept includes an
</p>
<p>orifice plate located near the trailing edge of Stage I thrust chamber
</p>
<p>assembly. The orifice plate will be designed to maintain positive
</p>
<p>pressures in the thrust chamber during the underwater trajectory. The
</p>
<p>ambient pressure at start is 135 psig and pressure control must be
</p>
<p>maintained to the sea surface where the pressure is 0 psig. The thrust
</p>
<p>Page III 1-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>chamber skirt must be designed for the highest pressure differential,
</p>
<p>or for ISO psig. The weight increment for the structure required is
</p>
<p>approximately 100,000 pounds which is equal to a 14,000 pound payload
</p>
<p>penalty. While it is expected that an orifice plate could be designed
</p>
<p>to control pressures in the Stage I thrust chamber during its under-
</p>
<p>water trajectory, the starting transients may introduce flow instability,
</p>
<p>Alternate means for reacting the loads on the expansion section of the
</p>
<p>thrust chamber have been considered.
</p>
<p>Where the ballast is designed not to provide
</p>
<p>control of the thrust chamber environment, the thrust chamber must be
</p>
<p>able to withstand the loading imposed by the separated exhaust gas
</p>
<p>flow. The estimated loads on the thrust chamber during start and for
</p>
<p>the underwater trajectory consist of a crushing pressure differential,
</p>
<p>over a limited length of the chamber, of approximately 50 psi. This
</p>
<p>crushing pressure level exists over short lengths of the thrust
</p>
<p>chamber and moves from the throat region to the trailing edge as part
</p>
<p>of the base pressure on the vehicle. Flow separation resulting from
</p>
<p>the high ambient presssure produces the negative pressure. The weight
</p>
<p>Page III 1-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>of structure to react to these loads is estimated as 190,000 Ib. This
</p>
<p>is equivalent to payload penalty of approximately 23,000 Ib.
</p>
<p>Structural support for the gimbaled DeLaval
</p>
<p>thrust chamber can be provided by a ballast element that provide a duct
</p>
<p>within the thrust chamber that extends from the trailing edge to the
</p>
<p>throat. The duct is sized to avoid overexpansion of the engine
</p>
<p>exhaust flow, and supports the thrust chamber skirt for external loads.
</p>
<p>The plug is sealed at its forward end. The plug type of ballast,
</p>
<p>because it counteracts all loads associated with the underwater tra-
</p>
<p>jectory, provides the least incremental structural weight penalty.
</p>
<p>The primary disadvantage of the approach is that staging of the ballast
</p>
<p>requires a relative motion of the annular body (plug ballast) of
</p>
<p>nearly 100 ft; the analytical prediction of the dynamics of this
</p>
<p>system is not too precise.
</p>
<p>d. Ballast Considerations and Alternate Thrust
Chamber Design Type
</p>
<p>Ballast requirements for thrust chamber design
</p>
<p>types applicable to Sea Dragon were considered qualitatively. Of
</p>
<p>Page III 1-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>primary interest are the requirements for a single fixed DeLaval thrust
</p>
<p>chamber, where thrust vector control is provided by secondary injection
</p>
<p>and for a plug nozzle with a truncated spike.
</p>
<p>A fixed DeLaval chamber simplifies the ballast
</p>
<p>design in regards to the support struts. Support struts are not
</p>
<p>required to counteract handling loads developed by the ballast unit.
</p>
<p>It is felt that the fixed nozzle can be reinforced, with a minor weight
</p>
<p>increment, to counteract ballast loads; there are no actuator elements
</p>
<p>in the ballast reaction structural path. Structural considerations in
</p>
<p>regard to the expansion section are similar to that for the gimbal
</p>
<p>engine.
</p>
<p>The plug thrust chamber assembly with trun-
</p>
<p>cated spike provides attractive features for design of the ballast
</p>
<p>and enables a thrust spoiling capability. Because the plug engine will
</p>
<p>compensate for the back pressure, no modification of the exhaust flow
</p>
<p>or structural support of chamber surfaces is required. The ballast
</p>
<p>unit can be interfaced structurally with the truncated spike on a face
</p>
<p>Page III I-1O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-SENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>III, I, Ballast Unit (cont.)
</p>
<p>engagement. Ballast staging will not require a telescoping of parts
</p>
<p>between the vehicle and the ballast. The ballast could provide a
</p>
<p>thrust spoiling function by redirecting flow outboard at the trailing
</p>
<p>edge of the truncated spike.
</p>
<p>Page III 1-11 (End III)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>IV. VEHICLE STRUCTURAL CHARACTERISTICS
</p>
<p>Airframe stiffness and mode shapes of the vehicle were computed
</p>
<p>for many different vehicle conditions, such as towing, fueling, launch,
</p>
<p>flight, and impact. Results of the weight and stiffness distribution
</p>
<p>calculations are given in Figures IV-1 -2, and -3.
</p>
<p>From these results, modal analyses were made using a modified
</p>
<p>Mylkestad technique, mechanized for digital computation. The mode
</p>
<p>shapes and frequencies for two vehicle conditions were thus determined;
</p>
<p>these are shown in Figures IV-4 and IV-5. Although used primarily for
</p>
<p>launch and towing dynamics analyses, these modal data were also used
</p>
<p>in considering the autopilot-control-body loops and instrument loca-
</p>
<p>tions.
</p>
