<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V. VEHICLE ANALYSIS
</p>
<p>A. SYSTEM PERFORMANCE
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>The Sea Dragon recoverable vehicle configuration No.
</p>
<p>135 has a capability to deliver 1.10 million Ib of payload to a circular
</p>
<p>earth orbit of 300 nm altitude when sea launched from Cape Canaveral in
</p>
<p>an Eastward direction. A preliminary optimization study established
</p>
<p>the following design parameters: first-stage nozzle area ratio of
</p>
<p>5.0:1; thrust to weight ratio of first stage of 2.0:1; and staging ratio
</p>
<p>(mass ratio second stage/mass ratio first stage) of 1.9:1.
</p>
<p>To accomplish sea launch, the first-stage engine
</p>
<p>operates under water for approximately 5 sec until total emergence of
</p>
<p>the vehicle from the sea. During this period the variations in thrust,
</p>
<p>drag, buoyancy, and ballast weight can combine to produce an inflection
</p>
<p>in the acceleration time history. The large ambient hydrostatic pres-
</p>
<p>sure and its subsequent decline is accommodated by a self-compensating
</p>
<p>Page V A-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>plug ballast unit. An analysis of these effects is presented in Section
</p>
<p>V,A,3. When the vehicle has reached 100 ft/sec it undergoes a pitch-
</p>
<p>down maneuver (kick) of approximately 7° rotation. Then it merges into
</p>
<p>a gravity turn and continues to orbit. The first stage burns out at an
</p>
<p>altitude of approximately 125,000 ft and a velocity of 5,800 ft/sec.
</p>
<p>The first stage is immediately separated and the second stage ignited,
</p>
<p>with minimum coasting. At the end of the second stage burning, 260 sec
</p>
<p>later, the altitude is 150 nm and the velocity 23,430 ft/sec. The aux-
</p>
<p>iliary engines continue burning for a total .time of about 22.4 min.
</p>
<p>During this low thrust period, the altitude increases to 300 nm and the
</p>
<p>trajectory is shaped to reduce eccentricity. The entire trajectory is
</p>
<p>illustrated in Figure II-3.
</p>
<p>Many other trajectories were studied with variations
</p>
<p>in propellant utilization rate, kick angle, and payload. For comparison
</p>
<p>some cases involving coast and restart were also examined. The best
</p>
<p>of these indicates that a payload gain (by restart) of 86,OOO Ib is
</p>
<p>possible; however, additional weight required to realize restart capa-
</p>
<p>bility was not evaluated and would reduce the advantage. Section V,A,
</p>
<p>3 and Appendix 11-16 present these studies in detail.
</p>
<p>Page V A-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>Analyses of staging dynamics show that the relative
</p>
<p>lateral motion between the first and second stages is sufficiently-small
</p>
<p>to preclude damaging contact or severe perturbations tc the flight, j.-.ith.
</p>
<p>Although the stages are aerodynamically unstable at the time of staging,
</p>
<p>the dynamic pressure and angle of attack are low enough to avoid appre-
</p>
<p>ciable rotation being developed before the longitudinal separation has
</p>
<p>proceeded past its critical point. This is made possible by the use
</p>
<p>of pressure-driven separation which results in a total staging time of
</p>
<p>3 sec.
</p>
<p>The first stage unit recoverable and reusable. The
</p>
<p>recovery operation starts after staging with the inflation of a drag
</p>
<p>device of flared shape that stabilizes the unit and reduces its veloc-
</p>
<p>ity for sea impact.
</p>
<p>A parametric study of re-entry and impact conditions
</p>
<p>showed that an impact velocity of 300 ft/sec was required by structural
</p>
<p>considerations of the first-stage tankage at a terminal pressure of
</p>
<p>100 psi. Analyses of various configurations for drag effectiveness
</p>
<p>Page V A-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>showed that a conical flare of 300-ft dia was required to achieve the
</p>
<p>impact velocity. An inflatable flare was chosen after a weight/payload
</p>
<p>tradeoff study of this, parachutes, and fixed structures. These studies
</p>
<p>are discussed in Section III, G.
</p>
<p>2. Propulsion Parameter Optimization
</p>
<p>A preliminary performance optimization was carried out
</p>
<p>for the Sea Dragon launch vehicle Configuration No. 132. These studies
</p>
<p>were made more current as more precise performance data became available.
</p>
<p>Three vehicle design parameters were studied: first-stage area ratio,
</p>
<p>liftoff thrust to weight ratio, and staging ratio. Interactions between
</p>
<p>the various parameters were neglected and the mass fractions were as-
</p>
<p>sumed constant for each stage (Figure V-A-1, -2, and -3). As a result
</p>
<p>of this study, the following values were selected: first-stage area
</p>
<p>ratio: 5.0:1, thrust-to-weight ratio: 2.0:1, and staging ratio (mass ratio
</p>
<p>second stage/mass ratio first stage): 1.92:1. The nominal payload ob-
</p>
<p>tainable with these vehicle parameters and the Configuration No. 135
</p>
<p>Page V A-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>.propellant mass fractions is 1.1O x 1O Ib (based on eastward luanch
</p>
<p>from Cape Canaveral). The assumptions and procedures used to arrive at
</p>
<p>these vehicle parameters are discussed in the following sections.
</p>
<p>a. Introduction
</p>
<p>The primary purpose of the Sea Dragon perform-
</p>
<p>ance optimization analysis was to provide a set of reference parameters
</p>
<p>for establishment of vehicle design and evaluation of the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>launch vehicle concept.
</p>
<p>When attempting to design a vehicle system that
</p>
<p>is optimum from an economic standpoint, it is obvious that the perform-
</p>
<p>ance parameters, such as the thrust-to-weight ratio, staging ratio, etc.,
</p>
<p>are materially affected by the economic constraints imposed. Thus, the
</p>
<p>best performing system may or may not be the most economic on terms of
</p>
<p>total program costs.
</p>
<p>As a basis for vehicle design and economic study,
</p>
<p>this section illustrates the results of a preliminary performance opti-
</p>
<p>mization analysis.
</p>
<p>Page V A-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. General Procedure and Assumptions
</p>
<p>In proceeding with the optimization procedure,
</p>
<p>the results of an earlier Aerojet-General study were utilized in selec-
</p>
<p>ting the nominal operating first stage chamber pressure of 300 psi.
</p>
<p>(The actual 'chanber pressure history is shown in Figure. III-A-4, Section
</p>
<p>III,1 A.) The 2.3:1 mixture ratio for first-stage propellants was se-
</p>
<p>lected on the basis of maximum ideal exhaust velocity. Second-stage
</p>
<p>mixture ratio was chosen on the basis of other vehicle studies which
</p>
<p>have indicated that a mixture ratio of 5:1 is near optimum when con-
</p>
<p>sidering the specific impulse and vehicle mass fraction trade-off.
</p>
<p>These assumptions have not been verified for the vehicle size discussed
</p>
<p>here. On the basis of these assumptions, three design variables were
</p>
<p>selected for optimization: first-stage thrust-to-weight ratio, staging
</p>
<p>ratio (mass ratio upper stage/mass ratio booster), and first stage
</p>
<p>nozzle area ratio.
</p>
<p>Page V A-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJ E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>c. Computational Procedure
</p>
<p>The boost trajectories considered were analyzed
</p>
<p>on an IBM 7090 computer. A two-dimensional, nonrotating earth was as-
</p>
<p>sumed, (the effect of an eastward launch from Cape Canaveral would be to
</p>
<p>add approximately 18% to the payload delivered). The trajectory was a
</p>
<p>typical idealized boost flight; vertical ascent until a velocity of 100
</p>
<p>ft/sec was reached and then an instantaneous kick angle applied that,
</p>
<p>with the subsequent gravity turn, gives the second-stage burnout con-
</p>
<p>ditions required to establish a 300-nm circular orbit. Staging was in-
</p>
<p>stantaneous and the second stage was permitted to coast to the proper
</p>
<p>orbital altitude and angle after burnout. The circularization of the
</p>
<p>orbit occurred at this point with the assumption made that the second
</p>
<p>stage had a restart capability to supply the small velocity increment
</p>
<p>required to complete injection. The use of the auxiliary engines to
</p>
<p>inject the vehicle into orbit does not change the optimum values of the
</p>
<p>various parameters to any extent.
</p>
<p>Page V A-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>Because this preliminary optimization was for
</p>
<p>the purpose of establishing a basic set of design parameters, any inter-
</p>
<p>action between area ratio, staging ratio, and thrust-to-weight ratio was
</p>
<p>ignored (i.e., each parameter was investigated independently of the other
</p>
<p>three, using past experience as to the value of the fixed quantities in
</p>
<p>each case). Also neglected is the variation in mass fraction with the
</p>
<p>various parameters. The values of mass fraction used initially were
</p>
<p>from the basic Sea Dragon design Configuration No. 132. First-stage
</p>
<p>mass fraction was 0.89 and second stage O.91. As more accurate design
</p>
<p>information became available the optimizations were rerun when it 'was
</p>
<p>felt a significant change would occur. The first-stage area ratio study
</p>
<p>was not rerun because of the relative insensitivity of area ratio op-
</p>
<p>timization to small changes in mass fraction. The staging and thrust/
</p>
<p>weight ratio studies were rerun with Configuration No. 135 data, al-
</p>
<p>though the information was not used to change the design, so that the
</p>
<p>degree of inefficiency could be determined.
</p>
<p>Engine performance parameters C and C* were
r
</p>
<p>modified with efficiency factors of 0.97 and O.96 respectively. Theo-
</p>
<p>retical vacuum thrust coefficients for the first and second stages

The burnout dynamic pressure drops from 115 Ib/ft to 8O Ib/ft however

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>effect of lower mass fraction is negligible.
</p>
<p>3. Trajectory Analyses
</p>
<p>In following sections are presented brief discussions
</p>
<p>of the studies made to date on the Sea Dragon trajectory. Each of the
</p>
<p>Page V A-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N .—
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>principle phases of the vehicles flight (i.e. launch, atmospheric flight,
</p>
<p>staging, injection, re-entry, and impact) were examined. In some cases
</p>
<p>preliminary optimization of events or design features affecting the
</p>
<p>phase was performed; in others, only a nominal definition of the phase
</p>
<p>was completed. Particular attention was given to those phases that are
</p>
<p>unique to the basic concept, namely, the underwater launch and water im-
</p>
<p>pact after re-entry. For the most part, the trajectory analyses dis-
</p>
<p>cussed in sections immediately following, dealt with forces influencing
</p>
<p>the longitudinal acceleration of the vehicle primarily, rather than
</p>
<p>with forces causing lateral dispersion. The latter are presented sepa-
</p>
<p>rately in Section V.
</p>
<p>a. Underwater Performance
</p>
<p>The underwater performance can be divided into
</p>
<p>two sections: engine operation and vehicle trajectory. These topics
</p>
<p>will be discussed separately.
</p>
<p>Page V A-12</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>(1) Engine Operation
</p>
<p>The engine operation underwater is domi-
</p>
<p>nated by the presence of the annular ring at the nozzle exit. The pur-
</p>
<p>pose of this ring is to maintain a gas pressure in the nozzle skirt suffi-
</p>
<p>cient to keep the nozzle from collapsing because of external hydrostatic
</p>
<p>and hydrodynamic pressures.
</p>
<p>The general operation of the nozzle-ring
</p>
<p>combination can be described as follows. The gas passes through the
</p>
<p>nozzle throat and enters the skirt under supersonic condition. Because
</p>
<p>of the high back pressure in the skirt, the gas jet separates from the
</p>
<p>nozzle wall with an accompanying oblique shock. The oblique shock turns
</p>
<p>the flow towards the nozzle axis. This separated supersonic jet then
</p>
<p>exits through the hole in the ring.
</p>
<p>The-nozzle-ring flow system is self-regu-
</p>
<p>lating. Should the hole in the ring be too small to pass all the flow,
</p>
<p>the pressure in the skirt area will rise. This will result in flow
</p>
<p>separation occurring at a smaller area ratio, reducing the diameter of
</p>
<p>Page V A-13</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>the separated jet. This self-adjusting process will continue until the
</p>
<p>separated .jet is small enough to pass through the ring. Obviously the
</p>
<p>hole in the ring must be larger than the nozzle throat.
</p>
<p>Similarly, if the separated jet is too
</p>
<p>small to fill the hole, the skirt area will vent around the edges of the
</p>
<p>jet,, reducing the pressure in the skirt and causing the jet to separate
</p>
<p>at a larger area ratio. The separated jet will thus increase in diameter
</p>
<p>until it fills the hole. This self-regulating feature of the system
</p>
<p>may give rise to oscillatory flow.
</p>
<p>The real difficulty in determining the
</p>
<p>steady-state operation of this sytem is in predicting the point of
</p>
<p>separation. The ordinary empirical separation correlations developed
</p>
<p>for nozzle flow are not valid at the pressure and area ratios encountered.
</p>
<p>The criteria used in this analysis is one developed by Magar (J. Aero
</p>
<p>Sc., February, 1956) for turbulent boundary layers. The pressure ratios
</p>
<p>and Mach numbers to which Magar's criteria correlates data are consider-
</p>
<p>ably lower than those covered by most other criteria. To cover very
</p>
<p>Page V A-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AER O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>low values of Mach number Magar's criteria was combined with normal
</p>
<p>shock theory, since the shock must approach a normal shock as the up-
</p>
<p>stream Mach number approaches 1.0.
</p>
<p>The basic method of calculation used for
</p>
<p>this report is as follows. A gas pressure is assumed for the separated
</p>
<p>region in the skirt and the point of separation is determined. With
</p>
<p>the pressure ratio across the shock and the initial Mach number known
</p>
<p>the downstream conditions can be calculated using oblique shock theory.
</p>
<p>The flow downstream of the shock is assumed to be axial, so that the
</p>
<p>jet cross section at the exit is essentially the same as that at the
</p>
<p>separation plane. No attempt was made to investigate the shock pattern
</p>
<p>initiated by the separation shock, as most of it will occur outside the
</p>
<p>nozzle and will not affect the vehicle performance.
</p>
<p>It is recognized that a much more rigor-
</p>
<p>ous approach to the flow problem is possible, obtaining the complete
</p>
<p>flow field with the method of characteristics. However, it is also
</p>
<p>felt this would be rather futile as the uncertainties involved in the
</p>
<p>Page V A-15</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>prediction of separation are much greater than those resulting from use
</p>
<p>of the simpler theory to predict the remainder of the flow.
</p>
<p>Variations in chamber pressure do not
</p>
<p>change the flow pattern within the nozzle. Thus the chamber pressure
</p>
<p>decay and recovery during the first 7 sec of flight do not affect the
</p>
<p>performance of the nozzle and ring provided the pressure in the sepa-
</p>
<p>rated region remains greater than the a'mbient. Chamber pressure vari-
</p>
<p>ations do affect the thrust.
</p>
<p>Ejection of the water from the chamber at
</p>
<p>ignition was not considered in this study. However, conservative calcu-
</p>
<p>lations indicated that the LO2-TEA lead might reasonably be expected
</p>
<p>to clear the chamber of water by ignition.
</p>
<p>(2) Vehicle Trajectory
</p>
<p>To determine the vehicle trajectory under-
</p>
<p>water all vertical forces acting on the vehicle must be known at all
</p>
<p>Page V A-16</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V,A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>points in time. For convenience these forces will be classed as either
</p>
<p>frictional, gravitational, thrust, hydrostatic, or hydrodynamic.
</p>
<p>Frictional drag is the result of viscous
</p>
<p>shearing forces acting on the vehicle skin. Frictional drag was neg-
</p>
<p>lected for this vehicle as calculations performed on a similar vehicle
</p>
<p>indicated this force was negligible in comparison with the other forces
</p>
<p>involved.
</p>
<p>Engine thrust was calculated by combining
</p>
<p>the momentum flux with the net pressure area term of the exhaust jet.
</p>
<p>The properties of the exhaust jet were obtained by the methbd given in
</p>
<p>the preceding section.
</p>
<p>The net hydrostatic force could be calcu-
</p>
<p>lated by integrating the vertical components of the pressure times area
</p>
<p>forces over the whole vehicle*. To do this rigorously would be extreme-
</p>
<p>ly difficult if not impossible. These terms were instead calculated by
</p>
<p>representing the complex vehicle shape with a combination of conical
</p>
<p>*With the exception of the exhaust jet and its projected area of the vehi-
cle nose. Those pressure times area terms were included in the engine
thrust.
</p>
<p>Page V A-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>sections and a cylinder. The mode which was chosen was also felt to be
</p>
<p>a reasonably good model for the calculation of the dynamic loads.
</p>
<p>The accuracy of the model was checked by
</p>
<p>comparing the free floating model and ballast with the calculated water
</p>
<p>line of the vehicle and ballast. This check indicated the model had
</p>
<p>a buoyant force 7 x 1O Ib greater than the vehicle and ballast unit
</p>
<p>weight. This discrepancy is not an error but actually represents the
</p>
<p>weight of the water trapped between the injector and the RP-1 tank. To
</p>
<p>make the calculations accurate while using this model, this water must
</p>
<p>be included in the vehicle weight until this portion of the vehicle
</p>
<p>clears the water.
</p>
<p>The ballast unit was not included direc-
</p>
<p>tly in the calculation of hydrostatic forces. The net static effect
i
</p>
<p>of the ballast was known as a function of time and this was included
</p>
<p>in the summation of hydrostatic forces.
</p>
<p>Page V A-18</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>The 'gravitational force was taken as
</p>
<p>simply the weight of the vehicle and trapped water. The weight of the
</p>
<p>ballast unit was not included as the net effect of its hydrostatic and
</p>
<p>gravitational forces was considered in the summation of the hydrostatic
</p>
<p>forces.
</p>
<p>The hydrodynamic forces acting on the
</p>
<p>vehicle are extremely difficult to evaluate. With the aid of the., model
</p>
<p>and empirical drag coefficients drag forces were obtained for the portion
</p>
<p>of the vehicle up to the nozzle exit. As the flow around the nozzle exit
</p>
<p>is complicated by the presence of the ballast tanks and the gas jet, no
</p>
<p>attempt was made to represent it with a model. Instead the drag was
</p>
<p>calculated by applying a conservative drag coefficient to the nozzle
</p>
<p>base area less the jet area.
</p>
<p>The drag terms are not completely accu-
</p>
<p>rate due to the acceleration of the system. This introduces corrections
</p>
<p>to the drag terms which make them larger. However, the inclusion of
</p>
<p>the trapped water in the vehicle inertial mass actually does correctly
</p>
<p>compensate for the acceleration corrections in the drag terms.
</p>
<p>Page V A-19</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>The inertial mass of the vehicle was ob-
</p>
<p>tained by combining the total mass of the vehicle, the trapped water,
</p>
<p>and the ballast unit. It was assumed that the ballast unit was drained
</p>
<p>of 14 x 10 Ib inertial mass over a period of two seconds, and that the
</p>
<p>trapped water was shed immediately as that portion of the vehicle clear-
</p>
<p>ed the water.
</p>
<p>The actual, calculations were performed
</p>
<p>by taking finite increments of vehicle travel and assuming all forces
</p>
<p>were constant over that increment. Fifteen increments were used to
</p>
<p>obtain the results presented here.
</p>
<p>(3) General Discussion
</p>
<p>The underwater trajectory is given on
</p>
<p>Figure V-A-5. These results are for the case in which the ballast
</p>
<p>unit fluid is ejected over the first two seconds of firing. It was
</p>
<p>also assumed that the chamber pressure dropped at a uniform rate from
</p>
<p>285 to 225 psia over the first 3.5 sec of firing and that it rose at a
</p>
<p>uniform rate to 294 psia over the next 3.5 sec.
</p>
<p>Page V A-2O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>The rather unusual trajectory is the
</p>
<p>result of several interacting effects. At ignition the engine thrust
</p>
<p>coupled with large buoyant forces gives the vehicle a moderately large
</p>
<p>acceleration. However, as the vehicle gains velocity and rises, the
</p>
<p>drag increases while both the buoyant forces and engine thrust decrease.
</p>
<p>This causes the acceleration to drop off and even briefly assume nega-
</p>
<p>tive values. The decreasing engine thrust is the result of dropping
</p>
<p>chamber pressure. The drop in chamber pressure referred to here is not
</p>
<p>related to this phenomenon but rather to ullage gas pressure decay. The
</p>
<p>coupling effect of these factors will be studied in more detail in future
</p>
<p>designs. The decay in 'pressure can be accommodated by increased ullage
</p>
<p>volume or redesign of the pressurization system.
</p>
<p>Finally as the lower end of the vehicle
</p>
<p>begins to clear the surface the vehicle sheds trapped water, drag de-
</p>
<p>creases, thrust increases and the acceleration again becomes positive.
</p>
<p>A plot of interest is that showing the
</p>
<p>net static pressure on the skirt at two positions as functions of depth
</p>
<p>Page V A-21</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>(Figure V-A-6). These curves represent the difference between the in-
</p>
<p>ternal static pressure and external static pressure at a position near
</p>
<p>the nozzle throat and near the exit. To obtain the net total pressure
</p>
<p>a value equal to about 25 to 3O% of the total external hydrodynamic pres-
</p>
<p>sure should be subtracted from each curve at each depth. This indicates
</p>
<p>that, except for a very short period at the nozzle exit, the nozzle is
</p>
<p>not subjected to crushing loads. Once the ballast-ring unit is staged
</p>
<p>these plots are invalid, as dropping the ring completely changes the
</p>
<p>pressure distribution within the nozzle.
</p>
<p>No breakdown on drag forces or buoyancy
</p>
<p>forces is given. Because this particular .model was used there are
</p>
<p>some net force terms that are not easily separable into drag, buoyancy,
</p>
<p>and other force components. This complication is primarily the result
</p>
<p>of the way in which the ballast unit and trapped water were handled.
</p>
<p>b. Atmospheric Mode
</p>
<p>The Sea Dragon vehicle is launched from a near
</p>
<p>vertical floating position in the ocean. Preliminary analysis has
</p>
<p>Page V A-22</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>shown that the vehicle requires a maximum of 8 sec to clear the ocean
</p>
<p>surface and emerges with a minimum velocity of 60 ft/sec if the water
</p>
<p>ballast is retained during the underwater portion of the trajectory.
</p>
<p>These values were used to determine the initial conditions of the atmos-
</p>
<p>pheric portion of the nominal ascent trajectory, and were based on an
</p>
<p>underwater trajectory which retained the water ballast. For this parti-
</p>
<p>cular analysis it was assumed that no lateral launch dispersion occurred
</p>
<p>during the underwater phase of motion. A separate study of launch
</p>
<p>dispersion was made, and is reported in Appendix 11-21. In addition,
</p>
<p>several other trajectories were computed as described in Appendix 11-16,
</p>
<p>which also contains trajectory analysis details. The ascent trajectory
</p>
<p>can be divided into two phases: (1) an initial two-'stage high-thrust
</p>
<p>phase, and (2) a low-thrust phase during which a small percentage of
</p>
<p>the second stage propellant is burned to inject the vehicle into orbit.
</p>
<p>The high thrust phase has a duration of 342 sec, while final injection
</p>
<p>at low thrust requires 1,010 sec.
</p>
<p>Table V-A-1 shows the data that was used
</p>
<p>for this part of the study. Slightly better data in some instances
</p>
<p>Page V A-23</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>became available near the close of the study but time did not permit a
</p>
<p>new analysis. A cursory examination of the changes, however, indicates
</p>
<p>that they would have no profound effect on the comparative results dis-
</p>
<p>cussed herein. The performance data was obtained from theoretical shifting
</p>
<p>equilibrium data by applying a nozzle efficiency of 96% and a combustion
</p>
<p>efficiency of 94%, which are very conservative values. The weight data
</p>
<p>shown applies to the basic launch vehicle with no payload. The burn-
</p>
<p>out weight of each stage includes a propellant outage of 1%. Using
</p>
<p>the weight data of Table V-A-1, the calculated propellant fractions are
</p>
<p>O.896 and 0.897 for Stages I and II, respectively.
</p>
<p>The significant parameters for the con-
</p>
<p>tinuous burn ascent trajectory are shown in Figures V-A-7 through V-A-
</p>
<p>12. Significant data is also shown in Table V-A-2, which includes a
</p>
<p>comparative restart case. Note that zero time is defined as the time
</p>
<p>when the vehicle has just cleared the ocean surface.
</p>
<p>The first stage burns out at an altitude
</p>
<p>of 107,800 ft (17.7 nm), a velocity of 5,6O3 ft/sec, and V equal to
</p>
<p>46.33°. Referring to Figure V-A-8, the acceleration on the vehicle
</p>
<p>Page V A-24</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>varies from 2.09g at launch to 6.06g at burnout. The first-stage tra-
</p>
<p>jectory is quite flat, as evidenced by the large kick angle and rapid
</p>
<p>2
increase in f , and results in a peak dynamic pressure of 1,93O Ib/ft .
</p>
<p>2
At burnout, the dynamic pressure has dropped to 350 Ib/ft . However,
</p>
<p>the trajectory analysis was based upon a constant chamber pressure in
</p>
<p>the first stage engine and a correspondingly short burning time (74 sec.)
</p>
<p>and low altitude. More recent design information shows that the cham-
</p>
<p>ber pressure decays near burnout of the first stage, increasing burning
</p>
<p>time by approximately 1O sec. The net result is to increase the first-
</p>
<p>stage burnout altitude without appreciably changing the velocity. It
</p>
<p>has been shown that the- increase in burnout altitude will decrease the
</p>
<p>dynamic pressure at staging to approximately 100 Ib/ft , and the burn-
</p>
<p>out acceleration to 4.2g.
</p>
<p>Figures V-A-11 and V-A-12 show the trajectory
</p>
<p>parameters during low thrust operation of Stage II. The thrust is as-
</p>
<p>sumed constant at 200,000 Ib for the duration of 1,010.04 sec. The
</p>
<p>altitude increases from 150.0 to 300.0 nm. The velocity decreases
</p>
<p>Page V A-25</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>initially from 23,43O ft/sec (note that the velocity is incremented by
</p>
<p>1,275 ft/sec to account for the rotating earth at this point) to 23,359
</p>
<p>ft/sec at 490 sec, and then increases to 24,878 ft/sec at burnout. This
</p>
<p>is due to the very low thrust and tangential acceleration. Acceleration
</p>
<p>on the vehicle due to thrust increases from O.O71g or 2.28 ft/sec2 at
</p>
<p>initiation of this low thrust phase to O.O87g or 2.81 ft/sec2 at burn-
</p>
<p>out'. Gamma increases from 83.72° to 90°, and thus the tangential de-
</p>
<p>celeration due to gravity starts at 3.23 ft/sec and decreases to zero
</p>
<p>at burnout. The net tangential acceleration thus varies from -0.95 to
</p>
<p>+2.81 ft/sec . The net velocity gain produced by the low thrust oper-
</p>
<p>ation of Stage II is 1,448 ft/sec.
</p>
<p>For the retained ballast case, the 300-nm
</p>
<p>circular orbit payload is 1,112,000 Ib. If the ballast fluid is ex-
</p>
<p>pelled, the duration of the underwater portion of the trajectory is
</p>
<p>reduced from 8 to 5.05 sec, the velocity of the vehicle as it clears
</p>
<p>the .ocean surface is increased from 60 to 84 ft/sec and the payload
</p>
<p>increases to 1,173,000 Ib.
</p>

<p></p>
</div>

</p>

</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>The exchange ratio in pounds of payload per sec
</p>
<p>change in specific impulse in both stages is 17,OOO Ib/sec. If a speci-
</p>
<p>fic impulse of 92% of theoretical is attained, the Stage I specific im-
</p>
<p>pulse increases approximately 7.5 sec, thereby increasing the payload by
</p>
<p>129,000 Ib.
</p>
<p>c. Separation
</p>
<p>Separation of the first and second stages is
</p>
<p>accomplished using the energy of a prepressurized interstage cavity.
</p>
<p>When the staging sequence is initiated, expansion of the gas charge in
</p>
<p>the cavity results in a relative acceleration of the two sections of
</p>
<p>the vehicle. A separation velocity between the two stages is thus
</p>
<p>developed, which is a function of the initial interstage pressure and
</p>
<p>the length of travel during which air is effectively confined to the
</p>
<p>interstage cavity. This length is referred to as the ejection stroke
</p>
<p>length.
</p>
<p>Page V A-27</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>In the analysis of staging dynamics, it was as-
</p>
<p>sumed that there is no leakage of air from the interstage during the
</p>
<p>staging sequence until the ejection stroke length is exceeded. Although
</p>
<p>there will undoubtedly be some leakage during this time, it should have
</p>
<p>no significant effect on staging time as long as the leakage rate is
</p>
<p>small. It was also assumed that the expansion of the air in the inter-
</p>
<p>stage is isentropic. External aerodynamic forces acting on the vehicle
</p>
<p>other than drag were neglected in computing the longitudinal motions
</p>
<p>during staging. The first-stage engine thrust is negligible when stag-
</p>
<p>ing begins. Staging is defined as being completed when the fore end of
</p>
<p>the first stage clears the end of the second-stage expandable nozzle;
</p>
<p>however the first part of the motion is the most critical as regards
</p>
<p>potential collision between stages.
</p>
<p>The acceleration of the first stage of the
</p>
<p>vehicle relative to the second was obtained by summing the forces act-
</p>
<p>ing on each of the stages caused by pressure in the interstage and to
</p>
<p>drag, and dividing by their masses. This acceleration was computed at
</p>
<p>0.01 sec intervals, and integrated numerically to obtain separation
</p>
<p>velocity and distance. Air in the interstage was assumed to expand
</p>
<p>Page V A-28</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>isentropically .during this process. When the ejection stroke length is
</p>
<p>exceeded, the pressure in the interstage drops to ambient, and only drag
</p>
<p>forces are considered for the remainder of the process. The results of
</p>
<p>these calculations are given in Figure V-A-13 which shows separation
</p>
<p>times as a function of ejection stroke length for initial interstage
</p>
<p>pressures of 20, 30 and 40 psi.
</p>
<p>The major problem expected in the staging process
</p>
<p>is that of maintaining alignment of the two stages to prevent interference
</p>
<p>between the first stage and the expandable nozzle of the second stage.
</p>
<p>For a very conservative- estimate of the interference potential during
</p>
<p>staging, the pressure distributions on the two stages were estimated
</p>
<p>for a dynamic pressure of 100 lb/ft2 and an angle of attack of 1°.
</p>
<p>These pressure distributions were integrated over each of the stages to
</p>
<p>obtain a lift force, and a moment tending to rotate the stage about its
</p>
<p>center of gravity. These moments were used to obtain the angular ac-
</p>
<p>celeration of each stage. The angular accelerations of each,stage were
</p>
<p>integrated numerically to obtain angular displacements, which were used
</p>
<p>Page V A-29</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>to obtain interference displacements between the expandable nozzle and
</p>
<p>the first stage fore end. The results of this calculation for an initial
</p>
<p>interstage pressure of 40 psi and an ejection stroke length of 10 feet
</p>
<p>are shown in Figure V-A-14. It was assumed in the calculation that the
</p>
<p>expandable nozzle remains essentially unexpanded, and thus contributes
</p>
<p>nothing to the aerodynamic stability of the second stage. Since the ex-
</p>
<p>pandable nozzle will be released during the staging process (after the
</p>
<p>ejection stroke is completed) it will begin to expand under the influ-
</p>
<p>ence of the air leaving the interstage cavity and the restraint of ex-
</p>
<p>ternal flow. The time rate of expansion has not been calculated; how-
</p>
<p>ever, only a very small amount of expansion (1.5°) will accommodate the
</p>
<p>full lateral excursion of the adjacent stage elements. During the first
</p>
<p>20 ft of separation, less than 1 inch of relative lateral motion will
</p>
<p>occur as indicated in the curve of Figure V-A-14.
</p>
<p>Considering that the foregoing analyses assumes
</p>
<p>that Stage II is free to pitch during staging, the results are con-
</p>
<p>servative. Since the Stage II TVC system will prevent this motion, the
</p>
<p>potential interference will be substantially less than indicated in
</p>
<p>Page V A-30</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, A, System Performance (cont.)
</p>
<p>Figure V-A-14. With this change the analysis showed a 65% reduction in
</p>
<p>the lateral motion. A further discussion of considerations pertaining
</p>
<p>to these factors is given in Appendix 11-17.
</p>
<p>The conclusion to be drawn from the foregoing
</p>
<p>discussion is that the relative lateral motion of the two stages is
</p>
<p>sufficiently small to avoid damaging contact between them. Pressure
</p>
<p>driven ejection will produce adequate separation velocity, essentially
</p>
<p>compensating for the drap shielding effect of the expandable nozzle on
</p>
<p>the first stage. If ultimately found necessary, the vehicle pitch
</p>
<p>rate can be programed briefly to further accommodate separation require-
</p>
<p>ments .
</p>

<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, Vehicle Analysis (cont.)
</p>
<p>B. VEHICLE LOADS AND DYNAMICS
</p>
<p>1. Summary
</p>
<p>a. Introduction
</p>
<p>A wide variety of problems associated with
</p>
<p>static and dynamic loads, vehicle kinematics and dynamics, and the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon .environment have been investigated with differing degrees of com-
</p>
<p>pleteness. The principal result of this work, however, has been the
</p>
<p>identification and definition of problems in the area of vehicle loads
</p>
<p>and dynamics which will require more attention in the next design phase
</p>
<p>of the program.
</p>
<p>In many instances, the findings of this prelimi-
</p>
<p>nary work have indicated that phenomena associated with the Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>concept (large size, water environment, etc.) do not give rise to in-
</p>
<p>surmountable engineering problems with respect to present technology
</p>
<p>and conservative estimates of future advances. For example,launch
</p>
<p>dispersion magnitudes and water re-entry loads can be readily handled.
</p>
<p>Page V B-l</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>In other areas it has been recognized that the
</p>
<p>problems are not yet fully defined or that methods of solution are not
</p>
<p>readily available, e.g., the acoustic environment and resulting vehicle
</p>
<p>response.
</p>
<p>The more significant results of loads and dy-
</p>
<p>namics studies conducted to date are summarized in the following para-
</p>
<p>graphs.
</p>
<p>b. Sea and Wind Environments
</p>
<p>Wave lengths, heights, and periods are given
</p>
<p>as a function of sea state (wind velocity) in Section V,B,2,a. The
</p>
<p>concept of "sea spectra" is introduced, and a source of sea spectrum
</p>
<p>data is referenced.
</p>
<p>The upper atmosphere wind environment used
</p>
<p>in analytical and design studies presented in this report is taken from
</p>
<p>NASA TN 1274. A maximum wind velocity of approximately 320 ft/sec at
</p>
<p>42,000-ft altitude is thereby specified.
</p>
<p>Page V B-2</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>c. Floating Loads
</p>
<p>The most severe loading condition for the float-
</p>
<p>ing vehicle is the case, "full tanks, no ballast." A bending moment of
</p>
<p>385 x 1O6 ft-lb is calculated for the interstage area. This load is
</p>
<p>substantially less severe than that experienced during erection and is
</p>
<p>easily reacted by 30 psi tank pressure.
</p>
<p>d. Towing Dynamics and Loads
</p>
<p>Maximum bending moments of 23O x 10 and 390
</p>
<p>x 10 ft-lbs are calculated for the "sagging" and "hogging" conditions
</p>
<p>respectively. ("Hogging refers to the condition in which the vehicle
</p>
<p>is supported near the center by an ocean swell, while "sagging" de-
</p>
<p>scribes the condition in which each .end of the vehicle is supported by
</p>
<p>a wave crest). No dynamic amplification of these loads is anticipated
</p>
<p>since the vehicle bending modes are much higher in frequency than are
</p>
<p>the significant frequencies in the sea spectra.
</p>
<p>Page V B-3</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJ E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>Difficulties in controlling the towed vehicle
</p>
<p>with respect to yaw and roll in a cross-sea environment are discussed
</p>
<p>briefly in Section V,B,4,b. A substantial analytical and model testing
</p>
<p>effort should be expended on this problem in future Sea Dragon studies.
</p>
<p>The problem of dynamic interactions of the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon vehicle, the towing cable, the towing vessel, and the sea envi-
</p>
<p>ronment is reviewed in Section V,B,4,c. A simplified method of analysis
</p>
<p>is also presented. A great deal of anlysis and model testing remains
</p>
<p>./ /
to be done in this area.
</p>
<p>Propellant sloshing frequencies for the vehicle
</p>
<p>in the towing attitude are found to be in the range O.208 to 0.722 cps
</p>
<p>for the first three modes (tanks 90% full). These sloshing frequencies
</p>
<p>will couple with wave forces when sea state 5 conditions exist (see
</p>
<p>Section V,B,2). The effects of this slosh coupling on the complete
</p>
<p>towing configuration remains to be evaluated. However, since the cryo-
</p>
<p>genic tanks will be completely full (97% full for RP-1 tank) during
</p>
<p>most towing operations of the complete vehicle, no problem is anticipated,
</p>
<p>Page V B-4</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>e. Vehicle Erection Loads
</p>
<p>The process of erecting the vehicle gives rise
</p>
<p>to the largest bending moments experienced by the structure, primarily
</p>
<p>because the Stage II LC&gt;2 tank was placed forward to achieve better aero-
</p>
<p>dynamic characteristics. A maximum bending moment of 1.0 x 10^ ft-lb
</p>
<p>is calculated for the case of a 5.0 x 10 Ib effective ballast weight.
</p>
<p>An internal gas pressure of approximately 30 psi in the first-stage
</p>
<p>LO2 tank and second-stage LH2 tank is required to react the erection
</p>
<p>bending moment.
</p>
<p>It appears that large propellant sloshing forces
</p>
<p>will not occur during erection due to the fact that the tanks will be
</p>
<p>nearly full.
</p>
<p>f. Prefire Loads and Motions
</p>
<p>Dynamic loads due to wave action calculated
</p>
<p>for sea states up and including Sea State No. 7 are found to be relatively
</p>
<p>Page V B-5</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>small. Heaving motion, however, becomes quite violent for sea states
</p>
<p>above five due to strong coupling between sea spectra and the vehicle
</p>
<p>heaving mode. There also appears to be possibility of encountering
</p>
<p>pitch-heave instability for wind velocities above 30 knots (Sea State
</p>
<p>No. 6). It appears that launching should be restricted to Sea State
</p>
<p>No. 5 or less, for which a maximum pitch amplitude of 0.17° and maxi-
</p>
<p>mum heave excursion of 3.0 ft are calculated. This area also requires
</p>
<p>additional study to determine if the same conditions exist for other
</p>
<p>configurations and to investigate ameliorating devices such as the use
</p>
<p>of different ballast unit configurations to change the dynamic charac-
</p>
<p>teristics of the vehicle. Forces due to wave induced vortex shedding
</p>
<p>will not be a problem for the Sea Dragon vehicle. Atmospheric vortex
</p>
<p>shedding, however, may result in significant responses of the erected
</p>
<p>vehicle due to coupling with the rigid body pitch mode. A rough calcu-
</p>
<p>lation indicates an angular excursion of 1.75° for a 25 ft/sec wind
</p>
<p>velocity.
</p>
<p>Propellant sloshing loads are not felt to be
</p>
<p>of importance during the prefire stage due to fullness of the propel-
</p>
<p>Page V B-6</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>lant tanks and frequency separation between sloshing modes and other
</p>
<p>forcing and natural frequencies of the environment and vehicle.
</p>
<p>g. Launching Dynamics
</p>
<p>"Launch dispersion" (motions of the vehicle as
</p>
<p>it leaves the water) are calculated for several disturbing forces. Wave
</p>
<p>forces in Sea States No. 5 and 6 result in maximum angular deflections
</p>
<p>and rates of 4.55 and 2.41 /sec respectively. A thrust misalignment
</p>
<p>angle of 0.25° was found to give rise to maximum dispersions of 1.56°
</p>
<p>displacement and 1.70°/sec rate. A 33-knot wind gives rise to 5.77°
</p>
<p>angular dispersion and 1.17°/sec angular rate dispersion. Combined
*. • i
</p>
<p>effects are also evaluated. .Maximum dispersion displacement and rate
</p>
<p>for all cases considered were calculated to be 5.82° and 2.41°/sec
</p>
<p>respectively. It appears that the control system can easily handle
</p>
<p>these dispersions if activated at the time the vehicle leaves the
</p>
<p>water.
</p>
<p>Page V B-7</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AER O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The possibility of large loads being generated
</p>
<p>by propellant sloshing response during launch seems remote, since only
</p>
<p>the higher (fifth and sixth) sloshing modes will couple with the funda-
</p>
<p>mental vehicle bending mode.
</p>
<p>The maximum bending moment experienced during
</p>
<p>the underwater phase of launch (control system inactive) is found to
</p>
<p>be 201.5 x 10^ ft-lb, which is less than the maximum erection load.
</p>
<p>h. Flight Loads
</p>
<p>The most significant flight load on the Sea
</p>
<p>Dragon vehicle occurs at maximum dynamic pressure conditions (q = 1866
</p>
<p>Ib/ft, altitude = 34,600 ft). For this condition a wind-induced angle
</p>
<p>of attack of 4 is found to give rise to a bending moment of 12O x
</p>
<p>10 ft-lb in the interstage area.
</p>
<p>First-stage tank pressures of 80 psi are re-
</p>
<p>quired to react the 4.2g longitudinal acceleration whicn occurs at
</p>
<p>Page V B-8</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, 6, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>burnout. Although larger decelerations occur at re-entry, the resulting
</p>
<p>axial loads are smaller than those encountered for the burnout condition
</p>
<p>noted above.
</p>
<p>i. Impact Dynamic Loads
</p>
<p>The response of the first-stage structure (in-
</p>
<p>cluding the fundamental longitudinal vibration mode) to impact loads
</p>
<p>is calculated in Section V,B,9. Impact velocities of 300 ft/sec and
</p>
<p>600 ft/sec have been considered. The relatively long rise time of the
</p>
<p>300 ft/sec impact force results in very little dynamic amplification.
</p>
<p>However, the rise time of the 600 ft/sec impact shock is sufficiently
</p>
<p>short to cause 93% dynamic overshoot in the acceleration felt at the
</p>
<p>nose.
</p>
<p>Analysis of the effects of impact shock on the
</p>
<p>nose cone, cylindrical tanks, and the nozzle involves considerations
</p>
<p>of dynamic buckling. A large testing and analytical effort should be
</p>
<p>directed toward this area in future Sea Dragon studies. Response of
</p>
<p>specific subsystems to impact loads also remains to be investigated.
</p>
<p>Page V B-9</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>Based on results of impact studies to date, it
</p>
<p>appears that impact problems can be handled with presently available
</p>
<p>technology. A balanced combination of nose cone geometry and impact
</p>
<p>velocity (accomplished by use of an appropriate drag mechanism) will
</p>
<p>result in a practical first stage structure. The problem of isolating
</p>
<p>sensitive components is compatible with present packaging techniques.
</p>
<p>j. Special Aeroelastic Effects
</p>
<p>In Section V,B,1O the problems of flutter' of
</p>
<p>the first- and second-stage nozzles and the possibility of encountering
</p>
<p>oscillatory side loads during underwater operation are discussed.
</p>
<p>k. Propellant Sloshing
</p>
<p>Propellant sloshing frequencies have been calcu-
</p>
<p>lated for several flight conditions. Fundamental mode frequencies
</p>
<p>ranging from 0.520 to 0.609 cps are noted.
</p>
<p>Page V B-1O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The primary importance of the excitation of
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing modes is in connection with autopilot stability.
</p>
<p>It is possible that sloshing motions will be reinforced by spurious
</p>
<p>attitude error signals resulting from sloshing forces. This closed-
</p>
<p>loop reinforcement is particularly acute on Sea Dragon, since the pro-
</p>
<p>pellant sloshing frequencies are in the range of control frequencies.
</p>
<p>It will probably be necessary to design damping
</p>
<p>devices to preclude closed loop instability; the design process should
</p>
<p>be guided by a complete analog simulation of the autopilot.
</p>
<p>1. Acoustics
</p>
<p>A discussion of the Sea Dragon acoustic environment
</p>
<p>and its effect on the vehicle is presented in Section V,B,12. The prin-
</p>
<p>cipal sources of acoustic environment are found to be (1) generated by
</p>
<p>the first-stage rocket engine (185 db); (2) caused by the aerodynamic
</p>
<p>boundary layer (150 db); and (3) created by towing turbulence (155 db).
</p>
<p>Page V B-ll</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The predominance of low frequencies in the rocket engine noise spectrum
</p>
<p>will result in levels of roughly 150 db at distances of 1O to 15 mi
</p>
<p>from the source for a duration of one minute. A more detailed summary
</p>
<p>of acoustic environments is given in Table V-B-1.
</p>
<p>Vibrational accelerations of 10 to 15g rms are anti-
</p>
<p>cipated for the airframe vibration modes. High-damped materials may
</p>
<p>have to be used to preclude intolerable panel responses.
</p>

<p>ascent occurs at 34,600 ft.)

</p>
<p>The static bending moments due to buoyant and weight
</p>
<p>distribution loading for various horizontal floating (in still sea) con-
</p>
<p>ditions have been derived. The following cases were considered: tanks
</p>
<p>empty, no ballast; tanks empty, ballast attached; tanks full, no ballast;
</p>
<p>Page V B-14</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>

</p>
<p>tanks full, ballast attached; and fuel tanks full, LO tanks empty,
</p>
<p>ballast attached. The only ordinary floating condition that has bend-
</p>
<p>ing moments greater than the aerodynamic moment is the case of full tank
</p>
<p>no ballast attached. In this condition the maximum bending moment oc-
</p>
<p>curred in the interstage area (Figure V-B-1) and was approximately 385
</p>
<p>x 10 ft-lb. Since this moment is much less than that incurred during
</p>
<p>erection, the 3O psi tank pressure required for structural integrity
</p>
<p>during erection is more than sufficient for safe operation of the vehi-
</p>
<p>cle under the floating condition described above.
</p>
<p>4. Towing Dynamics and Loads
</p>
<p>a. Hogging and Sagging Loads
</p>
<p>The terms "hogging" and "sagging" pertain to
</p>
<p>static conditions during which the vehicle is supported near the center
</p>
<p>by an ocean wave (hogging) or supported at each end by two crests (sag-
</p>
<p>ging).
</p>
<p>Page V B-15</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>Static internal shear "and bending moments along
</p>
<p>the length of the vehicle were determined in the following manner:
</p>
<p>The vehicle was supported horizontally on a
</p>
<p>trochoidal wave with a length to height ratio equal to L/20 where L =
</p>
<p>length of the vehicle. The pressure load, as distributed in the hogging
</p>
<p>and sagging conditions, is reacted by the weight distribution, and the
</p>
<p>resultant shear and bending moment distributions are derived. The tro-
</p>
<p>choidal wave profiles were determined through the use of Bonjean curves.
</p>
<p>The shear and bending moment distributions were then found by an iter-
</p>
<p>ative technique.
</p>
<p>Shear and bending moment curves are presented
</p>
<p>in Figures V-B,2,3,4,5. Maximum shear and bending moments are tabulated
</p>
<p>below:
</p>

(lb) (ft-lb)
</p>
<p>Hogging 8,000,000 390,000,000
</p>
<p>Sagging 5,1OO,OOO 230,000,000
</p>
<p>Page V B-16</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The maximum internal bending moment from the hogging conditions appears
</p>
<p>to be approximately twice as large as the maximum moment due to sagging.
</p>
<p>The slight irregularity of the curve.shapes are due to the irregularities
</p>
<p>in load distribution. No dynamic amplification of the internal shear
</p>
<p>and moments is indicated since the exciting frequencies o'f the wave spec-
</p>
<p>tra are sufficiently lower than the nature (free-free beam) frequencies.
</p>
<p>(The first free-free bending mode frequency of the towing configuration
</p>

</p>
<p>ging loads will be greater than five seconds; thus the exciting fre-
</p>
<p>quencies will be below O.2 cps.)
</p>
<p>b. Cross Sea Effects
</p>
<p>The Sea Dragon vehicle, being cylindrical in
</p>
<p>shape, will have tendencies to spin about its longitudinal axis in a
</p>
<p>cross-sea environment. The sponsons on Stage II will prevent any vehi-
</p>
<p>.cle roll.
</p>
<p>Page V B-17</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>Another complication is that the vessel will be
</p>
<p>driven to a steady yaw angle; it is also possible that yaw oscillation
</p>
<p>may result from the cross-sea forces, causing the towed vessel to swing
</p>
<p>from one side of the towing course to the other, imparting surging loads
</p>
<p>to the towing lines.
</p>
<p>c. Towing Cable Dynamics
</p>
<p>The dynamic interactions between the elastic
</p>
<p>towing cable, the Sea Dragon vehicle, the towing ship, and the sea envi-
</p>
<p>ronment may be one of the more important considerations in the evalu-
</p>
<p>ation of system towing dynamics. The primary consideration here is one
</p>
<p>of excitation of the mass-spring-mass system by "" ong ocean swells re-
</p>
<p>sulting in a whip-cracking motion between the vehicle and towing vessel.
</p>
<p>The solution to the towing dynamics problem
</p>
<p>should combine analysis with model testing. Parametric variations in
</p>
<p>tpwiine elasticity and length, towing configurations, damping devices,
</p>
<p>etc., may then be carried out to arrive at a satisfactory towing scheme.
</p>
<p>Page V B-18</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>Studies of this nature are common in marine practice; it should only be
</p>
<p>necessary to apply known techniques of analysis to the Sea Dragon case.
</p>
<p>An example of the type of analytical work in-
</p>
<p>volved in the towing dynamics problem is presented in an appendix to
</p>
<p>this volume. The dynamic model is shown, and wave forcing functions
</p>
<p>are formulated.
</p>
<p>d. Propellant Sloshing During Towing
</p>
<p>(1) Introduction
</p>
<p>The unsteady nature of the towing force
</p>
<p>and the wave-induced pitching motion of the Sea Dragon vehicle during
</p>
<p>the towing operation will cause fluid oscillations within the propel-
</p>
<p>lant tanks if the tanks are not completely full. If the sea is not
</p>
<p>calm, the suddenly applied force due to whip-lash of the towing cable
</p>
<p>will excite the fundamental longitudinal sloshing mode. The resulting
</p>
<p>Page V B-19</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>motion of the fluid will be sustained due to the periodic nature of the
</p>
<p>forcing function, and if the excitation frequency is nearly equal to
</p>
<p>slosh natural frequencies, substantial sloshing forces may result.
</p>

<p>(3) Discussion of Results
</p>
<p>The fundamental longitudinal modes of
</p>
<p>fluid oscillation for the propellants in the nearly full condition may
</p>
<p>be seen to be in the frequency range of 0.2 to 0.4 cps. This corres-
</p>
<p>ponds to an expected pitching frequency of the vehicle from 0.1 cps to
</p>
<p>0.3 cps during Sea State No. 5. Therefore, it is expected that a re-
</p>
<p>sonant condition will exist during towing for certain sea conditions.
</p>
<p>If the longitudinal propellant slosh
</p>
<p>modes are excited by the pitching motion of the vehicle, the fluid wave
</p>
<p>height within the tank will be restricted by the curved upper surface
</p>
<p>Page V B-21</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>of the cylindrical tanks. Because of the restricted fluid motion near
</p>
<p>the free surface, the slosh mass ratio (equivalent sprung mass to total
</p>
<p>propellant mass) is small, thus resulting in reduced oscillatory pres-
</p>
<p>sures on the tank walls. If the perturbation pressures result in too
</p>
<p>high a stress level within the tank walls, additional slosh suppression
</p>
<p>devices would be used, such as annular baffles or floating "cans."
</p>
<p>5. Vehicle Erection Loads
</p>
<p>a. Static Loads
</p>
<p>The bending moments encountered during
</p>
<p>erection of the fully loaded Sea Dragon have been calculated. Erection
</p>
<p>is effected by filling of the ballast tanks after arrival at the launch
</p>
<p>site. Figures V-B-6 and V-B-8 show the weight and buoyant force distri-
</p>
<p>bution on the vehicle for two ballast flooding condition; 2.5 x 106 Ib
</p>
<p>effective ballast and 5 x 10 Ib effective ballast. Figures V-B-7 and
</p>
<p>V-B-9 illustrate the bending moment distribution for the two respective
</p>
<p>Page V B-22</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>cases. The case of 5.0 x 10 Ib effective ballast represents the maxi-
</p>
<p>Q
mum moment during erection, 1 x 10 ft-lb. Further flooding of the'
</p>
<p>ballast causes the moment to decrease due to the rapidly increasing
</p>
<p>angle with the horizontal. The moment of 1 x 10^ ft-lb is the maximum
</p>
<p>bending moment experienced by the vehicle during all loading conditions
</p>
<p>The internal gas pressure in the first
</p>
<p>stage LO tank and second stage LH tank required to resist this moment
</p>
<p>is approximately 30 psi.
</p>
<p>b. Dynamic Loads due to Propellant Sloshing
</p>
<p>The possibility of large sloshing forces aris-
</p>
<p>ing during the erection operation is slight. If the tanks are com-
</p>
<p>pletely full, the sloshing frequency is infinite and there will be no
</p>
<p>sloshing forces transmitted to the structure. Probably there will be
</p>
<p>a small ullage resulting in a high slosh natural frequence which will
</p>
<p>not be excited by the quasistatic erection operation.
</p>
<p>Reference A: NASA TN D252, "Investigation of the Natural Frequencies of
Fluid in Spherical and Cylindrical Tanks." McCarty and
Stephens.

<p>6. . Prefire Loads and Motions
</p>
<p>The purpose of this section is to determine the wind
</p>
<p>and wave induced motions of the erected vehicle, to evaluate the effects
</p>
<p>of vortex shedding (wind and ocean waves), and to discuss the effects of
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing forces in the prefire condition.
</p>
<p>a. Wind and Wave Induced Motion
</p>
<p>The object of this portion of the study was to
</p>
<p>determine the maximum amplitudes of position, velocity and acceleration
</p>
<p>for two principal degrees of freedom, namely, pitch and heave. Two
</p>
<p>basic conditions were considered for various sea states. The first of
</p>
<p>these is the nominal prelaunch which was assumed to occur at less than
</p>
<p>sea state 5 (wind velocity 2O knots). The main consideration in this
</p>
<p>case was the effect of the motions on launching. The second case con-
</p>
<p>sidered was the standing condition which could occur where the sea
</p>
<p>would rise unexpectedly and it would be necessary to have the vehicle
</p>
<p>ride out the storm. In this case the main consideration was the effect

</p>

<p>b. • Prefire Vortex Shedding
</p>
<p>(1) Atmospheric Vortex Shedding
</p>
<p>The phenomenon of vortex shedding in the
</p>
<p>wake of a body is a familiar one to missile designers. Many instances
</p>
<p>of forced excitation of elastic structures can be found in engineering
</p>
<p>literature.
</p>
<p>Page V B-30</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The problem of vortex induced forces on
</p>
<p>the Sea Dragon vehicle prior to launch can be broken into two categories
</p>
<p>the excitation of the elastic body modes and excitation of the rigid
</p>
<p>pitching mode. It should be pointed out that structural response to
</p>
<p>forces produced by vortex shedding is not truly an aeroelastic phenome-
</p>
<p>non because the airloads are very nearly independent of the unsteady
</p>
<p>motion.
</p>
<p>The vortex shedding mechanism is strongly
</p>
<p>a function of Reynolds Number (RN). In the region of low RN. vort.ices
</p>
<p>are produced in a regular discrete manner. As RN is increased, the
</p>
<p>resulting turbulence causes the process to become more random in nature
</p>
<p>(See References A and B). Reference B is concerned with this problem
</p>
<p>in the "supercritical" range of RN (RN =- 500,000) .
</p>
<p>Reynolds numbers experienced by Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>when expressed as a function of velocity, can be written
</p>
<p>R.N. =PVd 478,000 V

<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION

<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)

</p>

<p>c. Propellant Sloshing During Prefire
</p>
<p>It is not expected that propellant sloshing
</p>
<p>will be a problem when the Sea Dragon vehicle is in the prelaunch condi-
</p>
<p>tion. The vibration excitation is predominantly low frequency per-
</p>
<p>turbations caused by wave action and the vehicle buoyant "heaving" mode.
</p>
<p>The frequency of the buoyant mode is approximately O.O6 cps, which is
</p>
<p>well below the lowest sloshing frequency of 0.52O cps for the prelaunch
</p>
<p>condition (assuming 5% ullage). Even if the sloshing modes are excited
</p>
<p>because of some transient disturbance, the sloshing mass (Equivalent
</p>
<p>sprung mass) is small for the nearly full condition. This is true be-
</p>
<p>cause fluid motion is limited at the foree surface because of the re-
</p>
<p>stricted boundary at the top of each tank.
</p>
<p>7. Launch Condition
</p>
<p>This section is concerned with dynamic loads experi-
</p>
<p>enced by the vehicle during launching caused by wave action, wind, and

<p>propellant sloshing. A second major concern in "launch dispersion"

<p>(motions of the vehicle at the time it leaves the water) caused by the

</p>
<p>displacements.

<p>b. Propellant Sloshing During Launch
</p>
<p>At the instant of engine firing there is . a
</p>
<p>suddenly applied pitching moment caused by an initial . thrust misalign-
</p>
<p>ment. The transient nature of the moment will excite the fundamental
</p>
<p>Page V B-42</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>and many of the higher antisymmetric sloshing modes in all prqpellant
</p>
<p>tanks. However, because of the nearly full condition, the sloshing
</p>
<p>mass (equivalent sprung mass) is small and its effect on the vehicle
</p>
<p>will be slight. As the vehicle breaks the water at approximately t =
</p>
<p>5 sec, the predominate excitation is caused by a structural bending mode
</p>
<p>at about 2 cps, which tends to excite the fifth and sixth sloshing modes
</p>
<p>in all tanks. Since the sloshing mass, and thus the sloshing force, is
</p>
<p>very small for modes higher than the third, there seems to be no seri-
</p>
<p>ous structural or dynamic stability problems during the launching mode
</p>
<p>of operation.
</p>
<p>The propellant acoustic modes (standing wave
</p>
<p>effect) may be excited by resonant burning during launch and should be
</p>
<p>investigated in some detail. The fundamental acoustic frequency for a
</p>
<p>propellant tank with large ullage at top is given by the formula,
</p>
<p>f = a
a 4h
</p>
<p>where a is the velocity of sound for the propellant and h is the height
</p>
<p>Page V B-43</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>of the propellant. Rough calculations show that the acoustic frequency
</p>
<p>is in the range of from 10 to 20 cps for the Sea Dragon vehicle. Further
</p>
<p>work should be done to investigate the effect of the compressibility of
</p>
<p>the propellant upon the longitudinal mode of the vehicle. The possibili-
</p>
<p>ty of these pressure fluctuations coupling with the thrust oscillations
</p>
<p>of the engine should also be determined.
</p>

<p>9. Impact Dynamic Loads
</p>
<p>The dynamic response of the first stage structure to
</p>
<p>impact loads at re-entry is calculated in this section.
</p>
<p>Page V B-46</p>
<p></p>

<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II

<p>A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N

<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>a. Impact Loads, Normal Entry
</p>
<p>The dynamic response of the first-stage of the
</p>
<p>vehicle at water entry is evaluated in this section. A rather complete
</p>
<p>analysis of the dynamic system is included in Appendix 11-22.
</p>
<p>The compliance of the gimbal ball has been com-
</p>
<p>puted to determine its influence on system response at impact. The
</p>
<p>stiffness of the ball in compression was found to be 68.4 x 1O8 .lb/ft
</p>
<p>8while the stiffness in tension was found to be 30.7 x 10 lb/ft. The
</p>
<p>resonant frequency of a mass-spring-mass system comprised of the gimbal
</p>
<p>ball, motor, and tank (using the tension spring).is roughly 62.7 cps.
</p>
<p>This frequency is high in comparison with the longitudinal mode frequency
</p>
<p>of the vehicle (33.1 cps). It is therefore concluded that the gimbal
</p>
<p>spring does not play an important role in the dynamic response.
</p>
<p>Page V B-47</p>
<p></p>

<p>10. Special Aeroelastic Effects
</p>
<p>Included in this section are discussions of special
</p>
<p>aeroelastic problems pertaining to Sea Dragon operation. The problem
</p>
<p>Page V B-5O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJ E T - G E N E R A L CORPORATION —
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>of flutter of the nozzle structures is dealt with in some detail. A
</p>
<p>discussion of the possibility of generating oscillatory side loads' on
</p>
<p>the first-stage nozzle during the underwater trajectory is also included.
</p>
<p>a. Nozzle Flutter
</p>
<p>A qualitative study of the flutter possibilities
</p>
<p>of the large first-stage DeLaval nozzle has been carried to mathematical
</p>
<p>formulation of some parts of the problem. Flutter of such structures
</p>
<p>in smaller sizes has been observed during operation with overexpanded
</p>
<p>flow conditions.
</p>
<p>Of particular interest are the nozzle bell modes
</p>
<p>and the several phases of flight conditions which may excite them. How-
</p>
<p>ever, other modes such as those in bending, pitching and longitudinal
</p>
<p>motion require similar attention. The problem is further complicated
</p>
<p>by the thermal effects which may substantially alter the elastic proper-
</p>
<p>ties and stress levels during the flight phase. None of these possibi-
</p>
<p>lities have been examined to a point where conclusions can be reached.
</p>
<p>Page V B-51</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics, (cont.)
</p>
<p>b. Oscillatory Side Loads
</p>
<p>The critical regime for nozzle flow separation
</p>
<p>occurs during the underwater portion of the engine operation. While
</p>
<p>under water the nozzle experiences back pressures substantially above
</p>
<p>those found during ordinary engine operation, so that a nozzle designed
</p>
<p>for above water operation will undoubtedly experience flow separation
</p>
<p>while operating underwater.
</p>
<p>One of the criteria used in predicting flow
</p>
<p>separation is the ratio of chamber pressure (PC) to ambient pressure
</p>
<p>(Pa). This ratio varies from about 2 at ignition (300 feet below the
</p>
<p>water surface) to a value of 20 at the water surface. Available data
</p>
<p>indicate that for Pc/Pa&gt;1O the separation phenomena is reasonably
</p>
<p>predictable and also reasonably stable and symmetrical.
</p>
<p>Small oscillations in the separation point
</p>
<p>may couple with some part of the structure to produce a resonating
</p>
<p>condition. However, as the vehicle is ascending, the point of separa-
</p>
<p>tion will be continually moving towards the nozzle exit, so that in all
</p>
<p>Page V B-52</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>likelihood the nozzle will only pass through a point of resonance rather
</p>
<p>than continually operate at such a point. There is a possibility that,
</p>
<p>because of the low natural frequencies found in a vehicle of Sea Dragon
</p>
<p>size, a transient resonant condition such as the one just described may
</p>
<p>almost be passed by unnoticed.
</p>
<p>The region of operation with P,,/P_&lt;C10 may bec a
</p>
<p>somewhat serious. During this time the vehicle will not be moving as
</p>
<p>rapidly as at the time when Pc/Pa &gt;10, so the period of time in which
</p>
<p>coupling might occur is greater.
</p>
<p>Tests on small nozzles at pc/Pa&lt;C10 have in-
</p>
<p>dicated that with these pressure ratios the separation phenomena may
</p>
<p>be both unstable and unsymmetrical. In addition, separation deep with-
</p>
<p>in a nozzle (as may be expected with PC/P_&lt; 10) has been found to be
- a»
</p>
<p>erratic, primarily because of the ejector action of the gas jet in pro-
</p>
<p>ducing rapid pressure fluctuations in the separated region of the
</p>
<p>nozzle.
</p>
<p>Page V B-53</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL C O R P O R A T I O N —
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>There may be some quest.ion as to the strict
</p>
<p>applicability of the above results to Sea Dragon. Separation in nozzles
</p>
<p>is primarily produced by boundary layer interactions, so that the condi-
</p>
<p>tion of the boundary layer has a pronounced influence on the character
</p>
<p>of the separation phenomena. A gas stream with a fully developed tur-
</p>
<p>bulent boundary layer does not separate as easily and separates more
</p>
<p>stably than one with a laminary or transition boundary layer.
</p>
<p>Because of the small size of the test nozzles,
</p>
<p>some of the instability of separation may have been associated with the
</p>
<p>boundary layer condition and was not necessarily a result of the low
</p>
<p>value of Pc/
Pa' There is no doubt that Sea Dragon will have a fully
</p>
<p>developed turbulent boundary layer at the point of separation and thus
</p>
<p>will tend to separate more stably.
</p>
<p>A solution to the problems associated with the
</p>
<p>unsymmetrical loads resulting from flow separation is complex. Flow
</p>
<p>separation itself can be prevented by placing a plug in the nozzle to
</p>
<p>Page V B-54</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>prevent over -expansion. Nonsymmetrical separation can be prevented by
</p>
<p>use of a separation inducing ring. If the final design allows free
</p>
<p>nozzle flow and separation, an attempt must be made to define the char-
</p>
<p>acteristic frequencies of the oscillating side loads and the nozzle
</p>
<p>designed such that nozzle bell mode or bending mode resonances do not
</p>
<p>fall at these frequencies.
</p>

</p>
<p>vehicle. This analysis is briefly explained in Appendix 11-23. The
</p>

</p>

*J
