</p>
<p>b. General Discussion of Propellant Sloshing
</p>
<p>The stability of a missile that is controlled
</p>
<p>by an autopilot control system depends on many parameters; such as
</p>
<p>bending frequency, natural slosh frequency, ratio of propellant mass
</p>
<p>to total vehicle mass, control frequency, tontrol system damping and
</p>
<p>gain, and the types and location of the vehicle attitude sensors.
</p>
<p>*Note that because of disimilar tank shapes, the numerical value of
the fullness ratio is not an indication of fluid utilization. In
Appendix 11-23 this seeming anomaly is clarified..
</p>
<p>Page V B-57</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>The interaction of the elastic missile fuselage, feedback control system,
</p>
<p>and liquid propellant may load., to an unstable mode of operation for
</p>
<p>any rocket vehicle with a liquid propellant under certain dynamic con-
</p>
<p>ditions .
</p>
<p>To minimize the effects of the propellant oscil-
</p>
<p>lations, several methods may be employed. The proposed method is the
</p>
<p>introduction of annular baffles, rigidly attached to the tank walls, to
</p>
<p>disturb the flow near the free surface and create enough turbulence to
</p>
<p>provide damping. Another slosh suppression device consists of covering
</p>
<p>the surface of the liquid with floating cans to inhibit the free sur-
</p>
<p>face oscillations. A very effective method to prevent the coupling
</p>
<p>of the sloshing natural frequencies and control frequency is to divide
</p>
<p>the propellant tank into several longitudinal subtanks by means of a
</p>
<p>system of bulkheads. The compartmented tank then consists of several
</p>
<p>slender tanks, thus increasing the natural slosh frequencies. Besides
</p>
<p>increasing the natural frequencies, this method effectively reduces
</p>
<p>the sloshing mass (the equivalent sprung mass), thereby reducing the
</p>
<p>propellant sloshing forces acting on the system during resonance.
</p>
<p>Page V B-58</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— AE R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>If annular ring baffles are used, as the present
</p>
<p>design indicates, the optimum configuration and location should be de-
</p>
<p>termined. The damping due to a ring baffle has been shown analytically
</p>
<p>(Reference A), and experimentally (Reference B) to decrease with the
</p>
<p>depth of the baffle below the surface. The baffle location necessary
</p>
<p>for substantial damping is limited to a narrow range below the surface
</p>
<p>so that the optimum spacing of the baffles is dependent on the critical
</p>
<p>depths of the liquid; i.e., the depth of fluid resulting in a resonant
</p>
<p>frequency or a condition demanding an increase in damping.
</p>
<p>Reference A : "Ring Damping of Free Surface Oscillations in a Circular
</p>
<p>Tank", Miles, Journal of Applied Mechanics, Vol.25, No. 2.
</p>
<p>Reference B : "Experimental Investigation of the Damping of Liquid
</p>
<p>Oscillations in Cylindrical Tanks with Various Baffles",
</p>
<p>Silveira, NASA TN-D-715.
</p>
<p>12. Acoustics
</p>
<p>a. Introduction
</p>
<p>The first-stage engine will produce the most
</p>
<p>Page V B-59</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>— A E R O J E T - G E N E R A L C O R P O R A T I O N
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>severe acoustic environment that is experienced by the Sea Dragon vehi-
</p>
<p>cle and the ground support equipment. Noise generated during the tow-
</p>
<p>ing operation and aerodynamic turbulence during the atmospheric mode of
</p>
<p>flight will contribute to this environment. The high intensity noise
</p>
<p>that is produced by the first-stage engine subjects the surface of the
</p>
<p>vehicle to sound pressure levels in the order of ISO db (re: .OOO2
n
</p>
<p>dynes/cm ) during the initial few seconds of engine firing. Aerodynamic
</p>
<p>turbulence and towing noise to levels of 150 db will constitute the
</p>
<p>longest duration excitation of the vehicle structure. Appendix 11-24
</p>
<p>reports an investigation of each of the acoustic noise sources with re-
</p>
<p>spect to the excitation of various modes of structural vibration. The
</p>
<p>appendix describes the method of noise generation and magnitude of the
</p>
<p>sound pressure levels, and indicates the relative response of the
</p>
<p>structure.
</p>
<p>b. Acoustic Environment
</p>
<p>The primary sources of acoustic environment
</p>
<p>are the first-stage rocket engine during boost phase, the noise of
</p>
<p>Page V B-6O</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>turbulence during vehicle towing, and aerodynamic pressure fluctuations
</p>
<p>during flight at high dynamic pressures. The relative magnitude, fre-
</p>
<p>quency, duration, and characteristics of the noise caused by the various
</p>
<p>sources are presented in Table V-B-1. The near field sound level that
</p>
<p>is experienced by the expendable Sea Dragon, during the effective life
</p>
<p>of the vehicle, is illustrated by Figure V-B-27, which shows the pre-
</p>
<p>dicted overall sound levels at a forward vehicle position versus time.
</p>
<p>Figure V-B-28 plots the contours of the near field sound pressures dur-
</p>
<p>ing launch and illustrates the interaction of the high velocity jet
</p>
<p>stream and the quiescent fluid medium. The noise levels caused by the
</p>
<p>rocket engine will be reduced as the vehicle gains velocity so that
</p>
<p>the sound pressures are maximum during the first few seconds of firing.
</p>
<p>In particular, the levels created at launch and those experienced by
</p>
<p>the vehicle at high subsonic speeds will be in the order of 175 db to
</p>
<p>185 db. The far field noise will be propagated over large distances
*
</p>
<p>as the result of the predominance of the low frequencies in the rocket
</p>
<p>noise, causing sound levels of 140 to 160 db to be received 1O-15 mi
</p>
<p>from launch position for a duration of about one min.
</p>
<p>Page V B-61</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
</p>
<p>AEROJET-GENERAL CORPORATION
</p>
<p>V, B, Vehicle Loads and Dynamics (cont.)
</p>
<p>c. Structural Response
</p>
<p>Noise produced during launch will excite the
</p>
<p>Sea Dragon air frame vibration modes, generating vibrational acceler-
</p>
<p>ations to 10 to 15g rms. Towing noise and aerodynamic turbulence will
</p>
<p>generate a lower level excitation for a longer duration. If correlation
</p>
<p>of the turbulence exists between the excitation and structural re-
</p>
<p>sponse, the vibration amplitudes could1be amplified extensively. The
</p>
<p>use of highly damped materials is thus recommended to reduce the possi-
</p>
<p>bility of the fatigue caused by highly amplified flexural vibrations.
</p>
<p>Page V B-62 (End V)</p>
<p></p>
</div>
<div class="page"><p></p>
<p>Report No. LRP 297, Volume II
